Adaptive Acceleration Without Strong Convexity Priors Or Restarts
Joao V. Cavalcanti, Laurent Lessard & Ashia C. Wilson

Abstract
Accelerated optimization methods are typically parametrized by the Lipschitz smoothness and strong convexity parameters of a given problem, which are rarely known in practice. While the Lipschitz smoothness parameter L L can be easily estimated by backtracking, directly estimating (i.e., without restarts) the strong convexity parameter m m remains an open problem. This paper addresses this challenge by proposing NAG-free, an optimization method that achieves acceleration while estimating m m online without priors or restarts . Our method couples Nesterov’s accelerated gradient (NAG) method with an inexpensive estimator that does not require additional computation, only storage of one more iterate and gradient. We prove that in the canonical setting of the open problem, NAG-free converges globally at least as fast as gradient descent and locally at an accelerated rate, without resorting to m m priors or restarts. We also introduce a complementary estimator for L L , symmetric to the m m estimator. We provide extensive empirical evidence that this NAG-free heuristic with L L and m m estimators often achieves acceleration in practice, outperforming restart schemes and traditional accelerated methods parametrized by offline estimates of L L and m m . Our experiments also suggest that NAG-free might be effective even for nonconvex problems when combined with restarts.



1
Introduction

Accelerated methods are notable for achieving optimal convergence rates among first-order optimization algorithms on key problem classes
(
Nesterov 2018
)
.
However, to attain these optimal rates in practice, they require knowledge of problem-specific parameters.
Consider the class of Lipschitz-smooth, strongly convex functions, characterized by the smoothness parameter
L
and the strong convexity parameter
m
. To apply accelerated methods effectively in this setting, both
L
and
m
must be known; yet, as noted by
Boyd and Vandenberghe 2004
, these parameters “are known only in rare cases.”
While
L
can often be estimated via backtracking
(
Tseng 2008
;
Beck and Teboulle 2009
)
, estimating
m
is considerably more difficult.
As
Su et al. 2016
put it: “while it is relatively easy to bound the Lipschitz constant
L
by the use of backtracking, estimating the strong convexity parameter
m
, if not impossible, is very challenging.”
Similarly,
O’Donoghue and Candès 2015
emphasize that “estimating the strong convexity parameter is much more challenging.” In light of this, restart schemes have emerged as the dominant approach to handling unknown
m

(
d’Aspremont et al. 2021
, Sec. 6)
.
These methods restart accelerated algorithms (e.g., Nesterov’s method) based on adaptive criteria, which can be predetermined
(
Roulet and d’Aspremont 2017
;
Aujol et al. 2024
)
or checked at runtime
(
Nesterov 2013
;
Lin and Xiao 2015
;
O’Donoghue and Candès 2015
;
Liang et al. 2022
;
Renegar and Grimmer 2022
;
Lan et al. 2023
;
Sujanani and Monteiro 2025
)
.
Despite these advances, it remains an:

“open question whether strong convexity parameters can be efficiently estimated while maintaining reasonable worst-case guarantees and without resorting to restart schemes.”

(
d’Aspremont et al. 2021
, p. 96)
.

Contributions

This paper directly addresses that open question, with two main contributions:

1.

An efficient method for estimating the strong convexity parameter without restarts.
We introduce
NAG-free
, an optimization algorithm that directly estimates the strong convexity parameter
m
online without priors and without relying on restart schemes.
NAG-free couples Nesterov’s accelerated gradient (NAG) method with a lightweight estimator that requires only the storage of one additional iterate and gradient, both already computed in standard NAG.
We prove that NAG-free converges globally at least as fast as gradient descent, and achieves local acceleration.

2.

An efficient parameter-free heuristic with good empirical performance:

Although our focus in this paper is the open question above, which only concerns estimating
m
, we also consider the problem of simultaneously estimating both
L
and
m
.
Namely, we equip NAG-free with an
L
estimator symmetric to the
m
estimator that requires no further computations, resulting in a parameter-free method.
We demonstrate that this NAG-free heuristic often achieves acceleration in practice, outperforming restart schemes and traditional accelerated methods that rely on problem-specific parameter bounds.
Moreover, we present promising results on the combination of NAG-free with a restart scheme on nonconvex problems.

2
Problem statement

Consider the task of finding
x^{\star}(f)
, the unique minimum of the problem

\displaystyle\min_{x}f(x),

(1)

where
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
, the set of functions that satisfy the following definition.

Definition 2.1

(Lipschitz-Smooth and Strongly Convex Functions)
.

We define
\mathcal{F}(L,m)
to be the set of all differentiable convex functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
that satisfy both:

•

Lipschitz-smoothness:
There exists
L>0
such that for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle f(y)\leq f(x)+\langle\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle+(L/2)\|y-x\|^{2}.

(2)

•

Strong convexity:
There exists
m>0
such that for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle f(x)+\langle\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle+(m/2)\|y-x\|^{2}\leq f(y).

(3)

That is,
\mathcal{F}(L,m)
contains functions that are both
L
-smooth and
m
-strongly convex.
1
1

1

More generally,
f
is
L
-smooth if
\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\|\leq L\|x-y\|
for all
x,y
. Under convexity, this is equivalent to (
2
); see
Nesterov 2018
.

Having defined
\mathcal{F}(L,m)
, we now precisely state the problem addressed in this paper:

Problem statement.

Given
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
, we address the
open question

as stated
in
(
d’Aspremont et al. 2021
, p. 96)
: whether
m
can be efficiently estimated while maintaining reasonable worst-case guarantees and without resorting to restart schemes.
Accordingly, to focus on the open question of estimating
m
, we assume that an upper bound on
L
is known, which is common in practice. Moreover, since the question presumes strong convexity, we assume that inequality (
3
) holds for some
m>0
.

3
The NAG-free algorithm

For any
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
and for all
x\neq y
, the following inequality holds:

\displaystyle m\leq c(x,y)\coloneqq\dfrac{\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\|}{\|x-y\|}\leq L.

(4)

This inequality follows from standard results on smooth and strongly convex functions
(
Nesterov 2018
, Thm. 2.1.5, 2.1.10)
. The quantity
c(x,y)
captures a local notion of curvature between two points and lies in the interval
[m,L]
.
Given iterates
x_{t+1}
and
x_{t}
produced by Nesterov’s accelerated gradient (NAG) method, we propose to estimate
m
online via the recurrence

\displaystyle m_{t+1}\coloneqq\min(m_{t},c_{t+1}),\quad\text{where }c_{t+1}=c(x_{t+1},x_{t}).

(5)

This estimate is then used to parametrize Nesterov’s method to produce a new iterate, which in turn is used to compute a new effective curvature and update
m_{t+1}
.
The resulting procedure is computationally lightweight: it reuses gradients already computed by NAG and only requires storing one additional iterate and gradient.
To initialize
m_{0}
, we use a single evaluation of
c(x_{0},y)
, where
x_{0}
is the initial point and
y
is sampled uniformly from a small neighborhood around
x_{0}
.
This initialization guarantees that
m_{0}\in[m,L]
, making it a safe and inexpensive starting point. The complete algorithm, which we call
NAG-free
, is presented below.

Algorithm 1

NAG-free: an accelerated method that estimates
m
without restarts.

1:

Input:

T>0,x_{0}=y_{0},\bar{L}>L

2:

Output:

x_{T},y_{T}

3:

y\sim x_{0}+U\mathopen{[}0,10^{-6}\mathclose{]}^{d}

4:

m_{0}\leftarrow\|\nabla f(x_{0})-\nabla f(y)\|/\|x_{0}-y\|

5:

for

t=0,\ldots,T-1

do

6:

y_{t+1}\leftarrow x_{t}-\nabla f(x_{t})/\bar{L}

7:

x_{t+1}\leftarrow y_{t+1}+(y_{t+1}-y_{t})(\sqrt{\bar{L}}-\sqrt{m_{t}})/(\sqrt{\bar{L}}+\sqrt{m_{t}})

8:

c_{t+1}\leftarrow\|\nabla f(x_{t+1})-\nabla f(x_{t})\|/\|x_{t+1}-x_{t}\|

9:

m_{t+1}\leftarrow\min(m_{t},c_{t+1})

10:

end

for

Convergence intuition.

NAG-free exhibits two key features that underlie its convergence behavior:

1.

Adaptive interpolation between GD and NAG.
The update rule for
x_{t+1}
interpolates between gradient descent and NAG.
If
m_{t}\to L
, then the momentum term becomes small NAG-free approximates gradient descent.
If
m_{t}\to m
, then the update becomes equivalent to NAG with correct parameterization.
Thus, NAG-free converges globally at least as fast as gradient descent.

2.

Power iteration-like behavior near the optimum.
Near the optimum, the curvature estimate
c_{t}
evolves similarly to a power method applied to the Hessian, with some additional dynamics.
As a result, the iterate
x_{t}
rapidly concentrates in the eigenspace corresponding to the least eigenvalue of the Hessian,
m
, which translates into
c_{t}
quickly approaching
m
, accelerating NAG-free.

4
Convergence guarantees

In this section, we present convergence results for
Algorithm

1
and proof sketches.
Full details can be found in the supplementary materials.

In the following, we assume that
Algorithm

1
receives an upper bound
\bar{L}
on
L
, which is often the case in practice, as the experiments in
Section

5
illustrate.
While we also believe other approximation strategies, such as backtracking line search, could also be incorporated into our framework, we make this mild assumption for simplicity and clarity of analysis, and to focus on the actual open problem which is to estimate
m
, not to estimate both
m

and

L
.

4.1
Global convergence

The main global result is that
Algorithm

1
converges at least as fast as gradient descent.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
,
\bar{L}\geq L
and
\bar{\kappa}=\bar{L}/m
.
Then, the iterates of
Algorithm

1
satisfy

\displaystyle f(y_{t})-f(x^{\star})\leq 2\bar{L}\biggl(\frac{\bar{\kappa}-1}{\bar{\kappa}}\biggr)^{t}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}.

Proof sketch.

The key idea is that NAG-free iterates can be viewed as convex combinations of gradient descent (GD) and NAG iterates, initialized appropriately.

Let
s_{t}=(x_{t},y_{t})
, and consider the Lyapunov function

\displaystyle W(s_{t})=f(y_{t})-f(x^{\star})+\frac{m}{2}\left\|x_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}(x_{t}-y_{t})-x^{\star}\right\|^{2},

adapted from
Bansal and Gupta 2019
, which we show to be a Lyapunov function for both GD and NAG. Then, since
W
is convex, using the fact that NAG-free iterates are convex combinations of GD and NAG iterations, we show that

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\mathrm{GD}})W(s_{t+1})-W(s_{t})\leq

\displaystyle\,(1-\alpha_{t})\left[(1+\delta^{\mathrm{GD}})W(s^{\mathrm{GD}}_{t+1})-W(s_{t})\right]

\displaystyle+\alpha_{t}\left[(1+\delta^{\mathrm{NAG}})W(s^{\mathrm{NAG}}_{t+1})-W(s_{t})\right],

where
s^{\mathrm{GD}}_{t}=(y_{t},y_{t})
,
s^{\mathrm{NAG}}_{t}=s_{t}
,
\delta^{\mathrm{GD}}=1/(\bar{\kappa}-1)
,
\delta^{\mathrm{NAG}}=1/(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}-1)
, and
\alpha_{t}\in[0,1]
determines the interpolation between GD and NAG.
Now,
(1+\delta^{\mathrm{NAG}})W(s^{\mathrm{NAG}}_{t+1})-W(s_{t})\leq 0
, but
(1+\delta^{\mathrm{GD}})W(s^{\mathrm{GD}}_{t+1})-W(s_{t})
is indefinite.
However, we know that
(1+\delta^{\mathrm{GD}})W(s^{\mathrm{GD}}_{t+1})-W(s^{\mathrm{GD}}_{t})\leq 0
, since
W
is a Lyapunov function centered at
s^{\mathrm{GD}}_{t}=(y_{t},y_{t})
.
To bridge this mismatch, we augment
W
with an auxiliary term.
Namely, we define

\displaystyle V_{t+1}(s_{t})=W(s_{t})+\frac{1-\alpha_{t}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}}U(s_{t}),

where

\displaystyle U(s_{t})=f(y_{t})-f(x^{\star})+\frac{\bar{L}}{2}\|y_{t}-x^{\star}\|^{2}.

Then, we show
(1+\delta^{\mathrm{GD}})V_{t+1}(s_{t+1})-V_{t+1}(s_{t})\leq 0
and bound
V_{t+1}(s_{t})
in terms of
V_{t}(s_{t})
.
Together, these establish that
V_{t}
is a Lyapunov function for NAG-free that decays at a rate of at least
1+\delta^{\mathrm{GD}}
, matching the worst-case global convergence rate of GD.
∎

4.2
Local acceleration

We now analyze
Algorithm

1
around
x^{\star}
, and prove that it achieves accelerated convergence up to a constant factor.
To formalize this result, we introduce the following set of assumptions, along with a succinct description of their significance and how they are used in the acceleration analysis.

Assumption 4.2
.

The estimates
m_{t+1}
of
Algorithm

1
decay by a factor of at least
\gamma\geq 2
every time they decrease and are always greater than
m/\gamma
: if
m_{t+1}<m_{t}
, then
m/\gamma\leq m_{t+1}\leq m_{t}/\gamma
.

On
4.2
.

This assumption can be enforced in practice with a simple backtracking procedure: if
c_{t+1}<m_{t}
, then
m_{t+1}\leftarrow\min\{c_{t+1},m_{t}/\gamma\}
; else
m_{t+1}\leftarrow m_{t}
.
Since
c_{t+1}\geq m
,
m_{t+1}
can only be adjusted
to a new value
if
m_{t}>m
, in which case
m_{t+1}
is reduced by a factor of at least
\gamma\geq 2
such that
m_{t+1}\geq m/\gamma
.
This implies that
m_{t}
takes finitely many values, which is essentially why we make this assumption.
Although
\gamma>1
would suffice, we assume
\gamma\geq 2
to simplify some results and derivations.
Importantly, this assumption alone does not imply that
m_{t}
converges to
m
, it only ensures that
m_{t+1}\in\mathopen{[}m/\gamma,L\mathclose{]}
.
The next assumption is a standard condition used in optimization theory and satisfied in many practical settings.

Assumption 4.3
.

The Hessian of
f
is locally Lipschitz-smooth at
x^{\star}
: there are
L_{H}>0
and
\epsilon_{H}>0
such that if
\|x-x^{\star}\|\leq\epsilon_{H}
, then
\|\nabla^{2}f(x)-\nabla^{2}f(x^{\star})\|\leq L_{H}\|x-x^{\star}\|
.

On
4.3
.

The condition of Lipschitz continuity for the Hessian is mild.
For example, all twice continuously differentiable functions satisfy this condition, which ensures that the Hessian does not exhibit erratic behavior within a local neighborhood around the optimal point
x^{\star}
.

To present the remaining assumptions, we introduce the following notation.

Notation.

Let
(\lambda_{i},v_{i})
denote the
d
eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
and associated eigenvectors
v_{i}
of
\nabla^{2}f(x^{\star})
. If
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
, then
v_{i}
can be chosen to form an orthonormal basis for
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Hence,
x_{0}-x^{\star}
uniquely decomposes into
x_{0}-x^{\star}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i,0}v_{i}
.
Moreover,
\lambda_{i}\in\mathopen{[}m,L\mathclose{]}
.
In the following, without loss of generality we assume
\lambda_{i}
ordered by their indices, as in
\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{d}
.
Thus,
x_{1,0}
denotes the coordinate of
x_{0}-x^{\star}
in the eigenspace associated with
\lambda_{1}
, the least eigenvalue of
\nabla^{2}f(x^{\star})
.

Assumption 4.4
.

There exists some
\delta_{\lambda}\in\mathopen{(}0,1\mathclose{)}
such that
|m_{t}-\lambda_{i}|>\delta_{\lambda}L
for every
\lambda_{i}>m
, where
m=\lambda_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{d}\leq L
denote the eigenvalues of
\nabla^{2}f(x^{\star})
.

On
4.4
.

This assumption ensures that the value of
m_{t}
remains sufficiently separated from the eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
of the Hessian, where
m
is the smallest eigenvalue.
Although it simplifies the proof by avoiding complications arising from closely spaced eigenvalues, this assumption is not strictly necessary.
Dropping it would require a more involved analysis, using Schur decompositions instead of diagonal matrices, and would introduce additional constant factors. In practice, this assumption holds with high probability due to numerical quantization errors.

Assumption 4.5
.

There exists some
\omega>0
such that
\omega x_{1,0}^{2}\geq\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}
.

On
4.5
.

This assumption, which is mild and holds in most practical scenarios, prevents pathological cases in which
x_{1,0}
, the component of
x_{0}-x^{\star}
along the eigenspace associated with
m
, is arbitrarily small compared to the other components of
x_{0}-x^{\star}
.

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
,
\bar{L}>L
and
\bar{\kappa}=\bar{L}/m
. Suppose that
\bar{\kappa}>\kappa=L/m\geq 4
and that
4.2
,
4.3
,
4.4
and
4.5
hold.
Then, there is some
\epsilon>0
such that if
\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|\leq\epsilon
, then the iterates
x_{t}
produced by
Algorithm

1
satisfy

\displaystyle\|x_{t+1}-x^{\star}\|\leq Cr_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\bar{\kappa})^{t}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|,

where
\sigma
depends on
\gamma
,
C
depends on
\bar{\kappa}
and
\omega
, with
\gamma
and
\omega
given by
4.2
,
4.3
,
4.4
and
4.5
.

Proof sketch.

In the proof, we analyze the two regimes that characterize the behavior of
Algorithm

1
around
x^{\star}
.
In the initial regime,
m_{t}
approaches
m
at an accelerated rate.
In the final regime, which begins once
m_{t}
becomes sufficiently accurate,
x_{t}\to x^{\star}
at a different, but also accelerated rate.

The fundamental mechanisms behind both regimes are captured by the case in which
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
is quadratic.
Having shown that
x_{t}\to x^{\star}
globally at a linear rate, we use
4.3
,
4.4
and
4.5
to show that the general case consists in a perturbation of the quadratic case.
We therefore convey the main ideas behind the proof by focusing our exposition on the quadratic case.

Let
H\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
denote
\nabla^{2}f(x^{\star})
, the Hessian of
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
at
x^{\star}
.
Since
\nabla^{2}f
is locally Lipschitz at
x^{\star}
, it is also continuous at
x^{\star}
, which implies that
H
is real symmetric.
Hence, its eigenvectors
v_{i}
can be chosen to form an orthonormal eigenbasis for
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
With that in mind, we consider
x_{t}-x^{\star}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i,t}v_{i}
.

4.2
implies that
m_{t}
can only take finitely many values, and using this fact we can show that each
x_{i,t}
evolves as sequence of finitely many second-order linear time-invariant (LTI) systems.
We analyze the dynamics of these
d
LTI systems, and show that
x_{i,t}
for which
\lambda_{i}>m_{t}
decrease much faster than
x_{i,t}
such that
\lambda_{i}\leq m_{t}
while
m_{t}
is not close to
m
.
Then, we note that
c_{t+1}=\|H(x_{t+1}-x_{t})\|/\|x_{t+1}-x_{t}\|
, which amounts to a power iteration with some additional dynamics due to the internal dynamics of
x_{t+1}-x_{t}
.
It follows that
c_{t}
approaches
m
at an accelerated rate
r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\bar{\kappa})
, where
\sigma_{\phi}
is a somewhat complicated suboptimality factor.
This can be seen by noticing that
\|H(x_{t+1}-x_{t})\|=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{2}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}
, which is a consequence of
v_{i}
being orthonormal.
We then analyze the final regime.
Since
m_{t}
is nonincreasing, once
m_{t}
reaches some neighborhood
\mathopen{[}m/\gamma,(1+\delta_{m})m\mathclose{]}
,
m_{t}
never leaves it.
Each neighborhood translates into different suboptimality factors.
The lower half of the neighborhood,
\mathopen{[}m/\gamma,m\mathclose{]}
, entails a suboptimality factor of
\gamma
, whereas the upper half,
\mathopen{[}m,(1+\delta_{m})m\mathclose{]}
, entails a factor of
\sigma_{m}=1+2\delta_{m}+2\sqrt{\delta_{m}(1+\delta_{m})}
.
The final convergence rate
r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\bar{\kappa})
accounts for
\sigma_{\phi},\gamma,\sigma_{m}
, the approximation error in the general case and an additional factor due to the Lyapunov argument that we use to analyze the final regime.
∎

5
Numerical Experiments

In this section, we present
Algorithm

2
, a heuristic to solve (
1
) based on
Algorithm

1
and benchmark it against several standard methods described below.

Algorithm

2
extends
Algorithm

1
by incorporating an analogous estimator for
L
, resulting in an entirely parameter-free method.
Since in this section we mostly present experiments involving
Algorithm

2
, we refer to it as NAG-free.

Algorithm 2

NAG-free: a parameter-free heuristic extension of
Algorithm

1
that estimates
L
and
m
.

1:

Input:

T>0,x_{0}=y_{0}

2:

Output:

x_{T}

3:

y\sim x_{0}+U\mathopen{[}0,10^{-6}\mathclose{]}^{d}

4:

L_{0},m_{0}\leftarrow\|\nabla f(x_{0})-\nabla f(y)\|/\|x_{0}-y\|

5:

for

t=0,\ldots,T-1

do

6:

y_{t+1}\leftarrow x_{t}-(1/L_{t})\nabla f(x_{t})

7:

x_{t+1}\leftarrow y_{t+1}+\dfrac{\sqrt{L_{t}}-\sqrt{m_{t}}}{\sqrt{L_{t}}+\sqrt{m_{t}}}(y_{t+1}-y_{t})

8:

c_{t+1}\leftarrow\|\nabla f(x_{t+1})-\nabla f(x_{t})\|/\|x_{t+1}-x_{t}\|

9:

L_{t+1}\leftarrow\max(L_{t},c_{t+1})

10:

m_{t+1}\leftarrow\min(m_{t},c_{t+1})

11:

end

for

Since
L_{0}
and
m_{0}
are random variables, for each experiment we run NAG-free five times with different seeds, take the mean, maximum and minimum of the suboptimality gaps at each iteration, and then plot the mean with a thicker line inside a shaded region between the maximum and minimum.

The code for experiments below was heavily based on two sources:
github.com/ymalitsky/adaptive_GD
and
github.com/konstmish/opt_methods
.
See
Appendix

C
for more details.

5.1
Methods

As baselines, we take GD, NAG and the Triple Momentum Method
(
Van Scoy et al. 2017
, TMM)
, replacing
L
and
m
with problem-specific bounds.
As competing methods, we consider two restart schemes based on
(
O’Donoghue and Candès 2015
)
: one where
L
is replaced with a problem-specific bound, that we refer to as NAG+R, and another where
L
is estimated online with backtracking, that we refer to as NAG+RB.
For NAG+RB, every time the
L
estimate
L_{t}
fails to produce enough descent, it is increased to
1.01L_{t}
and tested again.
This choice of adjustment factor produces less conservative estimates at the expense of more function evaluations, which largely favors NAG+RB since in all experiments we plot the suboptimality gap
f(x_{t})-f(x^{\star})
versus iterations.
As additional methods for comparison, we consider the adaptive gradient method AdGD
(
Malitsky and Mishchenko 2024
)
and its accelerated heuristic, AdGD-accel2, both using
\gamma=1/\sqrt{2}
, as well as a previous variant of the accelerated heuristic
(
Malitsky and Mishchenko 2020
)
, which we denote by AdGD-accel.

5.2
Smoothed and regularized log-sum-exp

First, we solve the smoothed, regularized log-sum-exp problem defined by the objective function

\displaystyle f(x)=\theta\log\Biggl(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\exp\biggl(\frac{A_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}x-b_{i}}{\theta}\biggr)\Biggr)+(\eta/2)\|x\|^{2},

(6)

where
(A_{i},b_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
are
n
observations,
\eta>0
and
\theta>0
is a smoothing parameter that controls how well
f
approximates the
\max
function (see
Appendix

C
for details).
For this problem, we have
L\leq\bar{L}=(1+1/\theta)\sigma_{\max}(A)^{2}+\eta
and
m\geq\eta
, where
A
and
\sigma_{\max}(A)
denote the matrix with rows given by
A_{i}^{T}
and the largest singular value of
A
.
We use an implementation of (
6
) and corresponding initialization settings available at
https://github.com/konstmish/opt_methods
.

We start by assessing how well different approaches to estimate
L
work together with our proposed
m
estimator.
Specifically, we consider three approaches: upper bounding
L
, backtracking, and directly estimating
L
.

Figure

1(a)
shows the suboptimality gap for log-sum-exp (
d=600,\eta=0.1,\theta=0.1
) solved by
Algorithms

1
,
3
and
2
, representing the three approaches, which we denote by NAG-free-L, NAG-free-back and NAG-free, respectively.
We see that
Algorithm

1
outperforms the other methods even though
Algorithm

3
uses an adjustment factor of
1.01
, which leads to minimally conservative bounds at the expense of an unreasonable number of backtracking adjustments.

As a sanity check, we also confirm whether the
m
estimator continues to produce accurate estimates when working along different
L
estimators.
In
Figure

1(b)
we see that
m_{t}
converges to
0.01
for all variants of NAG-free, which is the value of the regularization parameter
\eta
, indicating that the Hessian of the log-sum-exp component of (
6
) is singular at the minimum.

(a)

Suboptimality gap.

(b)

m_{t}
estimates.

Figure 1
:
Suboptimality gap and
m_{t}
estimates for log-sum-exp (
d=600,\eta=0.1,\theta=0.1
).

Having validated that
Algorithm

2
is a reasonable heuristic for this problem, we consider several variations of (
6
) by changing problem parameters.
To assess sensitivity to problem dimensionality, we fix
n=600
and
\theta=\eta=0.1
, and then vary
d\in\mathopen{\{}100,600,3600\mathclose{\}}
.
Likewise, we then vary
n
,
\theta
and
\eta
, fixing the other problem parameters.
The results in
Figures

2
and
8
(see
Appendix

C
) show that NAG-free again outperforms the other methods.

(a)

n=600
.

(b)

d=600
.

Figure 2
:
Suboptimality gap for log-sum-exp (
d=600,\eta=0.1
) using methods from
Section

5.1
.

5.3
Regularized logistic regression

Next, we consider the regularized logistic regression objective

\displaystyle f(x)=-(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\bigl(1+\exp(-b_{i}A_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}x)\bigr)+(\eta/2)\|x\|^{2},

(7)

where
\eta>0
and
(A_{i},b_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\left\{0,1\right\}
are
n
observations from a given dataset, which we take from the LIBSVM library
(
Chang and Lin 2011
)
.
See
Table

1
in
Appendix

C
for dataset details.
Together with
\eta
, the datapoints determine the unknown parameters of
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
, bounded by
L\leq\bar{L}=(1/4n)\lambda_{\max}(A^{\mathsf{T}}A)+\eta
and
m\geq\eta
, where
A
denotes the matrix with rows
A_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}
and
\lambda_{\max}(A^{\mathsf{T}}A)
denotes the top eigenvalue of
A^{\mathsf{T}}A
.

Figure 3
:
Suboptimality gap for logistic regression on six datasets using the methods from
Section

5.1
.

Figure

3
shows results for six datasets, when
x_{0}=0
.
We see that NAG-free is competitive with the other methods, while enjoying significant advantages over them.
For example, NAG-free iterations are simpler and computationally less expensive than those of restarting schemes, which translates into clock time savings.
Moreover, both NAG and TMM require pre-computing conservative parameter bounds to be used as surrogates for the true parameters.
If the bounds are tight, then TMM slightly outperforms NAG-free, which is expected since it enjoys the fastest known provable convergence rate for this kind of algorithm.
But in general, there are no guarantees of how tight the bounds are, which undermines the theoretical advantage that TMM has over NAG-free.

5.4
Cubic regularization

The third problem we consider is cubic regularization, defined by

\displaystyle f(x)=g^{\mathsf{T}}x+(1/2)x^{\mathsf{T}}Hx+(\eta/6)\|x\|^{3},

(8)

where
g\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
H\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
\eta>0
.
Following
https://github.com/konstmish/opt_methods
, from which we borrowed the code for this experiment,
g
and
H
are taken as the gradient and the Hessian at
x=0
of (
7
) initialized with LIBSVM datasets
(
Chang and Lin 2011
)
, without regularization—that is,
\eta=0
on (
7
), but
\eta>0
on (
8
).
Also, as in the original source code, the regularizer parameter is set to
\eta=10\bar{L}n
(see
Section

5.3
.)
Since (
7
) is convex, the eigenvalues of
H
are nonnegative, but not necessarily positive because
H
is taken as the Hessian of (
7
) with
\eta=0
.
Hence, the problem is not strongly convex because the regularizer in (
8
) is cubic.
For the same reason, (
8
) is only locally smooth.
Accordingly, the NAG method for weakly convex problems is used, with step sizes found through a grid search, which is also how the step sizes of GD are determined.

Figure 4
:
Suboptimality gap for cubic regularization on six datasets using methods from
Section

5.1
.

Because this problem is only locally smooth, we expect its curvature to change substantially locally.
Hence, to keep only relevant curvature information, we restart NAG-free after some fixed number of iterations, resetting
L_{t}
and
m_{t}
to the last value taken by
c_{t}
and resetting
y_{t}=x_{t}
.
In this experiment, NAG-free is restarted every iteration.

Figure

4
shows that NAG-free consistently outperforms all other methods substantially.
In particular, exploiting local information with NAG-free leads to better results than finding step sizes for GD and NAG through grid search.

5.5
Matrix factorization

Our last experiment is matrix factorization, defined by the objective

\displaystyle f(U,V)=\tfrac{1}{2}\|UV^{\mathsf{T}}-A\|^{2}_{F},

(9)

where
U\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times r}
,
V\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
,
r<\min\{m,n\}
, and
\|\cdot\|_{F}
denotes the Frobenius norm.
In this experiment, the matrix
A
is taken from the MovieLens 100K dataset
(
Harper and Konstan 2015
)
, so that
m=943
and
n=1682
.
Because (
9
) is nonconvex, we restart NAG-free every 100 iterations to retain only relevant curvature information.
As
Figure

5(a)
shows, after every restart there is a momentary spike in the norm of the gradient—which could be due to overly aggressive step sizes.
But the spikes plummet as local information is updated, and NAG-free outperforms the other methods.

(a)

Gradient norm for matrix factorization with three different ranks using
Section

5.1
methods.

(b)

Normalized performance for
f(x)=(1/2)x^{\mathsf{T}}Ax
, with
A=\diag(1,5,10^{4})
and
x_{0}=[1,10^{5},1]^{\mathsf{T}}
.

Figure 5
:
Matrix factorization and an adversarial toy problem.

5.6
Further experiments

In
Section

C.6
, we present further experiments targeting edge cases of the theoretical results from
Section

4.2
.
For example, on
Figure

5(b)
, we show the normalized performance of NAG-free on a quadratic problem on the left-hand y-axis and the estimate gap
m_{t}-m
with the golden line on right-hand y-axis.
The Hessian spectrum is specifically designed to stress test NAG-free when the initial conditions are concentrated on the second smallest eigenvalue.
We see that instead of slowing down, NAG-free in fact accelerates, and behaves as if the effective condition number had increased until the relative 2-norm of
x_{t}
on the
m
-eigenspace becomes comparable to the total 2-norm.

6
Conclusion

In this paper, we introduced NAG-free, an optimization method designed for Lipschitz-smooth, strongly convex problems.
NAG-free efficiently estimates the strong convexity parameter
m
, while maintaining reasonable worst-case guarantees without restart schemes.
The method couples Nesterov’s accelerated gradient (NAG) method with a lightweight estimator that requires only the storage of one more iterate and gradient already computed by NAG.
We proved that NAG-free converges globally as fast as gradient descent (GD) and achieves acceleration locally near the minimum.
In addition, we proposed a heuristic with good empirical performance, which estimates both
m
and the Lipschitz-smoothness parameter
L
online. Importantly, the
L
-estimator does not require additional storage or computation, and the two estimators make the heuristic free of hyperparameters.

Despite these advances, many questions remain for future work.
An important one is whether methods with stronger convergence guarantees than NAG, such as TMM, can be effectively coupled with parameter estimators.
Another avenue for exploration is whether replacing the curvature term used by NAG-free with similar terms that lie in
[m,L]
can further improve practical performance, such as

\displaystyle\langle\nabla f(x_{t+1})-\nabla f(x_{t}),x_{t+1}-x_{t}\rangle/\|x_{t+1}-x_{t}\|^{2}.

A key limitation of this work is that, while we address the open question of efficiently estimating
m
online without restarts, our experiments demonstrate that both
m
and
L
can be efficiently estimated in practice.
Theoretically analyzing the
L
-estimator will be an interesting future challenge.
Moreover, we conjecture that our
L
-estimator has promising applications in domains beyond the strong convexity setting, and exploring this broader applicability will be another important area for future investigation.

Appendix A
Global convergence

In the following two theorems we prove that, up to a constant factor,
Algorithm

1
converges globally at least as fast as gradient descent (GD).
The first theorem concerns the case where
m_{t}\geq m
, which always holds because
c_{t}\geq m
, by design.
The second theorem further allows for the case where
m_{t}<m
.
We consider this case for the sake of completeness because it can occur under
4.2
, which we use to prove local acceleration.
But since
4.2
only serves a theoretical purpose, we omit the second theorem in the main paper.

Theorem

(
4.1
)
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
,
\bar{L}\geq L
and
\bar{\kappa}=\bar{L}/m
.
Then, the iterates of
Algorithm

1
satisfy

\displaystyle f(y_{t})-f(x^{\star})\leq 2\bar{L}\biggl(\frac{\bar{\kappa}-1}{\bar{\kappa}}\biggr)^{t}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}.

Theorem A.1
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
,
\bar{L}\geq L
and
\bar{\kappa}=\bar{L}/m
.
Also, suppose that
4.2
holds for some
\gamma>1
.
Then the iterates
y_{t}
of
Algorithm

1
satisfy

\displaystyle f(y_{t})-f(x^{\star})\leq

\displaystyle\ 2\gamma\bar{L}\bar{\kappa}^{2}\biggl(\frac{\gamma\bar{\kappa}-1}{\gamma\bar{\kappa}}\biggr)^{t}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}.

(10)

Algorithm

1
takes some estimate
\bar{L}
of
L
such that
\bar{L}\geq L
as an input.
If
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
, then since
(\bar{L}/2)\|y-x\|^{2}\geq(L/2)\|y-x\|^{2}
for any
x
and
y
, we have that
f\in\mathcal{F}(\bar{L},m)
:

\displaystyle f(y)\leq f(x)+\langle\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle+(\bar{L}/2)\|y-x\|^{2}.

(11)

A.1
Case 1:
m_{t}\geq m

The iterations of
Algorithm

1
in which
m_{t}\geq m
can be expressed as a convex combination of appropriate GD and NAG iterations.
In this subsection, we exploit this property to prove that if
Algorithm

1
receives some
\bar{L}\geq L
as input, then it converges at least as fast as GD.
The argument is built upon a Lyapunov function that we denote by
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
.
The superscript “GD” indicates that
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
decreases at a gradient-descent type of rate along iterations in which
m_{t}\geq m
.
In the same vein, in the next subsection we introduce
V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}
and show that it decreases at an accelerated rate along iterations in which
m_{t}<m
.

The Lyapunov function
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
is the sum of two components
W
and
U
scaled.
First, we show
W
is a common Lyapunov function for GD and NAG, then we analyze
U
and finally combine all results to give
Algorithm

1
the same type of convergence guarantees of GD.
To analyze GD and NAG through a common Lyapunov function, we add a trivial momentum step
x^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}
to GD, as in

\displaystyle y^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}=

\displaystyle\ x^{\textup{GD}}_{t}-(1/\bar{L})\nabla f(x^{\textup{GD}}_{t}),

(12)

\displaystyle x^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}=

\displaystyle\ y^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1},

(13)

conforming GD to the algorithmic structure of NAG:

\displaystyle y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}=

\displaystyle\ x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}-(1/\bar{L})\nabla f(x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}),

(14)

\displaystyle x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}=

\displaystyle\ y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}+\theta(y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}-y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}),

(15)

where the coefficient
\theta
defining the affine combination in (
15
) is given by

\displaystyle\theta=(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}-1)/(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}+1),

(16)

a function of an upper bound on the
condition number
of
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
:

\displaystyle\bar{\kappa}=\bar{L}/m\geq L/m=\bar{\kappa}.

(17)

The coefficient
\theta
is defined in terms of
\bar{\kappa}
because we consider the NAG implementation (
14
)–(
15
) where
m
is known.
In contrast,
Algorithm

1
is implemented with an estimate
m_{t}
of
m
, so that

\displaystyle y_{t+1}=

\displaystyle\ x_{t}-(1/\bar{L})\nabla f(x_{t}),

(18)

\displaystyle x_{t+1}=

\displaystyle\ y_{t+1}+\beta_{t}(y_{t+1}-y_{t}),

(19)

where now the coefficient

\displaystyle\beta_{t}=(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-1)/(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}+1)

(20)

is a function of the
estimated condition number

\displaystyle\bar{\kappa}_{t}=\bar{L}/m_{t}.

(21)

The fact that we are able to write NAG-free as a convex combination of GD and NAG does not imply that
m
is known and is only possible for iterations in which
m_{t}\geq m
.

Expressed in the common structure of
12
,
13
,
14
and
15
, GD and NAG can be analyzed with a common Lyapunov function that can be found in
[
Bansal and Gupta 2019
, 5.5]
, and given by

\displaystyle W(s_{t})=\tilde{f}(y_{t})+(m/2)\|z^{\star}_{t}\|^{2},

(22)

where
s_{t}
groups the descent and momentum steps into a single pair, as in

\displaystyle s_{t}=(x_{t},y_{t}),

\displaystyle s^{\textup{GD}}_{t}=(x^{\textup{GD}}_{t},y^{\textup{GD}}_{t}),

and

\displaystyle s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}=(x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t},y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}),

(23)

\tilde{f}
denotes the objective function normalized to 0, meaning that

\displaystyle\tilde{f}=f-f(x^{\star}),

(24)

and
z^{\star}_{t}
is the pseudo-state defined as

\displaystyle z^{\star}_{t}=z_{t}-x^{\star},

\displaystyle z_{t}=x_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}(x_{t}-y_{t})

(25)

For the sake of exposition, we refer to the gradient at iteration
t
as
g_{t}=\nabla f(x_{t})
.
Also, we define

\displaystyle x^{\star}_{t}=x_{t}-x^{\star}

and

\displaystyle x^{y}_{t}=x_{t}-y_{t}.

(26)

Remark A.2
.

Superscripts carry over from
12
,
13
,
14
and
15
to the notation above in the natural way.
For example, by
g^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}=\nabla f(x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})
and
x^{\textup{GD},\star}_{t}=x^{\textup{GD}}_{t}-x^{\star}
.

Gradient descent is slower than Nesterov’s method, so even though
W
serves as a Lyapunov function for both, we should expect
W
to decrease at a faster rate along NAG iterations than along GD iterations.
We now show that
W
decreases at the expected rate for each of the two methods, namely
(1+\delta(\bar{\kappa}))^{-1}
for GD and
(1+\delta(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}))^{-1}
for NAG, where the rate increment
\delta
is defined by

\displaystyle\delta(p)=1/(p-1).

(27)

For the sake of presentation, we also define

\displaystyle\delta^{\textup{GD}}=\delta(\bar{\kappa})=1/(\bar{\kappa}-1)

and

\displaystyle\delta^{\textup{NAG}}=\delta(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}})=1/(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}-1).

(28)

To prove global convergence results for
Algorithm

1
, we work with Lyapunov functions that fit the same template of
W
: a sum of the difference
f(y_{t})-f(x^{\star})
and an appropriately weighted 2-norm of a pseudo-state,
z^{\star}_{t}
in the case of
W
.
We characterize two types of changes for
W
and subsequent Lyapunov functions: the decrease in a fixed
W
from one iteration
s_{t}
to the next
s_{t+1}
and the increase from
W
to
W
for the same iteration
s_{t}
.
Considering the second type of change is necessary because
W
varies with
t
, following
\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}
on (
25
) as it varies with
t
.
The first type of change always looks like an inequality such as (
29
).
To prove (
29
) and similar inequalities, the procedure is always the same: express the difference
f(y_{t})-f(x^{\star})
as the sum of two or three other differences, parse them using properties of
\mathcal{F}(L,m)
, expand the pseudo-states inside the 2-norms and then put everything together.
The proof of the next result illustrates this procedure.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
,
\bar{L}\geq L
,
y^{\textup{GD}}_{t}=x^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
. If
y^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1},x^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}
are given by
12
and
13
, then

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})W(s^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1})-W(s^{\textup{GD}}_{t})\leq-(1/2\bar{L})\|g^{\textup{GD}}_{t}\|^{2}.

(29)

Proof.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
and
\bar{L}\geq L
.
If
y^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}
and
x^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}
are given by
12
and
13
, then plugging
y=y^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}
and
x=x^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
back into (
11
), it follows that

\displaystyle f(y^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1})-f(x^{\textup{GD}}_{t})\leq-(1/2\bar{L})\|g^{\textup{GD}}_{t}\|^{2}.

(30)

Following the procedure described above, we start by expressing
f(y_{t})-f(x^{\star})
as the sum of two differences

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})\tilde{f}(y^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1})-\tilde{f}(y^{\textup{GD}}_{t})=\underbrace{(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})(f(y^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1})-f(y^{\textup{GD}}_{t}))}_{\#1}+\underbrace{\delta^{\textup{GD}}(f(y^{\textup{GD}}_{t})-f(x^{\star}))}_{\#2}.

In the gradient descent implementation above, step (
13
) simply propagates step (
12
), i.e.,
x^{\textup{GD}}_{t}=y^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
and in turn
f(y^{\textup{GD}}_{t})=f(x^{\textup{GD}}_{t})
.
Hence, since (
30
) holds, then the first difference is bounded as

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})(f(y^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1})-f(y^{\textup{GD}}_{t}))\leq

\displaystyle\ -\frac{1+\delta^{\textup{GD}}}{2\bar{L}}\|g^{\textup{GD}}_{t}\|^{2}.

(31)

Moreover, applying (
3
) with
x=y^{\textup{GD}}_{t}=x^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
and
y=x^{\star}
, we bound the second difference as

\displaystyle\delta^{\textup{GD}}(f(y^{\textup{GD}}_{t})-f(x^{\star}))\leq

\displaystyle\ \delta^{\textup{GD}}\langle g^{\textup{GD}}_{t},x^{\textup{GD},\star}_{t}\rangle-\delta^{\textup{GD}}(m/2)\|x^{\textup{GD},\star}_{t}\|^{2}.

(32)

Also from
x^{\textup{GD}}_{t}=y^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
, it follows that
z^{\textup{GD}}_{t}=x^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
and, likewise,
z^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}=y^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}
. Therefore

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})\|z^{\textup{GD},\star}_{t+1}\|^{2}-\|z^{\textup{GD},\star}_{t}\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\ (1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})\|y^{\textup{GD},\star}_{t+1}\|^{2}-\|x^{\textup{GD},\star}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})\Bigl(\frac{\|g^{\textup{GD}}_{t}\|^{2}}{\bar{L}^{2}}-\frac{2\langle g^{\textup{GD}}_{t},x^{\textup{GD},\star}_{t}\rangle}{\bar{L}}\Bigr)+\delta^{\textup{GD}}\|x^{\textup{GD},\star}_{t}\|^{2}.

(33)

To simplify the above and conclude the proof, we use the identities

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{\bar{\kappa}}\Bigr)=\frac{\bar{\kappa}}{\bar{\kappa}-1}\frac{\bar{\kappa}-1}{\bar{\kappa}}=1

and

\displaystyle\frac{1+\delta^{\textup{GD}}}{\bar{\kappa}}=\frac{\bar{\kappa}/(\bar{\kappa}-1)}{\bar{\kappa}}=\delta^{\textup{GD}}.

Multiplying (
33
) by
m/2
, summing the result with
31
and (
32
), then using the above, we obtain

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})W(s^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1})-W(s^{\textup{GD}}_{t})\leq

\displaystyle\ -(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})(1-1/\bar{\kappa})(1/2\bar{L})\|g^{\textup{GD}}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle-(\delta^{\textup{GD}}-(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})/\bar{\kappa})\langle g^{\textup{GD}}_{t},x^{\textup{GD},\star}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ -(1/2\bar{L})\|g^{\textup{GD}}_{t}\|^{2},

proving (
29
).
∎

Lemma A.4
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
and
\bar{L}\geq L
.
Given
y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t},x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}
, if
y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1},x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}
are generated by
14
and
15
, then

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{NAG}})W(s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})-W(s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})\leq 0.

(34)

Proof.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
and
\bar{L}
.
If
y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}
and
x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}
are generated by
14
and
15
, then plugging
y=y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}
and
x=x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}
back into (
11
), it follows that

\displaystyle f(y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})-f(x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})\leq-(1/2\bar{L})\|g^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}\|^{2},

(35)

To prove (
34
), our first move is to express a difference as the sum of three further differences:

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{NAG}})\tilde{f}(y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})-\tilde{f}(y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})=

\displaystyle\ \underbrace{(1+\delta^{\textup{NAG}})(f(y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})-f(x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}))}_{\#1}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{f(x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})-f(y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})}_{\#2}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\delta^{\textup{NAG}}(f(x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})-f(x^{\star}))}_{\#3}.

Since (
35
) holds, then we bound the first difference as

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{NAG}})(f(y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})-f(x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}))\leq

\displaystyle\ -(1+\delta^{\textup{NAG}})(1/2\bar{L})\|g^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}\|^{2}.

Using that
f
is convex and applying (
3
) with
x=x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}
and
y=x^{\star}
, we bound #2 and #3 as

\displaystyle f(x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})-f(y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})\leq

\displaystyle\ \langle g^{\textup{NAG}}_{t},x^{{\textup{NAG}},y}_{t}\rangle,

\displaystyle f(x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})-f(x^{\star})\leq

\displaystyle\ \langle g^{\textup{NAG}}_{t},x^{{\textup{NAG}},\star}_{t}\rangle-(m/2)\|x^{{\textup{NAG}},\star}_{t}\|^{2}.

To address the rest of
(1+\delta^{\textup{NAG}})W(s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})-W(s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})
, we expand
z^{{\textup{NAG}},\star}_{t+1}
, as in

\displaystyle z^{{\textup{NAG}},\star}_{t+1}=

\displaystyle\ x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}(x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}-y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})-x^{\star}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}+\theta(y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}-y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}\theta(y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}-y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})-x^{\star}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ -(1+\theta(1+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}))g^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}/\bar{L}+\theta(1+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}})x^{{\textup{NAG}},y}_{t}+x^{{\textup{NAG}},\star}_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ -\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}g^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}/\bar{L}+(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}-1)x^{{\textup{NAG}},y}_{t}+x^{{\textup{NAG}},\star}_{t},

and then note that the 2-norm term in
W(s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})
is
(m/2)\|x^{{\textup{NAG}},\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}x^{{\textup{NAG}},\star}_{t}\|^{2}
, because

\displaystyle W(s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})=\tilde{f}(y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})+(m/2)\|x^{{\textup{NAG}},\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}x^{{\textup{NAG}},\star}_{t}\|^{2}.

Also, we use the following identities after colons to simplify the coefficients of the terms before colons:

\displaystyle\langle g^{\textup{NAG}}_{t},x^{{\textup{NAG}},y}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{NAG}})\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}-1)/\bar{\kappa}=

\displaystyle\ 1,

\displaystyle\langle g^{\textup{NAG}}_{t},x^{{\textup{NAG}},\star}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{NAG}})\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}/\bar{\kappa}=

\displaystyle\ \delta^{\textup{NAG}},

\displaystyle\|x^{{\textup{NAG}},y}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{NAG}})(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}-1)^{2}=

\displaystyle\ \sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}-1),

\displaystyle\langle x^{{\textup{NAG}},y}_{t},x^{{\textup{NAG}},\star}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{NAG}})(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}-1)=

\displaystyle\ \sqrt{\bar{\kappa}},

Then, we write the 2-norm difference in
(1+\delta^{\textup{NAG}})W(s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})-W(s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})
as

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{NAG}})(m/2)\|z^{{\textup{NAG}},\star}_{t+1}\|^{2}-(m/2)\|x^{{\textup{NAG}},\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}x^{{\textup{NAG}},\star}_{t}\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1+\delta^{\textup{NAG}}}{2\bar{L}}\|g^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle-\langle g^{\textup{NAG}}_{t},x^{{\textup{NAG}},y}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle-\delta^{\textup{NAG}}\langle g^{\textup{NAG}}_{t},x^{{\textup{NAG}},\star}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle-(m/2)\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}\|x^{{\textup{NAG}},y}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\delta^{\textup{NAG}}(m/2)\|x^{{\textup{NAG}},\star}_{t}\|^{2}.

Finally, we put everything together and then cancel several terms to get

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{NAG}})W(s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})-W(s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})\leq-(m/2)\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}\|x^{{\textup{NAG}},y}_{t}\|^{2}\leq 0,

proving (
34
).
∎

Now that we have shown that
W
is a common Lyapunov function for GD and NAG, we introduce the second piece of
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
, the function
U
defined by

\displaystyle U(s_{t})=\tilde{f}(y_{t})+(\bar{L}/2)\|y^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}

(36)

where
\tilde{f}=f-f(x^{\star})
and, in the same spirit of
x^{\star}_{t}
and
z^{\star}_{t}
,
y^{\star}_{t}
is a pseudo-state defined by

\displaystyle y^{\star}_{t}=y_{t}-x^{\star}.

(37)

Lemma A.5
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
and
\bar{L}\geq L
.
If
Algorithm

1
receives
\bar{L}
as input, then it generates iterates
s_{t}
such that

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})U(s_{t+1})-U(s_{t})\leq\bar{L}\langle x^{y}_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle-(\bar{L}/2)\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}.

(38)

Proof.

First, we address the difference
(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})\tilde{f}(y_{t+1})-\tilde{f}(y_{t})
. By definition,
f(x^{\star})\leq f(y_{t})
, thus
-\tilde{f}(y_{t})\leq 0
.
Hence, adding
\pm(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})f(x_{t})
to
(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})\tilde{f}(y_{t+1})-\tilde{f}(y_{t})
and discarding
-\tilde{f}(y_{t})
, we get

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})\tilde{f}(y_{t+1})-\tilde{f}(y_{t})\leq

\displaystyle\ \underbrace{(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})(f(y_{t+1})-f(x_{t}))}_{\#1}+\underbrace{(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})(f(x_{t})-f(x^{\star}))}_{\#2}.

If
\bar{L}
, then plugging
y=y_{t+1}
and
x=x_{t}
back into (
11
), it follows that #1 is bounded as

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})(f(y_{t+1})-f(x_{t}))\leq

\displaystyle\ -(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})(1/2\bar{L})\|g_{t}\|^{2}.

To address the second difference, #2, we apply (
3
) with
x=x_{t}
and
y=x^{\star}
, obtaining

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})(f(x_{t})-f(x^{\star}))\leq

\displaystyle\ (1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})\Bigl(\langle g_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle-(m/2)\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}\Bigr).

Then, we put the two bounds together to get

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})\tilde{f}(y_{t+1})-\tilde{f}(y_{t})\leq

\displaystyle\ -\frac{1+\delta^{\textup{GD}}}{2\bar{L}}\|g_{t}\|^{2}+(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})\langle g_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle-\delta^{\textup{GD}}(\bar{L}/2)\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2},

(39)

where the coefficient multiplying
\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}
on the right-hand side above stems from the identity

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})(m/2)=\frac{\bar{\kappa}}{\bar{\kappa}-1}(m/2)=\delta^{\textup{GD}}(\bar{L}/2).

To address the 2-norm difference in
(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})U(s_{t+1})-U(s_{t})
, we expand pseudo-states inside 2-norms as:

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})\|y^{\star}_{t+1}\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\ (1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})\Bigl((1/\bar{L}^{2})\|g_{t}\|^{2}-(2/\bar{L})\langle g_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle+\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}\Bigr),

\displaystyle\|y^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\ \|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}-2\langle x^{y}_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle+\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}.

Expanding
\|y^{\star}_{t+1}\|^{2}
and
\|y^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}
as above, we get

\displaystyle(\bar{L}/2)((1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})\|y^{\star}_{t+1}\|^{2}-\|y^{\star}_{t}\|^{2})=

\displaystyle\ (1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})\Bigl((1/2\bar{L})\|g_{t}\|^{2}-\langle g_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle\Bigr)

\displaystyle+(\bar{L}/2)(\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}+2\langle x^{y}_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle+\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}).

(40)

Finally, combining (
39
) and (
40
), several terms cancel each other and we are left with

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})U(s_{t+1})-U(s_{t})\leq

\displaystyle\ \bar{L}\langle x^{y}_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle-(\bar{L}/2)\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2},

proving (
38
).
∎

With
Lemmas

A.3
,
A.4
and
A.5
, we have sufficiently characterized
W
and
U
to prove the main result for iterations of
Algorithm

1
in which
m_{t}\geq m
, using the Lyapunov function
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
given by

\displaystyle V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}=W+\dfrac{\bar{\alpha}_{t-1}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}}U,

(41)

with

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}_{t-1}=\begin{cases}1-\alpha_{0},&t-1\leq 0,\\[10.0pt]
1-\alpha_{t-1},&t-1>0,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\alpha_{t}=\frac{\beta_{t}}{\theta}.

(42)

Before using
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
, we show that
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}\geq 0
for iterations in which
m_{t}\geq m
.

Lemma A.6
.

If
m_{t}\geq m
and
m_{t}
is nonincreasing, then
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t^{\prime}}\geq 0
for all
0\leq t^{\prime}\leq t
.
Moreover,
\bar{\alpha}_{t}
is nonincreasing.

Proof.

The assumptions that
m_{t}\geq m
and that
m_{t}
is nonincreasing imply
m_{t}\geq m
for all
t^{\prime}\leq t
.
Moreover,
m_{t}\geq m
implies that
\bar{\kappa}_{t}=\bar{L}/m_{t}<\bar{L}/m=\bar{\kappa}
, therefore

\displaystyle\beta_{t}=\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-1}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}+1}<\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}-1}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}+1}=\theta.

Hence,
\beta_{t^{\prime}}<\theta
for all
t^{\prime}\leq t
.
Therefore,
\alpha_{t^{\prime}},\bar{\alpha}_{t^{\prime}}\in\mathopen{[}0,1\mathclose{]}
and, in turn,
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t^{\prime}}\geq 0
for all
t^{\prime}\leq t
.

Moreover, since
m_{t}
is nonincreasing,
\beta_{t}
given by (
20
) is nondecreasing, hence so is
\alpha_{t}
, by (
42
).
In turn, by (
42
), it follows that
\bar{\alpha}_{t}
is nonincreasing.
∎

Theorem A.7
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
and
\bar{L}\geq L
.
If
Algorithm

1
receives
\bar{L}
as input, then it generates iterates
s_{t}
such that

\displaystyle V^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}(s_{t+1})\leq 2\bar{L}(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})^{-t}\|x^{\star}_{0}\|^{2}.

(43)

Proof.

We establish (
43
) in three steps.
First, we bound
(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})V^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}(s_{t+1})
in terms of
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}(s_{t})
.
Second, we bound
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}(s_{t})
in terms of
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}(s_{t})
.
Third, we use the bounds in an inductive argument.

To bound
(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})V^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}(s_{t+1})
in terms of
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}(s_{t})
, we analyze their difference, which is the sum of one difference involving
W
and another one involving
U
.
We address the one involving
W
first.
To this end, we use the assumption that
m_{t}\geq m
to show
Algorithm

1
iterates can be expressed as a convex combination of appropriate GD and NAG iterates and then we exploit the fact that
W
is convex to bound the corresponding difference.

To show
Algorithm

1
iterates are a convex combination of GD and NAG iterations, we consider fictitious “one-shot” GD and NAG iterations initialized at the given iterate of
Algorithm

1
.
We let
y^{\textup{GD}}_{t}=x^{\textup{GD}}_{t}=x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}=x_{t}
and
y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}=y_{t}
.
We initialize
x^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
and
y^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
“backwards” from
x_{t}
to conform them to the GD iteration constraint that
y^{\textup{GD}}_{t}=x^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
.
On the other hand, since NAG works with arbitrary initial points, we initialize NAG at the
t
-th iteration of
Algorithm

1
exactly.
With these initial points in mind, let
y^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}
,
x^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}
,
y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}
and
x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}
be the GD and NAG iterations produced by
12
,
13
,
14
and
15
. Then, GD, NAG and
Algorithm

1
produce the same descent step

\displaystyle y^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}=x^{\textup{GD}}_{t}-\nabla f(x^{\textup{GD}}_{t})/\bar{L}=\underbrace{x_{t}-\nabla f(x_{t})/\bar{L}}_{y_{t+1}}=x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}-\nabla f(x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})/\bar{L}=y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}.

In turn,
x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}
reduces to an affine combination of descent steps
y_{t+1}
and
y_{t}
of
Algorithm

1
:

\displaystyle x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}=(1+\theta_{t})y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}-\theta_{t}y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}=(1+\theta_{t})y_{t+1}-\theta_{t}y_{t}.

Hence, for all
t\geq 0
such that
m_{t}\geq m
,
x_{t+1}
is a convex combination of
x^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}=y^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}=y_{t+1}
and
x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}
, as in

\displaystyle x_{t+1}=(1+\beta_{t})y_{t+1}-\beta_{t}y_{t}=

\displaystyle\ \Bigl(1+\theta_{t}\frac{\beta_{t}}{\theta_{t}}\pm\frac{\beta_{t}}{\theta_{t}}\Bigr)y_{t+1}-\theta_{t}\frac{\beta_{t}}{\theta_{t}}y_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \Bigl(1-\frac{\beta_{t}}{\theta_{t}}\Bigr)y_{t+1}+\frac{\beta_{t}}{\theta_{t}}((1+\theta_{t})y_{t+1}-\theta_{t}y_{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \Bigl(1-\frac{\beta_{t}}{\theta_{t}}\Bigr)y^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}+\frac{\beta_{t}}{\theta_{t}}((1+\theta_{t})y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}-\theta_{t}y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \bar{\alpha}_{t}x^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}+\alpha_{t}x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1},

where, as defined in (
41
), the coefficients defining the convex combination are given by

\displaystyle\alpha_{t}=\beta_{t}/\theta\in\mathopen{[}0,1\mathclose{]},

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}_{t}=1-\alpha_{t}\in\mathopen{[}0,1\mathclose{]}.

Likewise,
y^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}=y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}=y_{t+1}
trivially implies
y_{t+1}=\bar{\alpha}_{t}y^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}+\alpha_{t}y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}
so, in fact, the entire iterate of
Algorithm

1
can be expressed as a convex combination of GD and NAG iterates, as in

\displaystyle s_{t+1}=\bar{\alpha}_{t}s^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}+\alpha_{t}s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1},

where
s_{t+1}
,
s^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}
and
s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}
comprise the iterates of
Algorithm

1
, GD and NAG:

\displaystyle s_{t+1}

\displaystyle=(x_{t+1},y_{t+1}),

\displaystyle s^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}=(x^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1},y^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1})

\displaystyle=(y_{t+1},y_{t+1})

\displaystyle s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}=(x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1},y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})

\displaystyle=(x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1},y_{t+1}).

Hence, since
W
is convex
2
2

2

By assumption
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
is convex, thus so is
\tilde{f}
.
Moreover, the affine transformation that defines
z^{\star}_{t}
composed with the 2-norm yields a convex function.
That is,
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
is the positive sum of convex functions and is therefore convex.
, we can bound
W(s_{t+1})
solely in terms of GD and NAG iterations:

\displaystyle W(s_{t+1})\leq

\displaystyle\ \bar{\alpha}_{t}W(s^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1})+\alpha_{t}W(s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}).

In turn, since
W(s_{t})=\bar{\alpha}_{t}W(s_{t})+\alpha_{t}W(s_{t})
holds trivially, it follows that

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})W(s_{t+1})-W(s_{t})=

\displaystyle\ \bar{\alpha}_{t}((1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})W(s^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1})-W(s_{t}))

\displaystyle+\alpha_{t}\Bigl((1+\delta^{\textup{NAG}})W(s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})-W(s_{t})\Bigr)

\displaystyle+\alpha_{t}(\delta^{\textup{GD}}-\delta^{\textup{NAG}})W(s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}).

Now, since
y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}=y_{t+1}
and
x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}=x_{t}
, then applying (
11
) with
y=y_{t+1}
and
x=x_{t}
we get

\displaystyle f(y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})-f(x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})=f(y_{t+1})-f(x_{t})\leq-\|g_{t}\|^{2}/2\bar{L}=-\|g^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}\|^{2}/\bar{L}.

Moreover, by assumption,
m_{0}
is initialized in
\mathopen{[}m,L\mathclose{]}
and
\bar{L}\geq m_{0}
.
Hence, the assumption that
m_{t+1}
is computed by
Algorithm

1
from
m_{t}
implies that

\displaystyle\bar{L}\geq m_{0}\geq\dots\geq m_{t}\geq m_{t+1}>0.

(44)

That is,
m_{t}
is positive nonincreasing.
Therefore,
Lemma

A.4
applies because
Lemma

A.4
imposes no restrictions on neither
y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}
nor
x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}
.
So, letting

\displaystyle s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}=(x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t},y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t})=(x_{t},y_{t})\eqqcolon s_{t},

then
Lemma

A.4
combined with both the fact that
\delta^{\textup{NAG}}\geq\delta^{\textup{GD}}
and that
\alpha_{t}>0
, imply

\displaystyle\alpha_{t}\Bigl((1+\delta^{\textup{NAG}})W(s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})-W(s_{t})+(\delta^{\textup{GD}}-\delta^{\textup{NAG}})W(s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})\Bigr)\leq 0.

(45)

The natural next move would be to address
(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})W(s^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1})-W(s_{t})
in an analogous way.
The caveat, however, is that although
Lemma

A.4
applies to NAG iterations with arbitrary
x^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}
and
y^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}
, the same is not true of
Lemma

A.3
.
That is,
Lemma

A.3
applies to consecutive GD iterations, requiring that
y^{\textup{GD}}_{t}=x^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
.
Hence, to be able to apply
Lemma

A.3
, we add
\mp W(s^{\textup{GD}}_{t})
to the difference involving
W
, using a GD iteration
s^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
such that
y^{\textup{GD}}_{t}=x^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
.
That is, we define a fictitious GD iteration
s^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
“backwards” from
x_{t}
using the points that we already defined as
y^{\textup{GD}}_{t}=x^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
, as in

\displaystyle s^{\textup{GD}}_{t}=(x^{\textup{GD}}_{t},y^{\textup{GD}}_{t})=(x_{t},x_{t}).

Although
s^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
need not equal
s_{t}
, we do have that
y^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}=y_{t+1}
and
x^{\textup{GD}}_{t}=x_{t}
, thus

\displaystyle f(y^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1})-f(x^{\textup{GD}}_{t})=f(y_{t+1})-f(x_{t})\leq-\|g_{t}\|^{2}/2\bar{L}=-\|g^{\textup{GD}}_{t}\|^{2}/\bar{L}.

Therefore, since
\bar{L}>0
by (
44
),
Lemma

A.3
applies with the newly defined GD iteration
s^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
, and implies that

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})W(s^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1})-W(s^{\textup{GD}}_{t})\leq-\|g^{\textup{GD}}_{t}\|^{2}/2\bar{L}=-\|g_{t}\|^{2}/2\bar{L}.

(46)

Moreover,
y^{\textup{GD}}_{t}=x^{\textup{GD}}_{t}=x_{t}
implies that
x^{\textup{GD},y}_{t}=0
, thus

\displaystyle z^{\textup{GD},\star}_{t}=x^{\textup{GD},\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}x^{\textup{GD},y}_{t}=x^{\textup{GD},\star}_{t}=x^{\star}_{t}

and

\displaystyle f(y^{\textup{GD}}_{t})=f(x_{t}),

and it follows that

\displaystyle W(s^{\textup{GD}}_{t})-W(s_{t})=

\displaystyle\ f(x_{t})-f(y_{t})+(m/2)(\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}-\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}).

(47)

Applying (
3
) with
x=x_{t}
and
y=y_{t}
, then using the simple fact that a cross product can be bounded by a sum of quadratics, as in
2\langle g_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle\leq\bar{L}^{-1}\|g_{t}\|^{2}+\bar{L}\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle f(x_{t})-f(y_{t})\leq\langle g_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle-(m/2)\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}\leq(1/2\bar{L})\|g_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\bar{L}-m}{2}\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}.

Hence, expanding
\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}
on (
47
) and then using the above inequality, we get

\displaystyle W(s^{\textup{GD}}_{t})-W(s_{t})\leq

\displaystyle\ (1/2\bar{L})\|g_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\bar{L}-m}{2}\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}+(m/2)(-2\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}\langle x^{y}_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle-\bar{\kappa}\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (1/2\bar{L})\|g_{t}\|^{2}-(m/2)\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}-\sqrt{\bar{L}m}\langle x^{y}_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle,

(48)

where
m\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}=\sqrt{\bar{L}m}
follows directly from (
17
).
In turn, if we put (
46
) and (
48
) together, then

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})W(s^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1})\mp W(s^{\textup{GD}}_{t})-W(s_{t})\leq

\displaystyle\ -\sqrt{\bar{L}m}\langle x^{y}_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle.

(49)

Therefore, since
\delta^{\textup{GD}}\leq\delta^{\textup{NAG}}
, combining (
45
) and (
49
), we obtain

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})W(s_{t+1})-W(s_{t})\leq

\displaystyle\ \bar{\alpha}_{t}((1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})W(s^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1})\mp W(s^{\textup{GD}}_{t})-W(s_{t}))

\displaystyle+\alpha_{t}((1+\delta^{\textup{NAG}})W(s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})-W(s_{t}))

\displaystyle+\alpha_{t}(\delta^{\textup{GD}}-\delta^{\textup{NAG}})W(s^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ -\bar{\alpha}_{t}\sqrt{\bar{L}m}\langle x^{y}_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle.

(50)

Next, we address the
U
term in
(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})V^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}(s_{t+1})-V^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}(s_{t})
.
By
Lemma

A.5
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\alpha}_{t}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}}((1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})U(s_{t+1})-U(s_{t}))\leq\frac{\bar{\alpha}_{t}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}}\bar{L}\langle x^{y}_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle=\bar{\alpha}_{t}\sqrt{\bar{L}m}\langle x^{y}_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle,

(51)

where the identity on the right-hand side follows from (
17
).
Combining (
50
) with (
51
), we get

\displaystyle(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})V^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}(s_{t+1})\leq V^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}(s_{t}).

(52)

To continue the proof, we bound
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}(s_{t})
in terms of
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}(s_{t})
.
By (
44
),
m_{t}
are monotonic nonincreasing and so is
\bar{\alpha}_{t}
, by
Lemma

A.6
.
Thus,
\bar{\alpha}_{t}/\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}
is nonincreasing.
Hence, using the definition of
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
, we obtain

\displaystyle V^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}=W+\frac{\bar{\alpha}_{t}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}}U\leq W+\frac{\bar{\alpha}_{t-1}}{\bar{\kappa}}U=V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}.

(53)

In turn, combining (
52
) and (
53
), we get

\displaystyle V^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}(s_{t+1})\leq(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})^{-1}V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}(s_{t}).

(54)

To conclude the proof, we employ (
54
) in an inductive argument.
To establish the base case of the inductive argument, we apply (
11
) with
y=y_{0}
and
x=x^{\star}
, obtaining
\tilde{f}(y_{0})\leq(\bar{L}/2)\|y^{\star}_{0}\|^{2}
.
Then, using the assumptions that
y_{0}=x_{0}
and that
\bar{L}
, we have
z^{\star}_{0}=x^{\star}_{0}=y^{\star}_{0}
, and it follows that

\displaystyle W(s_{0})

\displaystyle=\tilde{f}(y_{0})+(m/2)\|x^{\star}_{0}\|^{2}\leq\frac{\bar{L}+m}{2}\|x^{\star}_{0}\|^{2}\leq\bar{L}\|x^{\star}_{0}\|^{2},

\displaystyle U(s_{0})

\displaystyle=\tilde{f}(y_{0})+(\bar{L}/2)\|y^{\star}_{0}\|^{2}\leq\bar{L}\|x^{\star}_{0}\|^{2}.

Since
\bar{\alpha}_{0}/\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}\in\mathopen{[}0,1\mathclose{)}
, the above inequalities imply

\displaystyle V^{\textup{GD}}_{0}(s_{0})

\displaystyle=W(s_{0})+\frac{\bar{\alpha}_{0}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}}U(s_{0})\leq 2\bar{L}\|x^{\star}_{0}\|^{2}.

(55)

Moreover,
V^{\textup{GD}}_{1}=V^{\textup{GD}}_{0}
.
Hence, if
m_{1}>m
, then combining (
52
) with (
55
) yields

\displaystyle V^{\textup{GD}}_{1}(s_{1})\leq 2\bar{L}(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})^{-1}\|x^{\star}_{0}\|^{2}.

(56)

Having established the base case (
56
), suppose that

\displaystyle V^{\textup{GD}}_{t^{\prime}+1}(s_{t^{\prime}+1})\leq 2\bar{L}(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})^{-t^{\prime}}\|x^{\star}_{0}\|^{2},

(57)

holds for all
t^{\prime}\leq t-1
such that
m_{t^{\prime}}\geq m
.
Then, suppose
m_{t}\geq m
.
By (
44
), we have that
m_{t^{\prime}}\geq m
for all
t^{\prime}\leq t
.
Hence, plugging the induction hypothesis (
57
) with
t^{\prime}=t-1
into (
54
), we obtain

\displaystyle V^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}(s_{t+1})\leq 2\bar{L}(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})^{-t}\|x^{\star}_{0}\|^{2},

recovering (
57
) with
t^{\prime}=t
. Therefore, by induction, (
57
) holds for all
t\geq 0
, proving (
43
).
∎

Theorem

4.1
follows immediately from
Theorem

A.7
.

Proof of
Theorem

4.1
.

From (
41
) and (
22
), we have that

\displaystyle f(y_{t+1})-f(x^{\star})\leq W(s_{t+1})\leq V^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}(s_{t+1}).

(58)

Hence, plugging (
43
) back into the right-hand side of the above inequality, we get

\displaystyle f(y_{t+1})-f(x^{\star})\leq 2\bar{L}(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})^{-t}\|x^{\star}_{0}\|^{2},

which proves
Theorem

4.1
.
∎

A.2
Case 2:
m_{t}<m

In the previous section, we analyzed iterations in which
m_{t}\geq m
.
Now, we analyze iterations in which
m_{t}<m
and also the transition iteration
t
such that
m_{t}\geq m
and
m_{t+1}<m
.
By design of
Algorithm

1
,
m_{t}
is nonincreasing, therefore there is at most one such
t
.

Iterations in which
m_{t}<m

In
Theorem

A.7
, we proved that up to a constant factor,
\tilde{f}(y_{t})
decreases along iterates of
Algorithm

1
in which
m_{t}\geq m
at least as fast as it does along GD iterates.
Now, we prove that up to a constant factor,
\tilde{f}(y_{t})
decreases along iterates of
Algorithm

1
in which
m_{t}<m
at an accelerated rate.
Specifically, if
m_{t}<m
, then
Algorithm

1
achieves the accelerated rate
(1+\delta(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t})})^{-1}
, where

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}=\begin{cases}\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{0}}-1},&t=0,\\
\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}-1},&t\geq 1,\end{cases}

(59)

to work with more concise notation while maintaining it consistent.

Our proof once again consists in an inductive argument built upon descending and ascending bounds on a Lyapunov function.
The function we work with this time is
V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}
, given by

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})=\begin{cases}\tilde{f}(y_{0})+(m_{0}/2)\|w^{\star}_{0}\|^{2},&t=0,\\[10.0pt]
\tilde{f}(y_{t})+(m_{t-1}/2)\|w^{\star}_{t}\|^{2},&t\geq 1,\end{cases}

(60)

where the pseudo-state
w^{\star}_{t}
, analogous to
z^{\star}_{t}
, is given by

\displaystyle w^{\star}_{t}=w_{t}-x^{\star},

\displaystyle w_{t}=\begin{cases}x_{0}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{0}}(x_{0}-y_{0}),&t=0,\\
x_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}(x_{t}-y_{t}),&t\geq 1.\end{cases}

(61)

Once again, we first prove a descending bound and then immediately after prove an ascending one.

Lemma A.8
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
and
\bar{L}\geq L
.
Given
x_{t},y_{t}
and
m_{t}\leq m
, if
s_{t+1}
is generated by
Algorithm

1
, then

\displaystyle(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t+1})-V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t})\leq 0.

(62)

Proof.

The difference
(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t+1})-V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t})
is the sum of two differences, one involving
\tilde{f}
and another involving 2-norms.
We start with the one involving
\tilde{f}
, splitting it into three further differences:

\displaystyle(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})\tilde{f}(y_{t+1})-\tilde{f}(y_{t})=

\displaystyle\ \underbrace{(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})(f(y_{t+1})-f(x_{t}))}_{\#1}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(f(x_{t})-f(x^{\star}))}_{\#2}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{f(x_{t})-f(y_{t})}_{\#3}.

If
s_{t+1}
is generated by
Algorithm

1
, then applying (
11
) with
y=y_{t+1}
and
x=x_{t}
, we get

\displaystyle(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})(f(y_{t+1})-f(x_{t}))\leq

\displaystyle\ -(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})(1/2\bar{L})\|g_{t}\|^{2}.

(63)

At the same time, the assumption that
m_{t}<m
combined with (
3
) imply that for all
x
and
y

\displaystyle f(x)+\langle\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle+(m_{t}/2)\|x-y\|^{2}\leq f(y).

(64)

Hence, plugging
x=x_{t}
and
y=x^{\star}
in (
64
) and using the fact that
f
is convex, we bound #2 and #3 above as

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(f(x_{t})-f(x^{\star}))\leq

\displaystyle\ \hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}\left\langle g_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\right\rangle-\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(m_{t}/2)\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2},

(65)

\displaystyle f(x_{t})-f(y_{t})\leq

\displaystyle\ \left\langle g_{t},x^{y}_{t}\right\rangle.

(66)

Next, we address the 2-norm difference in
(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t+1})-V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t})
, by expanding the pseudo-states inside 2-norms.
One pseudo-state is
w^{\star}_{t+1}
which, using
20
and
61
, we express as

\displaystyle w^{\star}_{t+1}=

\displaystyle\ x_{t+1}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}(x_{t+1}-y_{t+1})-x^{\star}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ y_{t+1}+\beta_{t}(y_{t+1}-y_{t})+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}\beta_{t}(y_{t+1}-y_{t})-x^{\star}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ -(1+\beta_{t}(1+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}))g_{t}/\bar{L}+\beta_{t}(1+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}})x^{y}_{t}+x^{\star}_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ -\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}g_{t}/\bar{L}+(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-1)x^{y}_{t}+x^{\star}_{t}.

After expanding
w^{\star}_{t+1}
inside the 2-norm, we use the following identities after colons to simplify the coefficients of terms before colons:

\displaystyle\|g_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle(\bar{\kappa}_{t}/\bar{L}^{2})(m_{t}/2)=

\displaystyle\ 1/2\bar{L},

\displaystyle\langle g_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle m_{t}(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-1)/\bar{L}=

\displaystyle\ 1,

\displaystyle\langle g_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle m_{t}(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}/\bar{L}=

\displaystyle\ \hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1},

\displaystyle\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-1)^{2}=

\displaystyle\ \sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-1),

\displaystyle\langle x^{y}_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-1)=

\displaystyle\ \sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}.

Thus, the 2-norm difference in
(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t+1})-V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t})
reduces to

\displaystyle(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})(m_{t}/2)\|w^{\star}_{t+1}\|^{2}-(m_{t}/2)\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}}{2\bar{L}}\|g_{t}\|^{2}-\left\langle g_{t},x^{y}_{t}\right\rangle-\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}\left\langle g_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\right\rangle+(m_{t}/2)\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-1)\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle+(m_{t}/2)(2\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}\langle x^{y}_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle+(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2})-(m_{t}/2)(\bar{\kappa}_{t}\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}+2\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}\langle x^{y}_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle+\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}}{2\bar{L}}\|g_{t}\|^{2}-\left\langle g_{t},x^{y}_{t}\right\rangle-\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}\left\langle g_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\right\rangle-(m_{t}/2)\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(m_{t}/2)\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}.

(67)

Finally, combining
63
,
65
,
66
and
67
, cancelling terms and then using the assumption that
m_{t}>0
, we obtain

\displaystyle(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1})V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t+1})-V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t})\leq-(m_{t}/2)\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}\leq 0.

∎

Lemma A.9
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
and
\bar{L}\geq L
.
Given
s_{t}
, if
m_{t-1}\geq m_{t}
and
m_{t}<m
, then

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t})\leq\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}^{2}}V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t}).

(68)

Proof.

We divide the analysis in two cases, each representing a possible sign of
\langle x^{y}_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle
.
Building upon the sign underpinning each case, we bound

\displaystyle\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}-\|w^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\ 2(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}})\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle+(\bar{\kappa}_{t}-\bar{\kappa}_{t-1})\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}.

(69)

Assuming
m_{t}\geq m_{t-1}
, bounds on (
69
) translate into bounds on
V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t})-V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})
, since

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t})-V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})=

\displaystyle\ (m_{t}/2)\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}-(m_{t-1}/2)\|w^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ (m_{t}/2)(\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}-\|w^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}).

(70)

Then, to prove (
68
), we express bounds on (
70
) in terms of
V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}
and
V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}
.

First, suppose
\langle x^{y}_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle\geq 0
.
Since
m_{t-1}\geq m_{t}
, then
\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}/\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}\leq 1
, so that

\displaystyle\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}\leq\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}}=\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}-\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}}.

Hence, applying the above inequality to (
69
) and then adding a nonnegative
\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}
term to it, we get

\displaystyle\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}-\|w^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\ 2\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}-\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}}\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle+(\bar{\kappa}_{t}-\bar{\kappa}_{t-1})\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}-\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}-\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}.

(71)

Plugging (
71
) back into (
70
) yields

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t})-V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})\leq\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}-\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}\frac{m_{t}}{2}\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}\leq\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}-\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t}),

(72)

where the last inequality follows from the definition of
V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}
, (
60
), as
\tilde{f}\geq 0
implies

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t})=\tilde{f}(y_{t})+(m_{t}/2)\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}\geq(m_{t}/2)\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}.

(73)

Thus, rearranging terms in (
72
) and then multiplying both sides by
\bar{\kappa}_{t}/\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}
, we obtain

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t})\leq\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})\leq\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}^{2}}V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t}),

where the second inequality holds because
\bar{\kappa}_{t}/\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}\geq 1
.

Now, suppose
\langle x^{y}_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle<0
.
We start by bounding the gap (
69
).
But given the negative sign of
\langle x^{y}_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle
term, we bound the
\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}
term instead.
To this end, we first use the assumption that
\langle x^{y}_{t},x^{\star}_{t}\rangle<0
to establish that

\displaystyle\|y^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}=\|y^{\star}_{t}\mp x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}=\|x^{\star}_{t}-x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}=\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}-2\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle+\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}\geq\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}.

(74)

To use the above inequality on (
69
), first we rewrite it more conveniently as

\displaystyle\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}-\|w^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\ 2\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}}\langle x^{\star}_{t},\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}x^{y}_{t}\rangle+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}})\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}})\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}\pm\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}}\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}}\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}})\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}}\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}.

(75)

Then, we establish an elementary inequality for 2-norms.
If
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
c\neq 0
, then

\displaystyle(1/c^{2})\|a\|^{2}+2\langle a,b\rangle+c^{2}\|b\|^{2}=\|a/c+bc\|^{2}\geq 0,

so that
-2\langle a,b\rangle\leq(1/c^{2})\|a\|^{2}+c^{2}\|b\|^{2}
, which implies

\displaystyle\|a-b\|^{2}=\|a\|^{2}-2\langle a,b\rangle+\|b\|^{2}\leq(1+1/c^{2})\|a\|^{2}+(1+c^{2})\|b\|^{2}.

(76)

Applying (
76
) with
a=w^{\star}_{t}
,
b=x^{\star}_{t}
and
c^{2}=\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}/\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}
to bound the
\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}
term on (
75
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}})\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\ \sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}})\|x^{y}_{t}\pm x^{\star}_{t}/\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}\|w^{\star}_{t}-x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}\Bigr)\|w^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}}\Bigr)\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}-\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}\|w^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}}\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}.

(77)

Plugging (
77
) back into (
75
) and then using (
74
), we get

\displaystyle\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}-\|w^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}}\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}-\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}\|w^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}}\Bigl(\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}-1\Bigr)\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq
