\displaystyle\ \frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}}\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}-\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}\|w^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}\|y^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}.

(78)

Then, plugging (
78
) back into (
70
) and using the assumptions that
m_{t-1}\geq m_{t}
and
m_{t}<m
yields

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t})-V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{m_{t}}{2}\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}}\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{m_{t}}{2}\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}-\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}\|w^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{m_{t}}{2}\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}\|y^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}}\frac{m_{t}}{2}\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}-\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}\frac{m_{t-1}}{2}\|w^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}\frac{m}{2}\|y^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}.

(79)

Now, as in (
73
), applying
\tilde{f}\geq 0
to the definition of
V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}
, we get

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})=\tilde{f}(y_{t})+(m_{t-1}/2)\|w^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}\geq(m_{t-1}/2)\|w^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}.

(80)

In the same vein, applying (
3
) with
x=x^{\star}
and
y=y_{t}
to the definition of
V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}
, we obtain

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})=\tilde{f}(y_{t})+(m_{t-1}/2)\|w^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}\geq(m/2)\|y^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}.

(81)

Plugging in (
73
), (
80
) and (
81
) back into (
79
), and then moving all
V^{\textup{acc}}_{t+1}(s_{t})
terms to the left-hand side and all
V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})
to the right-hand side, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}}V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t})\leq

\displaystyle\ \Bigl(\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}+\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}\Bigr)V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})

(82)

Multiplying both sides of (
82
) by
\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}/\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}
, and then using the fact that
\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}\geq\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}
yields

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t})\leq\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}_{t}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}\Bigl(\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}+\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}\Bigr)V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})\leq\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}^{2}}V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t}),

where the last inequality above holds because
\bar{\kappa}_{t}\geq\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}
implies the following equivalences hold:

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}+\frac{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}}\leq\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}^{3/2}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}^{3/2}}\iff

\displaystyle\ \sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}\bar{\kappa}_{t}+\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}})\leq\bar{\kappa}_{t}^{3/2},

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\ \bar{\kappa}_{t-1}(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}})\leq\bar{\kappa}_{t}(\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}).

Therefore, both when
\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle\geq 0
and when
\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle<0
, the inequality

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t})\leq\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}^{2}}V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})

holds generically for all
s_{t}
, proving (
68
).
∎

Theorem A.10
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
and
\bar{L}\geq L
.
If
Algorithm

1
receives
\bar{L}
as input and
m_{T}\leq m
for some
T
, then for all
t>T
the iterates
s_{t}
of
Algorithm

1
satisfy

\displaystyle f(y_{t+1})-f(x^{\star})\leq\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\kappa}_{T-1}^{2}}\Biggl(\prod\limits_{i=T}^{t+1}(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{i})^{-1}\Biggr)V^{\textup{NAG}}_{T}(s_{T}).

(83)

Proof.

Since
Algorithm

1
generates
m_{t}
, they are nonincreasing.
Hence,
m_{t}\leq L
for all
t\geq 0
since
m_{0}\leq L
by assumption.
Moreover, if
m_{T}\leq m
, then
m_{t}<m
for all
t\geq T
.
Therefore, if
m_{T}\leq m
, then
Lemmas

A.8
and
A.9
hold for all
t\geq T
.
So, plugging (
68
) into (
62
), we have that for all
t\geq T

\displaystyle(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{i})V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t+1})-\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}^{2}}V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})\leq(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{i})V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t+1})-V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t})\leq 0.

(84)

Rearranging terms, we obtain

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t+1})\leq(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{i})^{-1}\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}^{2}}V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t}).

(85)

Applying the above inductively, we conclude that for all
t>T

\displaystyle f(y_{t+1})-f(x^{\star})\leq V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t+1})\leq\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\kappa}_{T-1}^{2}}\Biggl(\prod\limits_{i=T}^{t+1}(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{i})^{-1}\Biggr)V^{\textup{NAG}}_{T}(s_{T}),

where the first inequality follows directly from (
60
), the definition of
V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}
, since

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}(s_{t+1})=\tilde{f}(y_{t+1})+(m_{t}/2)\|w^{\star}_{t+1}\|^{2}\geq\tilde{f}(y_{t+1}).

∎

We now have completed the analysis of iterations in which
m_{t}<m
.
To prove
Theorem

A.1
, it remains to analyze the transition to this kind of iteration from iterations in which
m_{t}\geq m
.

From iterations in which
m_{t}\geq m
to iterations in which
m_{t}<m

In
Section

A.1
and so far in this subsection, we have separately analyzed iterations in which
m_{t}\geq m
and iterations in which
m_{t}<m
.
To prove
Theorem

A.1
, we must join the analyses, allowing for a transition from the first kind of iteration to the second kind.
Since
m_{t}
is nonincreasing by the design of
Algorithm

1
, there can be at most
t
iterations such that
m_{t}\geq m
and
m_{t+1}<m
.
Prior to this transition iteration, we analyze
Algorithm

1
with
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
and after it, we analyze
Algorithm

1
with
V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}
.
Therefore, to join the analyses, we bound
V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}
in terms of
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
.

Lemma A.11
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
.
If
\bar{L}\geq L
and
m_{t-1}\geq m
, then for all
s_{t}

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})\leq\frac{m_{t-1}}{m}V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}(s_{t}).

(86)

Proof.

To prove (
86
), we split the analysis according to the sign of
\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle
, bounding the gap

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})-V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}(s_{t})=

\displaystyle\ \frac{m_{t-1}}{2}\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}-\frac{m}{2}\|x^{\star}_{t}+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{m_{t-1}-m}{2}\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}+2\frac{\sqrt{\bar{L}}(\sqrt{m_{t-1}}-\sqrt{m})}{2}\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle

(87)

in terms of
V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
or
V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}
.
We consider the case
\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle\geq 0
first.

Multiplying the coefficient of
\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle
on (
87
) by
(\sqrt{m_{t-1}}+\sqrt{m})/\sqrt{m_{t-1}}\geq 1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sqrt{\bar{L}}(\sqrt{m_{t-1}}-\sqrt{m})\leq\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}(m_{t-1}-m).

(88)

Hence, if
\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle\geq 0
, then plugging (
88
) into (
87
), adding a nonnegative
\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}
term, completing a square to form a
\|w^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}
term and then applying (
80
), we get

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})-V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}(s_{t})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{m_{t-1}-m}{m_{t-1}}\frac{m_{t-1}}{2}(\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}+2\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}}\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle+\bar{\kappa}_{t-1}\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{m_{t-1}-m}{m_{t-1}}V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t}).

Moving terms around and then multiplying both sides by
m_{t-1}/m\geq 1
, we get

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})\leq\frac{m_{t-1}}{m}V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}(s_{t}).

Now, suppose
\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle<0
.
In this case, we cannot increase the
\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle
coefficient to complete a square as we did before.
Instead, we complete a square with the given
\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle
coefficient by splitting the
\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}
term using the following identity and then handling an extra
\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}
term,

\displaystyle\frac{m_{t-1}-m}{m}=\frac{\sqrt{m_{t-1}}-\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{m}}\frac{\sqrt{m_{t-1}}+\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{m}}=\frac{\sqrt{m_{t-1}}-\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{m}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\sqrt{m_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{m}}\Bigr).

(89)

To handle the
\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}
term that stays out of the square, we use that
\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle<0
implies

\displaystyle\|y^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}=\|y^{\star}_{t}\pm x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}=\|x^{\star}_{t}-x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}=\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}-2\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle+\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}\geq\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}.

(90)

By the definition of
\bar{\alpha}_{t-1}
, the assumption that
m_{t-1}\geq m
yields
\bar{\alpha}_{t-1}\geq 0
, thus
\bar{\alpha}_{t-1}/\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}\geq 0
.
Moreover,
U\geq 0
by its definition, (
36
).
Since
\tilde{f}
is also nonnegative, from (
41
) we obtain

\displaystyle V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}(s_{t})\geq W(s_{t})=\tilde{f}(y_{t})+(m/2)\|z^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}\geq(m/2)\max\left\{\|z^{\star}_{t}\|^{2},\|y^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}\right\},

(91)

where the last inequality on the right-hand side follows from applying (
3
) with
x=x^{\star}
and
y=y_{t}
.

Splitting the coefficient of
\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}
on (
87
) according to (
89
), we get

\displaystyle\frac{m_{t-1}-m}{2}\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{t-1}-m}{m}\frac{m}{2}\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}=\frac{\sqrt{m_{t-1}}-\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{m}}\frac{m}{2}\Bigl(1+\frac{\sqrt{m_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{m}}\Bigr)\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}.

In the same vein, we rewrite the
\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle
term on (
87
) as

\displaystyle 2\frac{\sqrt{\bar{L}}(\sqrt{m_{t-1}}-\sqrt{m})}{2}\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=2\frac{\sqrt{\bar{L}}(\sqrt{m_{t-1}}-\sqrt{m})}{2}\frac{m}{m}\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=2\frac{\sqrt{m_{t-1}}-\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{m}}\frac{m}{2}\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle.

Plugging the above back into (
87
), adding a positive
\|x^{y}_{t}\|^{2}
term to form a
\|z^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}
term, applying (
90
) and then using (
91
), we obtain

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})-V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}(s_{t})=

\displaystyle\ \frac{\sqrt{m_{t-1}}-\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{m}}\frac{m}{2}\Bigl(\Bigl(1+\frac{\sqrt{m_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{m}}\Bigr)\|x^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}+2\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{\sqrt{m_{t-1}}-\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{m}}\frac{m}{2}\|z^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\sqrt{m_{t-1}}-\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{m}}\frac{\sqrt{m_{t-1}}}{\sqrt{m}}\frac{m}{2}\|y^{\star}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{m_{t-1}-m}{m}V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}(s_{t}).

Finally, moving terms around, we get

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})\leq\frac{m_{t-1}}{m}V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}(s_{t}).

Therefore, both when
\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle\geq 0
and when
\langle x^{\star}_{t},x^{y}_{t}\rangle<0
, the inequality

\displaystyle V^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}(s_{t})\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{m_{t-1}}{m}V^{\textup{GD}}_{t}(s_{t})

holds generically for all
s_{t}
, establishing (
86
).
∎

Now, we are ready to prove
Theorem

A.1
, which combines
Theorems

A.7
and
A.10
into a single result that holds for all iterations of
Algorithm

1
under
4.2
.
To this end, we replace
\delta^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
and
\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}
with a common rate increment for all iterations.
If
4.2
holds, then

\displaystyle\bar{\kappa}_{t}=\frac{\bar{L}}{m_{t}}\leq\frac{\bar{L}}{m/\gamma}=\gamma\bar{\kappa}.

(92)

Plugging (
92
) into the definitions of
\delta^{\textup{GD}}_{t}
and
\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\delta^{\textup{GD}}_{t+1}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{\bar{\kappa}_{t}-1}\geq\frac{1}{\gamma\bar{\kappa}-1}=\delta(\gamma\bar{\kappa}),

(93)

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{t+1}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{t}}-1}\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma\bar{\kappa}}-1}\geq\frac{1}{\gamma\bar{\kappa}-1}=\delta(\gamma\bar{\kappa}).

(94)

Moreover, by (
4
),
m_{t}\leq L
holds for all
m_{t}
generated by
Algorithm

1
.
Furthermore, if
4.2
holds, then
m_{t}\geq m/\gamma
.
Therefore, given
t
and
t^{\prime}
such that
t^{\prime}>t
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t^{\prime}}^{2}}{\bar{\kappa}_{t}\bar{\kappa}}=\frac{m_{t}^{2}}{m_{t^{\prime}}m}\leq\frac{\bar{L}^{2}}{m^{2}/\gamma}\leq\frac{\bar{L}^{2}}{m^{2}/\gamma}=\gamma\bar{\kappa}^{2}.

(95)

Proof of
Theorem

A.1
.

If
m_{t}\geq m
for all
t\geq 0
, then by
Theorem

4.1

\displaystyle f(y_{t+1})-f(x^{\star})\leq 2\bar{L}(1+\delta^{\textup{GD}})^{-t}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}\leq 2\gamma\bar{L}\bar{\kappa}^{2}\biggl(\frac{\gamma\bar{\kappa}-1}{\gamma\bar{\kappa}}\biggr)^{t}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}.

Otherwise, let
T+1
be the first iteration for which
m_{T+1}<m
.
Then, combining (
86
) with (
83
), plugging (
95
) in and then using (
43
), we obtain

\displaystyle f(y_{t+1})-f(x^{\star})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\kappa}_{T-1}^{2}}\prod\limits_{i=T}^{t+1}(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{i})^{-1}V^{\textup{NAG}}_{T}(s_{T})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\kappa}_{T-1}\bar{\kappa}}\prod\limits_{i=T}^{t+1}(1+\hat{\delta}^{\textup{NAG}}_{i})^{-1}V^{\textup{GD}}_{T}(s_{T})

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\bar{\kappa}^{2}(1+\delta(\gamma\bar{\kappa}))^{-(t+1-T)}V^{\textup{GD}}_{T}(s_{T})

\displaystyle\leq 2\gamma\bar{L}\bar{\kappa}^{2}(1+\delta(\gamma\bar{\kappa}))^{-(t+1)}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\gamma\bar{L}\bar{\kappa}^{2}\biggl(\frac{\gamma\bar{\kappa}-1}{\gamma\bar{\kappa}}\biggr)^{t+1}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}.

Therefore, (
10
) holds for all
t\geq 0
.
∎

Appendix B
Local acceleration

In this section, we prove that
Algorithm

1
converges at an accelerated rate to
x^{\star}
, the minimum of the objective function
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
, when the iterates of
Algorithm

1
get sufficiently close to
x^{\star}
.
By accelerated rate, we mean
r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\bar{\kappa})
, expressed in terms of a suboptimality factor
\sigma>1
multiplying the condition number
\bar{\kappa}=\bar{L}/m>L/m=\kappa
, and
r_{\textup{acc}}
defined over
\mathopen{[}1,+\infty\mathclose{)}
as

\displaystyle r_{\textup{acc}}(z)=\frac{\sqrt{z}-1}{\sqrt{z}},

(96)

where
\bar{L}>L
is the upper bound on
L
used in
Algorithm

1
.

Before proceeding, some notation remarks are in order.

Deriving most results assuming
\bar{L}=L
.

We derive most of the results in this section using
\bar{L}=L
to avoid working with the cluttered notation
\bar{L}
.
This does not incur any loss of generality as long as we replace
\bar{L}
and
\bar{\kappa}
for
L
and
\kappa
in the final results.
In fact, the consequence of allowing
\bar{L}=L
is that the eigenvalues of
\nabla^{2}f(x^{\star})
can actually take the value of
\bar{L}
, which cannot occur when
\bar{L}>L
.
That is, we derive more general results that would also apply to the case where
\bar{L}>L
.

Hessian eigenpairs and eigendecomposition of
x_{0}-x^{\star}
.

Let
(\lambda_{i},v_{i})
denote the
d
eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
and associated eigenvectors
v_{i}
of
\nabla^{2}f(x^{\star})
. If
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
, then
v_{i}
can be chosen to form an orthonormal basis for
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Hence,
x_{0}-x^{\star}
uniquely decomposes into
x_{0}-x^{\star}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i,0}v_{i}
.
Moreover,
\lambda_{i}\in\mathopen{[}m,L\mathclose{]}
.
In the following, without loss of generality we assume
\lambda_{i}
ordered by their indices, as in
\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{d}
.
Thus,
x_{1,0}
denotes the coordinate of
x_{0}-x^{\star}
in the eigenspace associated with
\lambda_{1}
, the least eigenvalue of
\nabla^{2}f(x^{\star})
.

Assumption

(
4.2
)
.

The estimates
m_{t+1}
of
Algorithm

1
decay by a factor of at least
\gamma\geq 2
every time they decrease and are always greater than
m/\gamma
: if
m_{t+1}<m_{t}
, then
m/\gamma\leq m_{t+1}\leq m_{t}/\gamma
.

Assumption

(
4.3
)
.

The Hessian of
f
is locally Lipschitz-smooth at
x^{\star}
: there are
L_{H}>0
and
\epsilon_{H}>0
such that if
\|x-x^{\star}\|\leq\epsilon_{H}
, then
\|\nabla^{2}f(x)-\nabla^{2}f(x^{\star})\|\leq L_{H}\|x-x^{\star}\|
.

Assumption

(
4.4
)
.

There exists some
\delta_{\lambda}\in\mathopen{(}0,1\mathclose{)}
such that
|m_{t}-\lambda_{i}|>\delta_{\lambda}L
for every
\lambda_{i}>m
, where
m=\lambda_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{d}\leq L
denote the eigenvalues of
\nabla^{2}f(x^{\star})
.

Assumption

(
4.5
)
.

There exists some
\omega>0
such that
\omega x_{1,0}^{2}\geq\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}
.

To prove
Theorem

4.6
, we first consider the simplified case where
f
is quadratic, and then analyze the general case by showing it is a perturbation of the quadratic case.
To this end, we use the fact established by
Theorem

4.1
that the iterates of
Algorithm

1
converge to
x^{\star}
at a rate no worse than that of gradient descent, regardless of the initial point
x_{0}
.
By that we mean

\displaystyle f(y_{t})-f(x^{\star})\leq 2\bar{L}r_{\textup{GD}}(\bar{\kappa})^{t}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2},

where
r_{\textup{GD}}
is defined over
\mathopen{[}1,+\infty\mathclose{)}
as

\displaystyle r_{\textup{GD}}(z)=\frac{z-1}{z}.

(97)

B.1
Quadratic case

First, we assume the objective function is quadratic and given by
f(x)=(1/2)(x-x^{\star})^{\mathsf{T}}H(x-x^{\star})
, with
H\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.
Every quadratic function
(1/2)x^{\mathsf{T}}Hx+x^{\mathsf{T}}g+f(0)
can be expressed
3
3

3

Since
H
is strongly convex,
H
is invertible and the first-order condition
Hx^{\star}+g=0
admits a unique solution
x^{\star}
. Plugging
x=x^{\star}
back into
f(x)
, and solving for
f(0)
, we get that
f(0)=-\frac{1}{2}x^{\star,\mathsf{T}}Hx^{\star}
. Then, plugging
f(0)
back into
f(x)
and replacing the inner-product
g^{\mathsf{T}}x
with
g^{\mathsf{T}}x=-x^{\star,\mathsf{T}}Hx=-\frac{1}{2}x^{\star,\mathsf{T}}Hx-\frac{1}{2}x^{\mathsf{T}}Hx^{\star}
yields the desired form of
f(x)
.
in the form
(1/2)(x-x^{\star})^{\mathsf{T}}H(x-x^{\star})+f(x^{\star})
, and minimizing the latter is equivalent to minimizing
(1/2)(x-x^{\star})^{\mathsf{T}}H(x-x^{\star})
.
Thus,
\nabla f(x)=H(x-x^{\star})
.
Moreover, since
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
,
H
must be positive definite with all
d
eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
inside
\mathopen{[}m,L\mathclose{]}
.
Hence, assuming
\lambda_{i}
ordered by their indices, we have that

\displaystyle m=\lambda_{1}\leq\dots\leq\lambda_{d}=L.

Since
\nabla^{2}f
is locally Lipschitz at
x^{\star}
, it is also continuous at
x^{\star}
.
Hence,
H=\nabla^{2}f(x^{\star})
is real symmetric in general, not only in the case where
f
is quadratic.
Therefore, by the spectral theorem
[
Conway 2019
]
we can pick eigenvectors
v_{i}
associated with
\lambda_{i}
such that
\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
form an orthonormal basis for
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then,
x_{t}-x^{\star}
and
y_{t+1}-x^{\star}
can be uniquely decomposed in this eigenbasis as

\displaystyle x_{t}-x^{\star}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}x_{i,t}v_{i},

(98)

\displaystyle y_{t+1}-x^{\star}

\displaystyle=x_{t}-\frac{1}{L}\nabla f(x_{t})-x^{\star}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}\Bigl(1-\frac{\lambda_{i}}{L}\Bigr)x_{i,t}v_{i}.

(99)

Substituting
99
for the descent steps yields

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}x_{i,t+1}v_{i}

\displaystyle=x_{t+1}-x^{\star}

\displaystyle=(1+\beta_{t})y_{t+1}-\beta_{t}y_{t}-x^{\star}\mp\beta_{t}x^{\star}

\displaystyle=(1+\beta_{t})(y_{t+1}-x^{\star})-\beta_{t}(y_{t}-x^{\star})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}\left[(1+\beta_{t})\Bigl(1-\frac{\lambda_{i}}{L}\Bigr)x_{i,t}-\beta_{t}\Bigl(1-\frac{\lambda_{i}}{L}\Bigr)x_{i,t-1}\right]v_{i},

(100)

where
\beta_{t}=\beta(m_{t})
is a particular value taken by the function
\beta:\mathopen{(}0,L\mathclose{]}\to\mathopen{[}0,1\mathclose{)}
defined by

\displaystyle\beta(m_{t})=\frac{\sqrt{L}-\sqrt{m_{t}}}{\sqrt{L}+\sqrt{m_{t}}}.

(101)

That is, each component
x_{i,t}
of
x_{t}-x^{\star}
behaves as an LTV system
Hespanha 2009
.
But under
4.2
,
m_{t}
decreases by a factor of at least
\gamma>1
every time it is updated, which implies
m_{t}
only changes finitely many times.
Hence, each
x_{i,t}
behaves as a sequence of linear time-invariant (LTI) systems described by

\displaystyle X_{i,t+1}=G_{i}(m_{t})X_{i,t},

(102)

where
X_{i,t}
denote the vectors of current and past coordinates stacked together as in

\displaystyle X_{i,t}=\begin{cases}\left[x_{i,0}\quad x_{i,0}\right]^{\mathsf{T}},&t=0,\\[5.69054pt]
\left[x_{i,t-1}\quad x_{i,t}\right]^{\mathsf{T}},&t>0,\end{cases}

(103)

and
G_{i}:\mathopen{(}0,L\mathclose{]}\to\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
map estimates
m_{t}
to system matrices given by

\displaystyle G_{i}(m_{t})=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
-\beta(m_{t})\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\lambda_{i}}{L}\Bigr)&(1+\beta(m_{t}))\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\lambda_{i}}{L}\Bigr)\end{bmatrix}.

(104)

Hence, the dynamics of (
102
) is determined by the eigenvalues of
G_{i}(m_{t})
, which are given by

\displaystyle\lambda(G_{i}(m_{t}))=\frac{1+\beta(m_{t})}{2}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\lambda_{i}}{L}\Bigr)\pm\sqrt{\dfrac{(1+\beta(m_{t}))^{2}}{4}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\lambda_{i}}{L}\Bigr)^{2}-\beta(m_{t})\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\lambda_{i}}{L}\Bigr)}.

(105)

The greatest between the two eigenvalues given by (
105
) defines the so-called spectral radius
[
Golub and Van Loan 2013
]
of
G_{i}
, captured by the function
\rho:\mathopen{(}0,L\mathclose{]}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
defined by

\displaystyle\rho(s,\ell)=\max\left\lvert\frac{1+\beta(m_{t})}{2}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)\pm\sqrt{\dfrac{(1+\beta(m_{t}))^{2}}{4}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)^{2}-\beta(m_{t})\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)}\right\rvert.

(106)

We also define a function
\varrho:\mathopen{(}0,L\mathclose{]}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
for the least of the two eigenvalues:

\displaystyle\varrho(s,\ell)=\min\left\lvert\frac{1+\beta(m_{t})}{2}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)\pm\sqrt{\dfrac{(1+\beta(m_{t}))^{2}}{4}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)^{2}-\beta(m_{t})\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)}\right\rvert.

(107)

Note that
\rho
and
\varrho
take an argument “
\ell
” that need not be an actual eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
of
H
, which will be convenient later on.
Before that, in the next section, we prove some auxiliary results on
\rho
and
\varrho
.

B.1.1
Properties of the spectral radius
\rho

Lemma B.1
.

Let
s,\ell\in\mathopen{(}0,L\mathclose{]}
.
The two numbers

\displaystyle\frac{1+\beta(s)}{2}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)\pm\sqrt{\dfrac{(1+\beta(s))^{2}}{4}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)^{2}-\beta(s)\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)}

(108)

have nonzero imaginary part if and only if
s<\ell<L
. If (
108
) have zero imaginary part, then

\displaystyle\rho(s,\ell)=\frac{1+\beta(s)}{2}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)+\sqrt{\dfrac{(1+\beta(s))^{2}}{4}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)^{2}-\beta(s)\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)},

(109)

otherwise, if (
108
) have nonzero imaginary part, then

\displaystyle\rho(s,\ell)=\sqrt{\beta(s)\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)}.

(110)

Proof.

Let
r_{+}
be defined by

\displaystyle r_{+}=\frac{1+\beta(s)}{2}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)+\sqrt{\dfrac{(1+\beta(s))^{2}}{4}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)^{2}-\beta(s)\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)},

and let
r_{-}
be defined by

\displaystyle r_{-}=\frac{1+\beta(s)}{2}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)-\sqrt{\dfrac{(1+\beta(s))^{2}}{4}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)^{2}-\beta(s)\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)}.

Also, let
\Delta
be defined by

\displaystyle\Delta(s,\ell)=\dfrac{(1+\beta(s))^{2}}{4}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)^{2}-\beta(s)\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr).

(111)

If
\ell=0
, then
\ell\leq s
since
s\geq 0
, because
s\in\mathopen{(}0,L\mathclose{]}
.
Moreover, plugging
\ell=0
into (
111
), we obtain

\displaystyle\Delta(s,\ell)=\frac{(1+\beta(s))^{2}}{4}-\beta(s)<0\iff(1-\beta(s))^{2}=(1+\beta(s))^{2}-4\beta(s)<0.

Hence,
\Delta(s,\ell)\geq 0
because
(1-\beta)^{2}\geq 0
.
Furthermore,
1-\ell/L=1
and
\rho(s,\ell)
trivially reduces to form (
109
).

Now, suppose
\ell>0
.
If
\ell=L
, then
1-\ell/L=0
and
\rho(s,\ell)=0
trivially has zero imaginary part and takes the form (
109
).
Otherwise, if
\ell<L
, then
1-\ell/L>0
and
\Delta<0
if and only if

\displaystyle(1+\beta)^{2}\Bigl(1-\frac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)-4\beta<0\iff(1-\beta)^{2}L<(1+\beta)^{2}\ell\iff\frac{L}{\ell}<\left(\frac{1+\beta}{1-\beta}\right)^{2},

(112)

where
L/\ell
is well-defined since
\ell>0
, by assumption, while
(1-\beta)^{-1}
is well-defined because
0\leq\beta(s)<1
for all
s\in\mathopen{(}0,L\mathclose{]}
.
Plugging (
101
) into
\beta
, the squared factor on the right-hand side of (
112
) turns into

\displaystyle\frac{1+\beta}{1-\beta}=\frac{2\sqrt{L}/(\sqrt{L}+\sqrt{s})}{2\sqrt{s}/(\sqrt{L}+\sqrt{s})}=\sqrt{L/s}.

(113)

Thus, by (
112
),
\Delta(s,\ell)
is negative if and only if
s<\ell
.
Hence, if
s\geq\ell
, then
\Delta\geq 0
, which combined with the the assumption that
L>\ell
implies

\displaystyle 1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}=\left\lvert 1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\right\rvert>0,

so that

\displaystyle\frac{1+\beta}{2}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)\geq\sqrt{\frac{(1+\beta)^{2}}{4}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)^{2}-\beta\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)}=\sqrt{\Delta}.

Plugging the above inequality back into
r_{+}
and
r_{-}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\lvert r_{+}\rvert

\displaystyle=r_{+}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\beta}{2}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)+\sqrt{\Delta}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1+\beta}{2}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)-\sqrt{\Delta}

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\frac{1+\beta}{2}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)-\sqrt{\Delta}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\lvert r_{-}\rvert.

That is,
\rho(s,\ell)
takes the form (
109
).

Finally, if
s<\ell
, then
\Delta(s,\ell)
is negative, so
r_{+}
and
r_{-}
are complex conjugates with the same norm given by

\displaystyle\lvert r_{+}\rvert=\sqrt{\frac{1+\beta(s)^{2}}{4}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)^{2}+\beta(s)\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)-\dfrac{(1+\beta(s))^{2}}{4}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)^{2}}=\sqrt{\beta(s)\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\ell}{L}\Bigr)}.

Therefore,
\rho(s,\ell)
takes the form (
110
).
∎

Corollary B.2
.

If
m_{t}\in\mathopen{(}0,L\mathclose{]}
, then the eigenvalues of
G_{i}(m_{t})
have nonzero imaginary part if and only if
m_{t}<\lambda_{i}<L
.
Moreover, if
\lambda_{i}<L
, then the eigenvalues of
G_{i}(m_{t})
coincide if and only if
m_{t}=\lambda_{i}
.
Furthermore, if
\lambda_{i}<m_{t}
, then the eigenvalues of
G_{i}(m_{t})
are positive and distinct.

Proof.

Plugging
s=m_{t}
and
\ell=\lambda_{i}
into (
108
), we recover the two eigenvalues of
G_{i}(m_{t})
which, by
Lemma

B.1
, have nonzero imaginary part if and only if
m_{t}<\lambda_{i}<L
.

Moreover, the eigenvalues of
G_{i}(m_{t})
coincide if and only if the discriminant (
111
) is zero for
\ell=\lambda_{i}
and
s=m_{t}
.
In turn, by (
112
) and (
113
), the discriminant (
111
) is zero for
\ell=\lambda_{i}
and
s=m_{t}
if and only if
m_{t}=\lambda_{i}
.

Furthermore, if
\lambda_{i}<m_{t}
, then for all
\lambda_{i}\in\mathopen{(}0,L\mathclose{]}
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1+\beta}{2}\Bigl(1-\frac{\lambda_{i}}{L}\Bigr)\geq\sqrt{\Delta(m_{t},\lambda_{i})}>0.

Therefore, the eigenvalues of
G_{i}(m_{t})
are positive and distinct.
∎

Lemma B.3
.

Given
a
and
b
such that
0\leq a<b\leq L
, then
\rho(s,b)<\rho(s,a)
for all
s\in\mathopen{(}0,L\mathclose{]}
.
In particular, if
b\in\mathopen{(}m,L\mathclose{]}
, then
\rho(s,b)<\rho(s,m)
for all
s\in\mathopen{(}0,L\mathclose{]}
.

Proof.

Consider the following two cases:

case 1 (
b\leq s
).
By assumption,
s\in\mathopen{(}0,L\mathclose{]}
, hence
s\leq L
and if
b\leq s
, then
1-b/L\geq 0
.
Moreover,
a<b\leq s
, so
Lemma

B.1
implies
\rho(s,a)
and
\rho(s,b)
both take form (
109
).
If, in addition
s=L
, then
\beta=0
, which when substituted back into (
109
) yields

\displaystyle\rho(s,b)=1-b/L<1-a/L=\rho(s,a).

Otherwise, if
s<L
, then
\beta>0
. Moreover,
a<b\leq s
, so that
b-a>0
, therefore

\displaystyle\Delta(s,b)<\Delta(s,a)\iff

\displaystyle\ (1+\beta)^{2}\frac{b^{2}-a^{2}}{L}

\displaystyle<2((1+\beta)^{2}-2\beta)(b-a)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\ (1+\beta)^{2}\frac{b+a}{L}

\displaystyle<2(1+\beta^{2})

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\ \frac{4(L/s)}{(\sqrt{L/s}+1)^{2}}\frac{b+a}{L}

\displaystyle<2(1+\beta^{2}),

where the last equivalence follows at once from (
101
).
Furthermore,
a<b\leq s<L
, thus
\sqrt{L/s}+1>2
and

\displaystyle\frac{4L/s}{(\sqrt{L/s}+1)^{2}}\frac{b+a}{L}=\frac{4}{(\sqrt{L/s}+1)^{2}}\frac{b+a}{s}\leq\frac{8}{(\sqrt{L/s}+1)^{2}}<2(1+\beta^{2}).

Thus,
\Delta(s,b)<\Delta(s,a)
.
Hence, since
\rho(s,a)
and
\rho(s,b)
are given by (
109
) and
1-b/L<1-a/L
, it follows that

\displaystyle\rho(s,b)=\frac{1+\beta}{2}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{b}{L}\Bigr)+\sqrt{\Delta(s,b)}<\frac{1+\beta}{2}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{a}{L}\Bigr)+\sqrt{\Delta(s,a)}=\rho(s,a).

case 2 (
s<b
).
By assumption
b\leq L
, so
a<b\leq L
and it follows that

\displaystyle\dfrac{(1+\beta)^{2}}{4}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{a}{L}\Bigr)^{2}-\beta\Bigl(1-\dfrac{b}{L}\Bigr)>\dfrac{(1+\beta)^{2}}{4}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{a}{L}\Bigr)^{2}-\beta\Bigl(1-\dfrac{a}{L}\Bigr)\geq 0,

that is

\displaystyle 0\leq\beta\Bigl(1-\dfrac{b}{L}\Bigr)<\dfrac{(1+\beta)^{2}}{4}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{a}{L}\Bigr)^{2}.

If, in addition
b=L
, then
\rho(s,b)=0
the above inequality implies
\rho(s,b)<\rho(s,a)
.
Otherwise, it must be that
s<b
, in which case
\rho(s,b)
takes the form (
110
) by
Lemma

B.1
and the above inequality yields

\displaystyle\rho(s,b)=\sqrt{\beta\Bigl(1-\dfrac{b}{L}\Bigr)}<\dfrac{1+\beta}{2}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{a}{L}\Bigr)\leq\rho(s,a).

∎

Lemma B.4
.

For every
s\in\mathopen{(}0,L\mathclose{]}
and every
\ell\in\mathopen{[}m,L\mathclose{]}
,
\rho(s,\ell)\leq r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)<1
.

Proof.

Let
s\in\mathopen{(}0,L\mathclose{]}
and
\ell\in\mathopen{[}m,L\mathclose{]}
.
By
Lemma

B.3
,
\rho(s,\ell)\leq\rho(s,m)
, so it suffices to show
\rho(s,m)\leq r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)
.
If
m<m_{t}
, then by
Lemma

B.1
, the eigenvalues of
G_{1}(s)
have zero imaginary part and, omitting the argument
s
in
\beta=\beta(s)
,
\rho(s,m)
is given by

\displaystyle\rho(s,m)=\frac{1+\beta}{2}\Bigl(1-\frac{m}{L}\Bigr)+\sqrt{\frac{(1+\beta)^{2}}{4}\Bigl(1-\frac{m}{L}\Bigr)^{2}-\beta\Bigl(1-\frac{m}{L}\Bigr)}.

Hence, after simple manipulations, we obtain the equivalences

\displaystyle\rho(s,m)\leq r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)\iff

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{(1+\beta)^{2}}{4}\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{\kappa}\Bigr)^{2}-\beta\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{\kappa}\Bigr)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1-\beta}{2}\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\beta)^{2}}{4}\Bigl(\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}\Bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(1-\beta)^{2}}{4}\Bigl(\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}\Bigr)^{2}+\beta\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}.

Since
(1+\beta)^{2}=(1-\beta)^{2}+4\beta
,
\beta\geq 0
and
(\kappa-1)<\kappa
, it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\beta)^{2}}{4}\Bigl(\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}\Bigr)^{2}=\frac{(1-\beta)^{2}+4\beta}{4}\Bigl(\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}\Bigr)^{2}\leq\frac{(1-\beta)^{2}}{4}\Bigl(\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}\Bigr)^{2}+\beta\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}.

Therefore,
\rho(s,m)\leq r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)
.
Otherwise, if
s\leq m
, then by
Lemma

B.1
the eigenvalues of
G_{1}(s)
are complex, so that

\displaystyle\rho(s,m)=\sqrt{\beta\Bigl(1-\frac{m}{L}\Bigr)}.

Hence, after simple manipulations, we obtain the equivalences

\displaystyle\rho(s,m)\leq r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)\iff\beta\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}\leq\Bigl(\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}\Bigr)^{2}\iff\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}-1}{\sqrt{\kappa}+1}\leq\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}-1}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}+1}{\sqrt{\kappa}}.

Since the right-hand side inequality above holds, so does
\rho(s,m)\leq r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)
and we are done.
∎

Lemma B.5
.

If
4.4
holds, then
|\zeta_{i}-\xi_{i}|\geq\sqrt{\delta_{L}\delta_{\lambda}}
, where
\zeta_{i}=\zeta_{i}(m_{t})
and
\xi_{i}=\xi_{i}(m_{t})
denote the eigenvalues of
G_{i}(m_{t})
and
\delta_{L}=(\bar{L}-L)/\bar{L}
.

Proof.

If
4.4
holds, then there exists some
\delta_{\lambda}>0
such that
|m_{t}-\lambda_{i}|\geq\delta_{\lambda}
.
Moreover, since
\bar{L}>L
, we have that
\delta_{L}=(\bar{L}-L)/\bar{L}
.
Moreover, whether
\zeta_{i}=\zeta_{i}(m_{t})
and
\xi_{i}=\xi_{i}(m_{t})
are complex or real, we have that

\displaystyle|\zeta_{i}-\xi_{i}|=2\biggl|\frac{(1+\beta)^{2}}{4}\frac{(\bar{L}-\lambda_{i})^{2}}{\bar{L}^{2}}-\beta\frac{\bar{L}-\lambda_{i}}{\bar{L}}\biggr|^{1/2}=\frac{1+\beta}{\bar{L}}|(\bar{L}-\lambda_{i})(m_{t}-\lambda_{i})|^{1/2}\geq\sqrt{\delta_{L}\delta_{\lambda}},

where in the last equality we have replaced
L
with
\bar{L}
in the identity

\displaystyle\frac{4\beta L}{(1+\beta)^{2}}=4\frac{\sqrt{L}-\sqrt{s}}{\sqrt{L}+\sqrt{s}}\frac{(\sqrt{L}+\sqrt{s})^{2}}{4L}L=L-s.

(114)

∎

B.1.2
Sufficiently accurate
m_{t}
estimates

In this section, we determine how good the estimate
m_{t}
must be for
x_{t}
to converge to
x^{\star}
at an accelerated rate.
From
Lemma

B.3
, it follows that
\rho(m_{t},m)
dominates the convergence of
x_{t}
, therefore our goal is to characterize
\sigma=\sigma(m_{t})
such that
\rho(m_{t},m)\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)
, which may represent a suboptimality factor relative to the optimal convergence rate of
r_{\textup{acc}}(\kappa)
.

By the arguments in
Section

A.2
, and
Theorem

A.10
in particular, if
m_{t}<m
, then the iterates converge at an accelerated rate.
So, in this section, we focus on
m_{t}\in\mathopen{[}m,(1+\delta_{m})m\mathclose{]}
, where
\delta_{m}>0
is a small number.
We proceed in two steps.
First, we bound
\rho(m_{t},m)
for
m_{t}\in\mathopen{[}m,(1+\delta)m\mathclose{]}
in terms of a rate
r_{\delta}
that depends on the relative precision
\delta>0
and the condition number
\kappa
.
Second, given some
\sigma>0
, we characterize
\delta_{\sigma}
for which
r_{\delta}(\kappa)\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)
holds for all
\delta\in\mathopen{(}0,\delta_{\sigma}\mathclose{]}
and
\kappa\geq 1+\delta
.
The rate
r_{\delta}
is parameterized by
\delta\in\mathopen{(}0,1\mathclose{)}
and defined over
z\geq 1+\delta
as

\displaystyle r_{\delta}(z)=\frac{1+\beta_{\delta}(z)}{2}\dfrac{z-1}{z}+\sqrt{\dfrac{(1+\beta_{\delta}(z))^{2}}{4}\Bigl(\dfrac{z-1}{z}\Bigr)^{2}-\beta_{\delta}(z)\dfrac{z-1}{z}},

(115)

where
\beta_{\delta}
is also defined over
z\geq 1+\delta
as

\displaystyle\beta_{\delta}(z)=\frac{\sqrt{z}-\sqrt{1+\delta}}{\sqrt{z}+\sqrt{1+\delta}}.

(116)

Lemma B.6
.

If
m\leq s\leq(1+\delta)m\leq L
, then
\rho(s,m)\leq r_{\delta}(\kappa)
for all
\kappa\geq 1+\delta
.

Proof.

Let
m\leq s\leq(1+\delta)m\leq L
.
Since
m\leq s\leq L
, then by
Lemma

B.1

\displaystyle\rho(s,m)=\frac{1+\beta(L,s)}{2}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{m}{L}\Bigr)+\sqrt{\dfrac{(1+\beta(L,s))^{2}}{4}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{m}{L}\Bigr)^{2}-\beta(L,s)\Bigl(1-\dfrac{m}{L}\Bigr)}.

Omitting the arguments in
\beta=\beta(L,s)
and using the identity (
114
), the discriminant above can be expressed as

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\beta)^{2}}{4}\Bigl(1-\dfrac{m}{L}\Bigr)^{2}-\beta\Bigl(1-\dfrac{m}{L}\Bigr)=\frac{4L(L-m)(s-m)}{4L^{2}(\sqrt{L}+\sqrt{s})^{2}}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{(L-m)(s-m)}{L(\sqrt{L}+\sqrt{s})^{2}}.

Plugging the above expression back into
\rho(s,m)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\rho(s,m)=\frac{\sqrt{L}}{\sqrt{L}+\sqrt{s}}\frac{L-m}{L}+\frac{\sqrt{L-m}}{\sqrt{L}}\frac{\sqrt{s-m}}{\sqrt{L}+\sqrt{s}}=\frac{\sqrt{L-m}}{\sqrt{L}}\frac{\sqrt{L-m}+\sqrt{s-m}}{\sqrt{L}+\sqrt{s}}.

The right-hand side above is increasing in
s\geq m
since
Ls>(L-m)(s-m)
, which implies that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\frac{\sqrt{L-m}+\sqrt{s-m}}{\sqrt{L}+\sqrt{s}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{s-m}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}+\sqrt{s}}-\frac{\sqrt{L-m}+\sqrt{s-m}}{2\sqrt{s}(\sqrt{L}+\sqrt{s})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m+\sqrt{Ls}-\sqrt{(L-m)(s-m)}}{2\sqrt{s}\sqrt{s-m}(\sqrt{L}+\sqrt{s})^{2}}

\displaystyle>0.

Therefore, for all
m\leq s\leq(1+\delta)m
and
\kappa\geq 1+\delta
, we have that

\displaystyle\rho(s,m)\leq\rho((1+\delta)m,m)=r_{\delta}(\kappa).

∎

Next, we bound
r_{\delta}
in terms of
r_{\textup{acc}}
.
We start with an identity involving
\beta_{\delta}(\kappa)
, analogous to (
114
):

\displaystyle 4\frac{\beta_{\delta}(\kappa)}{(1+\beta_{\delta}(\kappa))^{2}}=4\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}-\sqrt{1+\delta}}{\sqrt{\kappa}+\sqrt{1+\delta}}\frac{(\sqrt{\kappa}+\sqrt{1+\delta})^{2}}{4\kappa}=\frac{\kappa-(1+\delta)}{\kappa}.

Plugging the above identity into the discriminant of
r_{\delta}(\kappa)
yields

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\beta_{\delta}(\kappa))^{2}}{4}\Bigl(\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}\Bigr)^{2}-\beta_{\delta}(\kappa)\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{(\sqrt{\kappa}+\sqrt{1+\delta})^{2}}\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}\Bigl(\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}-\frac{\kappa-(1+\delta)}{\kappa}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa-1}{(\sqrt{\kappa}+\sqrt{1+\delta})^{2}}\frac{\delta}{\kappa}.

In turn, plugging the above expression for the discriminant back into
r_{\delta}(\kappa)
, we obtain an alternative expression for
r_{\delta}(\kappa)
:

\displaystyle r_{\delta}(\kappa)=\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}}{\sqrt{\kappa}+\sqrt{1+\delta}}\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}+\frac{\sqrt{\kappa-1}}{\sqrt{\kappa}+\sqrt{1+\delta}}\frac{\sqrt{\delta}}{\sqrt{\kappa}}=\frac{\sqrt{\kappa-1}}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\frac{\sqrt{\kappa-1}+\sqrt{\delta}}{\sqrt{\kappa}+\sqrt{1+\delta}}.

(117)

Using this alternative expression, we obtain the following.

Lemma B.7
.

Given
\sigma>1
, then
r_{\delta}(\kappa)\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma^{\prime}\kappa)
for all
\delta\in\mathopen{(}0,\delta_{\sigma}\mathclose{]}
,
\sigma^{\prime}\geq\sigma
and
\kappa\geq 1+\delta
, where
\delta_{\sigma}=(\sigma-1)^{2}/4\sigma
.
Conversely, given
\delta>0
, then
r_{\delta^{\prime}}(\kappa)\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)
for all
\delta^{\prime}\in\mathopen{(}0,\delta\mathclose{]}
,
\sigma\geq\sigma_{\delta}
and
\kappa\geq 1+\delta^{\prime}
, where
\sigma_{\delta}=1+2\delta+2\sqrt{\delta(1+\delta)}
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma>1
.
From (
117
) and (
96
), it follows that the condition that
r_{\delta}(\kappa)\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)
for some
\delta>0
and
\kappa\geq 1+\delta
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\kappa-1}}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\frac{\sqrt{\kappa-1}+\sqrt{\delta}}{\sqrt{\kappa}+\sqrt{1+\delta}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{\sigma\kappa}-1}{\sqrt{\sigma\kappa}}.

(118)

By successively manipulating (
118
), it follows that

\displaystyle r_{\delta}(\kappa)\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)

\displaystyle\iff\sqrt{\kappa-1}(\sqrt{\kappa-1}+\sqrt{\delta})\sqrt{\sigma}\leq(\sqrt{\sigma\kappa}-1)(\sqrt{\kappa}+\sqrt{1+\delta})

\displaystyle\iff\sqrt{\kappa}+\sqrt{1+\delta}\leq(1+\sqrt{(1+\delta)\kappa}-\sqrt{\delta(\kappa-1)})\sqrt{\sigma}

\displaystyle\iff\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}+\sqrt{1+\delta}}{1+\sqrt{(1+\delta)\kappa}-\sqrt{\delta(\kappa-1)}}\leq\sqrt{\sigma}.

(119)

Taking the derivative of the left-hand side of the (
119
) with respect to
\kappa
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\kappa}\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}+\sqrt{1+\delta}}{1+\sqrt{(1+\delta)\kappa}-\sqrt{\delta(\kappa-1)}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\sqrt{\kappa}+\delta\kappa\sqrt{(1+\delta)}-\delta\sqrt{\delta(\kappa-1)\kappa}}{2\kappa\sqrt{\delta(\kappa-1)}(1+\sqrt{(1+\delta)\kappa}-\sqrt{\delta(\kappa-1)})^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{2\sqrt{\delta\kappa(\kappa-1)}(1+\sqrt{(1+\delta)\kappa}-\sqrt{\delta(\kappa-1)})}>0.

That is, the left-hand side of (
119
) is increasing in
\kappa\geq 1+\delta
and it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}+\sqrt{1+\delta}}{1+\sqrt{(1+\delta)\kappa}-\sqrt{\delta(\kappa-1)}}\leq\lim_{k\to+\infty}\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}+\sqrt{1+\delta}}{1+\sqrt{(1+\delta)\kappa}-\sqrt{\delta(\kappa-1)}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\delta}-\sqrt{\delta}}.

Moreover,
1/(\sqrt{1+\delta}-\sqrt{\delta})
is increasing in
\delta>0
.
Therefore, if
\delta_{\sigma}=(\sigma-1)^{2}/4\sigma
, then for all
\delta\in\mathopen{(}0,\delta_{\sigma}\mathclose{]}
,
\kappa\geq 1+\delta
and
\sigma^{\prime}\geq\sigma
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}+\sqrt{1+\delta}}{1+\sqrt{(1+\delta)\kappa}-\sqrt{\delta(\kappa-1)}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\delta}-\sqrt{\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\delta_{\sigma}}-\sqrt{\delta_{\sigma}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\sqrt{\sigma}}{\sqrt{(1+\sigma)^{2}}-\sqrt{(\sigma-1)^{2}}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\sigma}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\sigma^{\prime}}.

Conversely, given
\delta>0
, if
\delta^{\prime}\in\mathopen{(}0,\delta\mathclose{]}
and
\sigma\geq\sigma_{\delta}
, where
\sigma_{\delta}=1+2\delta+2\sqrt{\delta(1+\delta)}
, then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\delta^{\prime}}-\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\delta}-\sqrt{\delta}}=\sqrt{\sigma_{\delta}}\leq\sqrt{\sigma}.

Therefore,
r_{\delta^{\prime}}(\kappa)\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)
for all
\delta^{\prime}\leq\delta
,
\kappa\geq 1+\delta^{\prime}
and
\sigma\geq\sigma_{\delta}
.
∎

Corollary B.8
.

Given
\sigma>1
, then
\rho(s,m)\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma^{\prime}\kappa)
for all
s\in\mathopen{[}m,(1+\delta)m\mathclose{]}
,
\delta\in\mathopen{(}0,\delta_{\sigma}\mathclose{]}
,
\sigma^{\prime}\geq\sigma
and
\kappa\geq 1+\delta
, where
\delta_{\sigma}=(\sigma-1)^{2}/4\sigma
.
Conversely, given
\delta>0
, then
\rho(s,m)\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)
for all
s\in\mathopen{[}m,(1+\delta)m\mathclose{]}
,
\delta^{\prime}\in\mathopen{(}0,\delta\mathclose{]}
,
\sigma\geq\sigma_{\delta}
and
\kappa\geq 1+\delta^{\prime}
, where
\sigma_{\delta}=1+2\delta+2\sqrt{\delta(1+\delta)}
.

Proof.

The corollary follows by combining
Lemmas

B.6
and
B.7
.
∎

B.1.3
Iterate dynamics between
m_{t}
updates

Through
G_{i}(m_{t})
, the dynamics of
X_{i,t}
are determined by
m_{t}
, which is updated by
Algorithm

1

after
the
t
-th iterate is computed.
Moreover, under
4.2
,
m_{t}
is updated at most
\log_{\gamma}\kappa+1
times.
So, suppose the estimates
m_{t}
take
M+1\leq\log_{\gamma}\kappa+1
values.
Then, let
t_{j}
denote the iteration in which
m_{t}
is adjusted to its
j
-th value
\mu_{j}
,
j=0,\ldots,M
.
Since NAG-free computes the iterate
x_{t}
and then adjusts
m_{t}
in iteration
t
, this means that
t_{j}+1
is the first iteration in which the estimate
\mu_{j}
takes effect, and
Algorithm

1
computes iterates for
t\in\mathopen{(}t_{j},t_{j+1}\mathclose{]}
using
m_{t}=\mu_{j}
.
For example,
t_{0}=0
and
m_{t}=\mu_{0}=m_{0}
for all
t<t_{1}
.
Therefore, given
t
and
t^{\prime}
such that
t_{j}<t^{\prime}\leq t_{j+1}\leq t_{J}<t\leq t_{J+1}
,

\displaystyle X_{i,t}=\prod_{k=0}^{t-1}G_{i}(\mu_{k})X_{i,0}=G_{i}(\mu_{J})^{t-t_{J}}\Biggl(\prod_{k=j+1}^{J-1}G_{i}(\mu_{k})^{t_{k+1}-t_{k}}\Biggr)G_{i}(\mu_{j})^{t_{j+1}-t^{\prime}}X_{i,t^{\prime}}.

(120)

Now, if
m_{t}>m
, then under
4.4
,
Corollary

B.2
implies that the eigenvalues of
G_{i}(m_{t})
are distinct.
So, letting
\zeta_{i}=\zeta_{i}(m_{t})
and
\xi_{i}=\xi_{i}(m_{t})
denote the eigenvalues of
G_{i}(m_{t})
, we define

\displaystyle T_{i}(m_{t})=\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
\zeta_{i}&\xi_{i}\end{bmatrix}.

(121)

It can be checked that the columns of
T_{i}(m_{t})
are eigenvectors of
G_{i}(m_{t})
, therefore
T_{i}(m_{t})
diagonalizes
G_{i}(m_{t})
:

\displaystyle G_{i}(m_{t})=T_{i}(m_{t})D_{i}(m_{t})T_{i}(m_{t})^{-1}.

(122)

That is,
D_{i}(m_{t})
is a diagonal matrix whose diagonal entries are the eigenvalues of
G_{i}(m_{t})
:

\displaystyle D_{i}(m_{t})=\begin{bmatrix}\zeta_{i}&0\\
0&\xi_{i}\end{bmatrix}.

(123)

Combining
120
,
122
and
123
, then applying
Lemma

B.3
it follows that for every
t_{j}<t\leq t_{j+1}

\displaystyle\|X_{i,t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\overline{C}_{i}\rho(\mu_{j},\lambda_{i})^{2(t-t_{j})}\biggl(\prod_{k=0}^{j-1}\rho(\mu_{k},\lambda_{i})^{2(t_{k+1}-t_{k})}\biggr)x_{i,0}^{2},

(124)

where the constant
\overline{C}_{i}
that is uniformly bounded, since

\displaystyle\|X_{i,t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\biggl\|T_{i}(\mu_{j})D_{i}(\mu_{j})^{t-t_{j}}T_{i}(\mu_{j})^{-1}\Biggl(\prod_{k=0}^{j-1}T_{i}(\mu_{k})D_{i}(\mu_{k})^{t_{k+1}-t_{k}}T_{i}(\mu_{k})^{-1}\Biggr)X_{i,0}\biggr\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|T_{i}(\mu_{j})D_{i}(\mu_{j})^{t-t_{j}}T_{i}(\mu_{j})^{-1}\|^{2}\Biggl(\prod_{k=0}^{j-1}\|T_{i}(\mu_{k})D_{i}(\mu_{k})^{t_{k+1}-t_{k}}T_{i}(\mu_{k})^{-1}\|^{2}\Biggr)x_{i,0}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\Biggl(\prod_{k=0}^{M}\|T_{i}(\mu_{k})\|^{2}\|T_{i}(\mu_{k})^{-1}\|^{2}\Biggr)\|D_{i}(\mu_{j})^{t-t_{j}}\|^{2}\Biggl(\prod_{k=0}^{j-1}\|D_{i}(\mu_{k})^{t_{k+1}-t_{k}}\|^{2}\Biggr)2x_{i,0}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\Biggl(2\prod_{k=0}^{\log_{\gamma}\kappa+1}\|T_{i}(\mu_{k})\|^{2}\|T_{i}(\mu_{k})^{-1}\|^{2}\Biggr)\rho(\mu_{j},\lambda_{i})^{2(t-t_{j})}\Biggl(\prod_{k=0}^{j-1}\rho(\mu_{k},\lambda_{i})^{2(t_{k+1}-t_{k})}\Biggr)x_{i.0}^{2}

and, by applying
Lemmas

B.4
and
B.5
to (
121
), for all
\mu_{k}
we obtain

\displaystyle\|T_{i}(\mu_{k})\|^{2}\leq 4,

\displaystyle\|T_{i}(\mu_{k})^{-1}\|^{2}=\frac{1}{|\zeta_{i}-\xi_{i}|^{2}}\biggl\|\begin{bmatrix}\xi_{i}&-1\\
-\zeta_{i}&1\end{bmatrix}\biggr\|^{2}\leq\frac{4}{\sqrt{\delta_{\lambda}\delta_{L}}},

where
\delta_{L}=(\bar{L}-L)/\bar{L}
and
\delta_{\lambda}
is given by
4.4
.
Furthermore, omitting the
m_{t}
arguments, for
t\in\mathopen{(}t_{j},t_{j+1}\mathclose{]}
, we have that

\displaystyle X_{i,t}

\displaystyle=G_{i}^{t-t_{j}}X_{i,t_{j}}

\displaystyle=T_{i}D_{i}^{t-t_{j}}T_{i}^{-1}X_{i,t_{j}}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
\zeta_{i}&\xi_{i}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\zeta_{i}^{t-t_{j}}&0\\
0&\xi_{i}^{t-t_{j}}\end{bmatrix}\frac{1}{\xi_{i}-\zeta_{i}}\begin{bmatrix}\xi_{i}&-1\\
-\zeta_{i}&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x_{i,t_{j}-1}\\
x_{i,t_{j}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\xi_{i}-\zeta_{i}}\begin{bmatrix}\zeta_{i}^{t-t_{j}}&\xi_{i}^{t-t_{j}}\\
\zeta_{i}^{t+1-t_{j}}&\xi_{i}^{t+1-t_{j}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\xi_{i}&-1\\
-\zeta_{i}&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x_{i,t_{j}-1}\\
x_{i,t_{j}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\xi_{i}-\zeta_{i}}\begin{bmatrix}\xi_{i}\zeta_{i}^{t-t_{j}}-\zeta_{i}\xi_{i}^{t-t_{j}}&\xi_{i}^{t-t_{j}}-\zeta_{i}^{t-t_{j}}\\
\xi_{i}\zeta_{i}^{t+1-t_{j}}-\zeta_{i}\xi_{i}^{t+1-t_{j}}&\xi_{i}^{t+1-t_{j}}-\zeta_{i}^{t+1-t_{j}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x_{i,t_{j}-1}\\
x_{i,t_{j}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\xi_{i}-\zeta_{i}}\begin{bmatrix}(\xi_{i}x_{i,t_{j}-1}-x_{i,t_{j}})\zeta_{i}^{t-t_{j}}+(x_{i,t_{j}}-\zeta_{i}x_{i,t_{j}-1})\xi_{i}^{t-t_{j}}\\
(\xi_{i}x_{i,t_{j}-1}-x_{i,t_{j}})\zeta_{i}^{t+1-t_{j}}+(x_{i,t_{j}}-\zeta_{i}x_{i,t_{j}-1})\xi_{i}^{t+1-t_{j}}\end{bmatrix}.

Therefore,
X_{i,t}
can be decomposed into two modes:

\displaystyle X_{i,t}=A_{i,t_{j}}\zeta_{i}^{t-t_{j}}+B_{i,t_{j}}\xi^{t-t_{j}},

(125)

where
A_{i}
and
B_{i}
are two-dimensional vectors given by

\displaystyle A_{i,t_{j}}=\frac{x_{i,t_{j}}-\xi_{i}x_{i,t_{j}-1}}{\zeta_{i}-\xi_{i}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\zeta_{i}\end{bmatrix}

and

\displaystyle B_{i,t_{j}}=\frac{\zeta_{i}x_{i,t_{j}-1}-x_{i,t_{j}}}{\zeta_{i}-\xi_{i}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\xi_{i}\end{bmatrix},

(126)

which are well-defined, by
Lemma

B.5
.
In particular, for
t_{0}<t\leq t_{1}
, we have that

\displaystyle X_{i,t}=\frac{(1-\xi_{i})x_{i,0}}{\zeta_{i}-\xi_{i}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\zeta_{i}\end{bmatrix}\zeta_{i}^{t}+\frac{(\zeta_{i}-1)x_{i,0}}{\zeta_{i}-\xi_{i}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\xi_{i}\end{bmatrix}\xi_{i}^{t}.

In turn, if without loss of generality we assume
x_{1,0}>0
, then

\displaystyle x_{1,t}-x_{1,t-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-\xi_{1})(\zeta_{1}-1)\zeta_{1}^{t}x_{1,0}+(\zeta_{1}-1)(\xi_{1}-1)\xi_{1}^{t}x_{1,0}}{\zeta_{1}-\xi_{1}}\leq\kappa^{-1}\zeta_{1}^{t-1}x_{1,0}<0,

where in first inequality above we used the fact that
0<\xi_{1}<\zeta_{1}
and the identity

\displaystyle(1-\zeta_{i})(1-\xi_{i})=\biggl(1-\frac{1+\beta}{2}\Bigl(1-\frac{\lambda_{i}}{L}\Bigr)\biggr)^{2}-\frac{(1+\beta)^{2}}{4}\Bigl(1-\frac{\lambda_{i}}{L}\Bigr)^{2}+\beta\Bigl(1-\frac{\lambda_{i}}{L}\Bigr)=\frac{\lambda_{i}}{L}.

Moreover, for
t_{0}<t\leq t_{1}
we also have that

\displaystyle x_{1,t}-\xi_{1}x_{1,t-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-\xi_{1})(\zeta_{1}-\xi_{1})\zeta_{1}^{t}x_{1,0}+(\zeta_{1}-1)(\xi_{1}-\xi_{1})\xi_{1}^{t}x_{1,0}}{\zeta_{1}-\xi_{1}}=(1-\xi_{1})\zeta_{1}^{t}x_{1,0}<0,

\displaystyle\zeta_{1}x_{1,t-1}-x_{1,t}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-\xi_{1})(\zeta_{1}-\zeta_{1})\zeta_{1}^{t}x_{1,0}+(\zeta_{1}-1)(\zeta_{1}-\xi_{1})\xi_{1}^{t}x_{1,0}}{\zeta_{1}-\xi_{1}}=(\zeta_{1}-1)\xi_{1}^{t}x_{1,0}>0.

It follows that, for
t_{1}<t\leq t_{2}

\displaystyle x_{1,t}-x_{1,t-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{(x_{1,t_{j}}-\xi_{1}x_{1,t_{1}-1})(\zeta_{1}-1)\zeta_{1}^{t-t_{1}}+(\zeta_{1}x_{1,t_{1}-1}-x_{1,t_{1}})(\xi_{1}-1)\xi_{1}^{t-t_{1}}}{\zeta_{1}-\xi_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\zeta_{1}^{t-t_{1}}\frac{(x_{1,t_{j}}-\xi_{1}x_{1,t_{1}-1})(\zeta_{1}-1)+(\zeta_{1}x_{1,t_{1}-1}-x_{1,t_{1}})(\xi_{1}-1)}{\zeta_{1}-\xi_{1}}

\displaystyle=\zeta_{1}^{t-t_{1}}(x_{1,t_{1}}-x_{1,t_{1}-1})

\displaystyle\leq\kappa^{-1}\zeta_{1}(\mu_{1})^{t-t_{1}}\zeta_{1}(\mu_{0})^{t_{1}-1}x_{1,0}

\displaystyle<0,

since
0<\xi_{1}(m_{1})<\zeta_{1}(m_{1})
, and moreover

\displaystyle x_{1,t}-\xi_{1}x_{1,t-1}

\displaystyle=\zeta_{1}^{t-t_{1}}(x_{1,t_{1}}-\xi_{1}x_{1,t_{1}-1})=\zeta_{1}(\mu_{1})^{t-t_{1}}(1-\xi_{1}(\mu_{0}))\zeta_{1}(\mu_{0})^{t}x_{1,0}<0,

\displaystyle\zeta_{1}x_{1,t-1}-x_{1,t}

\displaystyle=\xi_{1}^{t-t_{1}}(\zeta_{1}x_{1,t_{1}-1}-x_{1,t_{1}})=\xi_{1}(\mu_{1})^{t-t_{1}}(\zeta_{1}(\mu_{0})-1)\xi_{1}(\mu_{0})^{t}x_{1,0}>0.

Therefore, using the fact that
\zeta_{1}(m_{t})=\rho(m_{t},m)
, it follows by induction that for
t_{j}<t\leq t_{j+1}

\displaystyle(x_{1,t+1}-x_{1,t})^{2}\geq\underline{C}_{1}\rho(\mu_{j},m)^{2t-t_{j}}\Biggl(\prod_{k=0}^{j-1}\rho(\mu_{k},m)^{2(t_{k+1}-t_{k})}\Biggr)x_{1,0}^{2}\geq 0,

(127)

for some
\underline{C}_{1}\geq\kappa^{-2}
.

B.1.4
The dynamics of
c_{t}

Lemma

B.7
bounds the suboptimality factor in the convergence rate of
x_{t}
when
m_{t}\in\mathopen{[}m,(1+\delta)m\mathclose{]}
, for a given
\delta>0
.
Now, we determine how long
m_{t}
takes to reach the interval
\mathopen{[}m,(1+\delta)m\mathclose{]}
.
Our starting point is to determine the dynamics of
c_{t+1}
.
To this end, we plug (
98
) and (
100
) into (
4
), obtaining
4
4

4

Note that
x_{t+1}-x_{t}=(x_{t+1}-x^{\star})-(x_{t}-x^{\star})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})v_{i}
.

\displaystyle c^{2}_{t+1}=\left\|\frac{\nabla f(x_{t+1})-\nabla f(x_{t})}{x_{t+1}-x_{t}}\right\|^{2}=\left\|\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})\lambda_{i}v_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})v_{i}}\right\|^{2}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}\lambda_{i}^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}.

(128)

The identity (
128
) reveals that
c^{2}_{t+1}
can be expressed as an average of the squared eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}^{2}
weighted by
(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}
.
Since the weights are a static map of
x_{i,t}
, the dynamics of
x_{i,t}
determine the dynamics of the estimated effective curvature
c_{t+1}
.
In particular,
x_{i,t}
determine if one weight can outweigh the others, in which case
c_{t+1}
tends to
\lambda_{i}
.

By
Lemma

B.3
,
\rho(s,\lambda_{i})<\rho(s,m)
for all
\lambda_{i}\in\mathopen{(}m,L\mathclose{]}
.
Hence, from (
124
) and (
127
), we conclude that the weight associated with
m
eventually dominates the other weights, so that
c_{t+1}
converges to
m
.
In the following, we show that this happens at an accelerated rate.
To this end, we define
5
5

5

Note that
\rho<0
cannot occur by the definition of
\rho
, (
106
).

\phi:\mathcal{D}\to\mathopen{[}0,1\mathclose{]}
as

\displaystyle\phi(s,a,b)=\begin{cases}\min\left\{1,\dfrac{\rho(s,a)}{\rho(s,b)}\right\},&\rho(s,b)>0,\\
1,&\rho(s,b)=0,\end{cases}

(129)

where the domain
\mathcal{D}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}=\mathopen{(}0,L\mathclose{]}\times\left\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{>0}:a\neq b\right\},

(130)

\mathbb{R}_{>0}
being the set of positive real numbers.
With
\phi
, we can bound how fast
c_{t+1}
takes to decrease below
(1+\delta)\ell
for a given
\ell\in\mathopen{[}m,L\mathclose{]}
, not necessarily an eigenvalue of
H
, where
\delta>0
represents some estimate precision relative to
\ell
.
To this end, we characterize
\phi((1+\delta)\ell,\ell,m)^{2}
, first showing that it is decreasing in
\ell
.

Lemma B.9
.

If
\delta\in\mathopen{(}0,1\mathclose{]}
and
\kappa\geq 2
, then
\phi((1+\delta)\ell,\ell,m)
is decreasing in
\ell\geq m>0
.

Proof.

Let
L>m>0
.
Given
\ell
and
\delta>0
such that
m\leq\ell<(1+\delta)\ell\leq L
, by (
129
) and
Lemma

B.1
, we have that

\displaystyle\phi((1+\delta)\ell,\ell,m)=

\displaystyle\frac{L-\ell+\sqrt{(L-\ell)\delta\ell}}{L-m+\sqrt{(L-m)((1+\delta)\ell-m)}}.

Letting
\phi_{\ell}
the derivative of
\phi((1+\delta)\ell,\ell,m)
with respect to
\ell
, we obtain

\displaystyle\phi_{\ell}=

\displaystyle\frac{-(L-m)\delta^{2}\ell-(L-m)\sqrt{(L-\ell)\delta\ell}(L+\ell+\sqrt{(L-m)((1+\delta)\ell-m)}-2m)}{2\sqrt{(L-\ell)\delta\ell}\sqrt{(L-m)((1+\delta)\ell-m)}(L+\sqrt{(L-m)((1+\delta)\ell-m)}-m)^{2}}

\displaystyle-\frac{(L-m)\delta(\ell^{2}+\ell(\sqrt{(L-\ell)\delta\ell}+2\sqrt{(L-m)((1+\delta)\ell-m)}-2m))}{2\sqrt{(L-\ell)\delta\ell}\sqrt{(L-m)((1+\delta)\ell-m)}(L+\sqrt{(L-m)((1+\delta)\ell-m)}-m)^{2}}

\displaystyle-\frac{(L-m)\delta L(\sqrt{(L-\ell)\delta\ell}-\sqrt{(L-m)((1+\delta)\ell-m)}+m)}{2\sqrt{(L-\ell)\delta\ell}\sqrt{(L-m)((1+\delta)\ell-m)}(L+\sqrt{(L-m)((1+\delta)\ell-m)}-m)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle-\frac{(L-m)((L-m)\sqrt{(L-\ell)\delta\ell}-\delta(L-2\ell)\sqrt{(L-m)((1+\delta)\ell-m)})}{2\sqrt{(L-\ell)\delta\ell}\sqrt{(L-m)((1+\delta)\ell-m)}(L+\sqrt{(L-m)((1+\delta)\ell-m)}-m)^{2}}.

So, to show
\phi((1+\delta)\ell,\ell,m)
is decreasing in
\ell
, it suffices to show the numerator above is positive.
To this end, since
L>m
, it suffices to show that the second factor is positive:

\displaystyle(L-m)\sqrt{(L-\ell)\delta\ell}+\sqrt{(L-\ell)\delta\ell}\sqrt{(L-m)((1+\delta)\ell-m)}

\displaystyle-\delta(L-2\ell)\sqrt{(L-m)((1+\delta)\ell-m)}>0.

(131)

The negative term on the left-hand side above is maximized at the critical point characterized by

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\ell}(L-2\ell)\sqrt{((1+\delta)\ell-m)}

\displaystyle=-2\sqrt{(1+\delta)\ell-m}+\frac{(L-2\ell)(1+\delta)}{2\sqrt{(1+\delta)\ell-m}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+\delta)(L-2\ell)-4((1+\delta)\ell-m)}{2\sqrt{(1+\delta)\ell-m}}

\displaystyle=0.

Taking
\ell
at this critical point,
\ell=\frac{1}{6}L+\frac{2}{3(1+\delta)}m
, and using the assumptions that
\kappa\geq 2
and
\delta\leq 1
, it follows that

\displaystyle(L-\ell)\ell\geq\frac{5L-4m}{6}\frac{(1+\delta)L+4m}{6(1+\delta)}=\frac{5(1+\delta)L^{2}+4(5-(1+\delta))Lm-16m^{2}}{36(1+\delta)}\geq\frac{5}{36}L^{2}.

Hence, plugging
\ell=\frac{1}{6}L+\frac{2}{3(1+\delta)}m
back into (
131
) and using the assumptions that
\delta\leq 1
and
\kappa\geq 2
yields

\displaystyle(\delta(L-2\ell)-\sqrt{(L-\ell)\delta\ell})\sqrt{(L-m)((1+\delta)\ell-m)}

\displaystyle\leq\delta\frac{(4-\sqrt{5})L}{6}\sqrt{(L-m)\frac{1+\delta}{6}\biggl(L-\frac{2m}{1+\delta}\biggr)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\delta\sqrt{1+\delta}}{6\sqrt{6}}L(L-m),

and, similarly

\displaystyle\sqrt{(L-\ell)\delta\ell}(L-m)\geq\sqrt{\delta\frac{5L-4m}{6}\frac{L}{6}}(L-m)\geq\frac{\sqrt{\delta}}{3\sqrt{2}}L(L-m).

Hence, canceling the common factor
\sqrt{\delta}L(L-m)
above and then rearranging, we conclude that (
131
) holds if

\displaystyle\sqrt{\delta}\sqrt{1+\delta}\leq\sqrt{3},

which is true since
\sqrt{\delta}\leq 1
.
∎

In fact,
\phi((1+\delta)\ell,\ell,m)
is decreasing for any
\delta>0
, which can be seen in its graph, but the case where
\delta\in\mathopen{(}0,1\mathclose{]}
suffices for the upcoming results.
Namely, given
\delta_{\ell}>0
and
\delta_{u}\in\mathopen{(}0,1\mathclose{]}
, by
Lemma

B.9
we have that for every
\ell\in\mathopen{[}(1+\delta_{\ell})m,L\mathclose{]}

\displaystyle\phi((1+\delta_{u})\ell,\ell,m)=

\displaystyle\frac{L-\ell+\sqrt{(L-\ell)\delta_{u}\ell}}{L-m+\sqrt{(L-m)((1+\delta_{u})\ell-m)}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{L-(1+\delta_{\ell})m+\sqrt{(L-(1+\delta_{\ell})m)\delta_{u}(1+\delta_{\ell})m}}{L-m+\sqrt{(L-m)((1+\delta_{u})(1+\delta_{\ell})m-m)}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa-(1+\delta_{\ell})+\sqrt{(\kappa-(1+\delta_{\ell}))\delta_{u}(1+\delta_{\ell})}}{\kappa-1+\sqrt{(\kappa-1)(\delta_{u}+\delta_{\ell}+\delta_{u}\delta_{\ell})}}

\displaystyle\eqqcolon

\displaystyle r_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa).

(132)

Hence, to show
\phi((1+\delta_{u})\ell,\ell,m)^{2}
is an accelerated rate, suffices to show that
r_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)^{2}
is an accelerated rate for appropriate
\kappa,\delta_{u}
and
\delta_{\ell}
, which we do in the next result.
The function
r_{\phi}
is well-defined for
\delta_{\ell}>0
,
\delta_{u}>0
and
k\geq 1+\delta_{\ell}
and, by simple inspection, it follows that
r_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)\in\mathopen{(}0,1\mathclose{)}
.

Lemma B.10
.

Given
\delta_{u}>0
,
\delta_{\ell}>0
and
\kappa\geq 1+\delta_{\ell}
, there is a
\sigma_{\phi}=\sigma_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)
such that

\displaystyle r_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)^{2}\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa).

Moreover, the function
\kappa\mapsto\sigma_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)
is bounded and satisfies

\displaystyle\lim_{\kappa\to+\infty}\sigma_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)=\frac{1}{4(\sqrt{\delta_{u}+\delta_{\ell}+\delta_{u}\delta_{\ell}}-\sqrt{\delta_{u}(1+\delta_{\ell})})^{2}}.

Proof.

Let
\delta_{u}>0
,
\delta_{\ell}>0
and
\kappa\geq 1+\delta_{\ell}
.
By direct algebraic manipulation, we obtain

\displaystyle r_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)^{2}\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)=\frac{\sqrt{\sigma_{\phi}\kappa}-1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{\phi}\kappa}}\iff\frac{1}{(1-r_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)^{2})^{2}\kappa}\leq\sigma_{\phi}.

(133)

For such
\delta_{u}
,
\delta_{\ell}
and
\kappa
, we have
r_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)\in\mathopen{(}0,1\mathclose{)}
, so that
1-r_{\phi}^{2}>0
.
Therefore, the lower bound of the inequality on the right-hand side of (
133
) is well-defined.
So, let
\sigma_{\phi}
be defined such that (
133
) holds with equality:

\displaystyle\sigma_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)=\frac{1}{(1-r_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)^{2})^{2}\kappa}.

For fixed
\delta_{u}>0
and
\delta_{\ell}>0
, the map
r_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)
is continuous in
\kappa>1+\delta_{\ell}
and right-continuous at
\kappa=1+\delta_{\ell}
, hence so is
(1-r_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)^{2})\sqrt{\kappa}
.
Moreover,
(1-r_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)^{2})\sqrt{\kappa}>0
for
\kappa\geq 1+\delta_{\ell}
.
Therefore,
1/((1-r_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)^{2})^{2}\kappa)
is continuous in
\kappa>1+\delta_{\ell}
and right-continuous at
\kappa=1+\delta_{\ell}
.
Furthermore,
\lim_{\kappa\to+\infty}1+r_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)=2
and

\displaystyle\lim_{\kappa\to+\infty}(1-r_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa))\sqrt{\kappa}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\kappa\to+\infty}\frac{\delta_{\ell}\sqrt{\kappa}+\sqrt{\kappa(\kappa-1)\delta_{s}}-\sqrt{\kappa(\kappa-(1+\delta_{\ell}))\delta_{u}(1+\delta_{\ell})}}{\kappa-1+\sqrt{(\kappa-1)\delta_{s}}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\delta_{s}}-\sqrt{\delta_{u}(1+\delta_{\ell})},

where
\delta_{s}=\delta_{\ell}+\delta_{u}+\delta_{u}\delta_{\ell}
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{\kappa\to+\infty}\sigma_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)=\lim_{\kappa\to+\infty}\frac{1}{((1-r_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)^{2})^{2}\kappa}=\frac{1}{4(\sqrt{\delta_{s}}-\sqrt{\delta_{u}(1+\delta_{\ell})})^{2}}.

Hence,
\kappa\mapsto\sigma_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)
attains a maximum on
\mathopen{[}1+\delta_{\ell},\infty\mathclose{)}
and is bounded.

∎

Figure

6
shows a plot of the map
\kappa\mapsto 1/((1-r_{\phi}(\kappa)^{2})^{2}\kappa)
for
\kappa=10,\ldots,10^{9}
and the asymptotic value of
\sigma_{\phi}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\kappa\to+\infty}\sigma_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)=\frac{1}{4(\sqrt{\delta_{s}}-\sqrt{\delta_{u}(1+\delta_{\ell})})^{2}}\approx 2.31,

for
\delta_{u}=0.01
and
\delta_{\ell}=0.18
.
We see that the asymptotic value of
\sigma_{\phi}
is slightly less than the peak value of
\sigma_{\phi}
, but the first still provides a good approximation to the second.

Figure 6
:
Numerical (black solid line) lower bound on and asymptotic value (dashed red line) of
\sigma_{\phi}
such that
r_{\phi}^{2}\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)
holds for all
\kappa\geq 1+1/\delta_{s}
, with
\delta_{u}=0.01
and
\delta_{\ell}=0.18
.

Building upon the two lemmas above, we now establish that
\phi((1+\delta)\ell,\ell,m)
is actually much faster than
r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)
for most values of
\ell
.

Lemma B.11
.

Given
\delta_{u}\in\mathopen{(}0,1\mathclose{]}
,
\delta_{\ell}\in\mathopen{(}0,1\mathclose{]}
and
\kappa\geq 2
, there exist
\sigma_{\phi}=\sigma_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)>0
and
\alpha_{\phi}=\alpha_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},m)>0
such that for all
\ell\in\mathopen{[}(1+\delta_{\ell})m,L/(1+\delta_{u})\mathclose{]}

\displaystyle\phi((1+\delta_{u})\ell,\ell,m)^{2}\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)^{1+\alpha_{\phi}(\ell-(1+\delta_{\ell})m)},

(134)

where the function
\kappa\mapsto\sigma_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)
is bounded and satisfies

\displaystyle\lim_{\kappa\to+\infty}\sigma_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)=\frac{1}{4(\sqrt{\delta_{u}+\delta_{\ell}+\delta_{u}\delta_{\ell}}-\sqrt{\delta_{u}(1+\delta_{\ell})})^{2}}.

Proof.

Combining
Lemmas

B.9
and
B.10
, we have that

\displaystyle\phi((1+\delta_{u})\ell,\ell,m)^{2}\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)

for all
\ell\in\mathopen{[}(1+\delta_{\ell})m,L/(1+\delta_{u})\mathclose{]}
.
Moreover,
\phi((1+\delta_{u})\ell,\ell,m)
is decreasing and continuously differentiable with respect to
\ell
.
So, consider the maximum slope of
\phi((1+\delta_{u}\ell,\ell,m))
over the interval
\mathopen{[}(1+\delta_{\ell})m,L/(1+\delta_{u})\mathclose{]}
:

\displaystyle s=\max_{\ell\in\mathopen{[}(1+\delta_{\ell})m,L/(1+\delta_{u})\mathclose{]}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\ell}\phi((1+\delta_{u})\ell,\ell,m)<0.

Then, it follows that for all
\mathopen{[}(1+\delta_{\ell})m,L/(1+\delta_{u})\mathclose{]}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\ell}\phi((1+\delta_{u}\ell,\ell,m))\leq s\leq a\sqrt{r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi})}\leq a\phi((1+\delta_{u}\ell,\ell,m))<0,

where
a=s/r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)<0
.
Hence, Grönwall’s inequality implies that

\displaystyle\phi((1+\delta_{u}\ell,\ell,m))^{2}\leq\exp(2a(\ell-(1+\delta_{\ell})m))r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)=r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)^{1+\alpha_{\phi}(\ell-(1+\delta_{\ell})m)},

(135)

where, since
a<0
and
\log_{r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)}e<0
,
\alpha_{\phi}
is a positive constant given by

\displaystyle\alpha_{\phi}=2a\log_{r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)}e>0,

which proves the claim.
∎

Figure

7
illustrates
Lemma

B.11
numerically with
L=10,m=1,\delta_{u}=1,\sigma_{\phi}=4
and
\alpha_{\phi}=10
.
We see that
\phi((1+\delta_{u})\ell,\ell,m)^{2}
becomes significantly smaller than
r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)
as
\ell
approaches
L
.
In fact,
\phi(L,L,m)=0
, therefore the estimate adjustments take place extremely fast when the estimate is large and gradually slow down as the estimate improves, but always at an accelerated rate.
As we now show, this implies drastic estimate convergence speed-up.

Figure 7
:
Numerical illustration of
Lemma

B.11
with
L=10,m=1,\delta_{\ell}=1,\sigma_{\phi}=4
and
\alpha_{\phi}=10
.

Lemma B.12
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
be a quadratic function, let
\bar{L}>L
, and suppose that
4.2
and
4.5
hold.
Also, let
\delta_{m}
and
\delta_{u}
be positive numbers such that
\delta_{m}>\delta_{u}>0
and let
r^{\prime}=r^{\prime}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)
be a function such that
r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)\leq r^{\prime}<1
for all
\kappa\geq 1+\delta_{\ell}
, where
\delta_{\ell}=((1+\delta_{m})/(1+\delta_{u}))-1>0
and
\sigma_{\phi}=\sigma_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)
is given by
Lemma

B.10
.
Then, the estimates
m_{t}
of
Algorithm

1
reach
[m/\gamma,(1+\delta_{m})m]
after no more than
\tau
iterations, where

\displaystyle\tau=\frac{-\log(4\kappa^{2}M_{1}\omega/\delta_{u})}{\log r^{\prime}}\sum_{j=0}^{\log_{\gamma}(\kappa/(1+\delta_{m}))}\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{\phi}m(1+\delta_{\ell})(\gamma^{j}-1)},

(136)

M_{1}=\max_{i}\overline{C}_{i}/\underline{C}_{1}
, with
\omega
and
\gamma
given by
4.2
and
4.5
.

Proof.

Suppose that the last value of
m_{t}
before reaching the interval
[m/\gamma,(1+\delta_{m})m)
is
(1+\delta_{m})m
.
Then, suppose that the value before last is
\gamma(1+\delta_{m})m
, and so on, up to
\gamma^{K}(1+\delta_{m})m
for some
K
such that
\gamma^{K}(1+\delta_{m})m\leq L<\gamma^{K+1}(1+\delta_{m})m
.
Using this
m_{t}
schedule, we bound the number of iterations that
m_{t}
takes to reach the interval
[m/\gamma,(1+\delta_{m})m]
, and then we argue that no other
m_{t}
schedule can lead to a worse bound.

Let
\ell_{j}=\gamma^{j}(1+\delta_{m})m/(1+\delta_{u})
.
Then, we have that
\ell_{j}\geq(1+\delta_{\ell})m
for
\delta_{\ell}=((1+\delta_{m})/(1+\delta_{u}))-1
.
Since
\delta_{m}>\delta_{u}
, then
\delta_{\ell}>0
, and
Lemma

B.11
applies.
Now, let
I_{j}=\min\left\{i:\lambda_{i}\geq\ell_{j}\right\}
.
That is,
\lambda_{i}\geq\ell_{j}
if and only if
i\geq I_{j}
.
Then, using this fact and separating the terms indexed by
i<I_{0}
from those indexed by
i\geq I_{0}
in (
128
) into two sums yields

\displaystyle c^{2}_{t+1}<\ell_{0}^{2}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{I_{0}-1}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}+\frac{\sum_{i=I_{0}}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{2}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}.

In turn, plugging the above inequality into the identity
(c_{t+1}+\ell_{0})(c_{t+1}-\ell_{0})=c^{2}_{t+1}-\ell_{0}^{2}
, and then using the fact that
\lambda_{i}\leq L
and
\ell_{0}\geq m
, we obtain

\displaystyle c_{t+1}-\ell_{0}=\frac{c^{2}_{t+1}-\ell_{0}^{2}}{c_{t+1}+\ell_{0}}<\frac{\sum_{i=I_{0}}^{d}(\lambda_{i}^{2}-\ell_{0}^{2})(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}{\ell_{0}\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}\leq\ell_{0}\kappa^{2}\sum_{i=I_{0}}^{d}\frac{(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}{(x_{1,t+1}-x_{1,t})^{2}}.

(137)

Moreover, using (
124
), we have that

\displaystyle(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}=(\begin{bmatrix}-1&1\end{bmatrix}X_{i,t+1})^{2}\leq 2\|X_{i,t+1}\|^{2}\leq 2\overline{C}_{i}\rho(\mu_{0},\lambda_{i})^{2t}x_{i,0}^{2}.

(138)

To address the terms in the sum in (
137
), we combine (
138
) and (
127
), assuming
t_{K}\leq t<t_{K+1}
.
That is, we consider the last adjustment before
m_{t}
reaches the interval
[m/\gamma,(1+\delta_{m})m)
.
Then, we apply
Lemma

B.3
twice, to get
\rho(m_{k},\lambda_{i})\leq\rho(m_{k},\ell_{0})
and
\rho(m_{k},\ell_{0})<\rho(m_{k},m)
for all
i\geq I_{0}
, which gives

\displaystyle\sum_{i=I_{0}}^{d}\frac{(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}{(x_{1,t+1}-x_{1,t})^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2M_{1}\sum_{i=I_{0}}^{d}\frac{x_{i,0}^{2}}{x_{1,0}^{2}}\prod_{k=t_{K}}^{t}\frac{\rho(m_{k},\lambda_{i})^{2}}{\rho(m_{k},m)^{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{\tau_{K}}\frac{\rho(m_{k},\lambda_{i})^{2}}{\rho(m_{k},m)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2M_{1}\omega\phi((1+\delta_{u})\ell_{0},\ell_{0},m)^{2(t-t_{K}+1)}.

(139)

where
M_{1}=2\max_{i}\overline{C}_{i}/\underline{C}_{1}
.
Next, we put (
137
) and (
139
) together, and since
\ell_{0}\geq m
, we get

\displaystyle c_{t+1}-\ell_{0}<2\kappa^{2}M_{1}\omega\phi((1+\delta_{m})m,\ell_{0},m)^{2(t-t_{K}+1)}\ell_{0}\leq\delta_{u}\ell_{0}/2,

for all
t\geq t_{K}+\Delta t_{0}
, where

\displaystyle\Delta t_{0}=-\frac{\log(4\kappa^{2}M_{1}\omega/\delta_{u})}{\log r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)}.

Therefore,
c_{t+1}<(1+\delta_{u})\ell_{0}=(1+\delta_{m})m
for
t\geq t_{K}+\Delta t_{0}
or, equivalently,

\displaystyle t_{K+1}-t_{K}\leq\Delta t_{0}.

Note that for every
m_{t}
schedule, if
\mu_{K}
denotes the last value of
m_{t}
before reaching
[m/\gamma,(1+\delta_{m})m]
, then
\mu_{K}\geq(1+\delta_{m})m
, by definition.
Hence, letting
\ell_{0}^{\prime}=\mu_{K}/(1+\delta_{u})
and
I_{0}^{\prime}=\{i:\lambda_{i}\geq\ell_{0}^{\prime}\}
, then
I_{0}^{\prime}\geq I_{0}
, and it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=I_{0}^{\prime}}^{d}\frac{(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}{(x_{1,t+1}-x_{1,t})^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2M_{1}\sum_{i=I_{0}^{\prime}}^{d}\frac{x_{i,0}^{2}}{x_{1,0}^{2}}\prod_{k=t_{K}}^{t}\frac{\rho(m_{k},\lambda_{i})^{2}}{\rho(m_{k},m)^{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{\tau_{K}}\frac{\rho(m_{k},\lambda_{i})^{2}}{\rho(m_{k},m)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2M_{1}\sum_{i=I_{0}}^{d}\frac{x_{i,0}^{2}}{x_{1,0}^{2}}\prod_{k=t_{K}}^{t}\frac{\rho(m_{k},\lambda_{i})^{2}}{\rho(m_{k},m)^{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{\tau_{K}}\frac{\rho(m_{k},\lambda_{i})^{2}}{\rho(m_{k},m)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2M_{1}\omega\phi((1+\delta_{u})\ell_{0},\ell_{0},m)^{2(t-t_{K}+1)}.

Therefore, the last adjustment cannot take more than
\Delta t_{0}
iterations for any
m_{t}
schedule.

Then, let
\Delta t_{j}
be quantities analogous to
\Delta t_{0}
, defined for
j=0,\ldots,K
as

\displaystyle\Delta t_{j}=-\frac{\log(4\kappa^{2}M_{1}\omega/\delta_{u})}{(1+\alpha_{\phi}(\ell_{j}-(1+\delta_{\ell})m))\log r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)}.

By
4.2
,
m_{t}
decreases by a factor of at least
\gamma
every time it is adjusted to a new value.
Hence,
\mu_{K-1}\geq\gamma\mu_{K}
for every
m_{t}
schedule, which implies that
\ell_{1}\leq\mu_{K-1}/(1+\delta_{u})
for every
m_{t}
schedule.
Hence, by the same rationale above, it cannot take more than
\Delta t_{1}
for
m_{t}
to be adjusted to its second last value before reaching the interval
[m/\gamma,(1+\delta_{m})m]
.
It follows by induction that it cannot take more than
\Delta t_{j}
for
m_{t}
to be adjust to its
K-j
-th to last value before reaching the interval
[m/\gamma,(1+\delta_{m})m]
.
Moreover, since by design
m_{t}\leq L
, it cannot more than
K\leq\log_{\gamma}(\kappa/(1+\delta_{m}))
adjustments before
m_{t}
reaches the interval
[m/\gamma,(1+\delta_{m})m]
.
Therefore, we conclude that
m_{t+1}\leq(1+\delta_{m})m
for all
t\geq\tau
, where

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{-\log(4\kappa^{2}M_{1}\omega/\delta_{u})}{\log r^{\prime}}\sum_{j=0}^{\log_{\gamma}(\kappa/(1+\delta_{m}))}\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{\phi}m(1+\delta_{\ell})(\gamma^{j}-1)}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{-\log(4\kappa^{2}M_{1}\omega/\delta_{u})}{\log r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)}\sum_{j=0}^{K}\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{\phi}m(1+\delta_{\ell})(\gamma^{j}-1)},

because
\log
is monotone and
r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)\leq r^{\prime}<1
, and
K\leq\log_{\gamma}(\kappa/(1+\delta_{m}))
.
∎

B.1.5
Main result in the quadratic case

We now prove the main local convergence result for NAG-free when the objective function is quadratic.
There is no difference between local and global convergence in this case, but it will be the foundation to derive the main local convergence in the general case later.
To this end, we first establish that for every
G_{i}(m_{t})
, there is a quadratic Lyapunov function certifying convergence of
X_{i,t}
at rate
\rho(m_{t},\lambda_{i})
up to arbitrary precision, at the expense of worse condition numbers.

Lemma B.13
.

Let
m_{t}\in\mathopen{[}m/\gamma,L\mathclose{]}
for some
\gamma>1
, and let
\rho(G_{i}(m_{t}))
denote the spectral radius of
G_{i}(m_{t})
.
Then, given
r\in\mathopen{[}\rho(G_{i}(m_{t})),1\mathclose{)}
and
\delta>0
such that
(1+\delta)r<1
, there is some
P=P(G_{i}(m_{t}),r,\delta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
such that
G_{i}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{T}}PG_{i}(m_{t})\prec(1+\delta)^{2}r^{2}P
and
P\succeq I
.
Moreover, letting
\lambda_{\min}(P)
and
\lambda_{\max}(P)
denote the least and the greatest eigenvalues of
P
, then

\displaystyle\max_{m_{t}\in\mathopen{[}m/\gamma,L\mathclose{]}}\|P(G_{i}(m_{t}),r,\delta)\|<\ \frac{1+(1+\delta)^{-2}}{1-(1+\delta)^{-2}}+\frac{2M_{2}^{2}}{(1+\delta)^{2}r^{2}}\frac{1+(1+\delta)^{-2}}{(1-(1+\delta)^{-2})^{3}},

(140)

where
M_{2}
is an appropriate constant that does not depend on neither
\delta
nor
r
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

B.4
,
\rho(m_{t},\lambda_{i})<1
for all
m_{t}\in\mathopen{(}0,L\mathclose{]}
and
i=1,\ldots,d
.
Thus,
\rho(G_{i}(m_{t}))<1
, where
\rho(G_{i}(m_{t}))
denotes the spectral radius of
G_{i}(m_{t})
.
Therefore, the interval
\mathopen{[}\rho(G_{i}(m_{t})),1\mathclose{)}
is nonempty.
So, let
r
and
\delta
be two positive numbers such that
r\in\mathopen{[}\rho(G_{i}(m_{t})),1\mathclose{)}
and
(1+\delta)r<1
.
Then, take
\nu=(1+\delta)r
and
P=\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}(G_{i}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{T}}/\nu)^{k}(G_{i}(m_{t})/\nu)^{k}
.
The matrix
P
is well-defined because
\rho(G_{i}(m_{t})/\nu)\leq 1/(1+\delta)<1
, and
P\succeq I
, by construction.
Moreover, it satisfies

\displaystyle(G_{i}(m_{t}))/\nu)^{\mathsf{T}}P(G_{i}(m_{t})/\nu)=\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}(G_{i}(m_{t}))^{\mathsf{T}}/\nu)^{k}(G_{i}(m_{t}))/\nu)^{k}=P-I.

Therefore,
G_{i}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{T}}PG_{i}(m_{t})\prec(1+\delta)^{2}r^{2}P
, which proves the first claim.

To prove the second claim, we first express
G_{i}(m_{t})
in Schur form
[
Golub and Van Loan 2013
, 7.1.3]
.
To this end, we construct a two-by-two orthogonal matrix
Q_{i}(m_{t})
, whose first column is a unit eigenvector
q_{i,1}
associated with
\zeta_{i}=\zeta_{i}(m_{t})
, the top eigenvalue of
G_{i}(m_{t})
, as in

\displaystyle q_{i,1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|\zeta_{i}|^{2}}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\zeta_{i}\end{bmatrix}.

To determine the second column of
Q_{i}(m_{t})
, we apply the Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization procedure
[
Golub and Van Loan 2013
, 5.2.7]
to obtain from
e_{1}
a vector orthonormal to
q_{i,1}
:

\displaystyle e_{1}-\frac{\langle e_{1},q_{i,1}\rangle}{\langle q_{i,1},q_{i,1}\rangle}q_{i,1}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}-\frac{1}{1+|\zeta_{i}|^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\zeta_{i}\end{bmatrix}=\frac{1}{1+|\zeta_{i}|^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}|\zeta_{i}|^{2}\\
-\zeta_{i}\end{bmatrix}.

Normalizing the vector above, we obtain

\displaystyle q_{i,2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|\zeta_{i}|^{2}}}\frac{1}{|\zeta_{i}|}\begin{bmatrix}|\zeta_{i}|^{2}\\
-\zeta_{i}\end{bmatrix}.

So, letting
Q_{i}(m_{t})
be the orthogonal matrix given by

\displaystyle Q_{i}(m_{t})=\begin{bmatrix}q_{i,1}&q_{i,2}\end{bmatrix},

(141)

and letting
T_{i}(m_{t})
be the matrix given by

\displaystyle T_{i}(m_{t})=Q_{i}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{H}}G_{i}(m_{t})Q_{i}(m_{t}),

(142)

where
Q_{i}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{H}}
denotes the conjugate-transpose of
Q_{i}(m_{t})
, it follows that

\displaystyle T_{i}(m_{t})

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}q_{i,1}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{H}}G_{i}(m_{t})q_{i,1}(m_{t})&q_{i,1}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{H}}G_{i}(m_{t})q_{i,2}(m_{t})\\
q_{i,2}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{H}}G_{i}(m_{t})q_{i,1}(m_{t})&q_{i,2}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{H}}G_{i}(m_{t})q_{i,2}(m_{t})\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\zeta_{i}&q_{i,1}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{H}}G_{i}(m_{t})q_{i,2}(m_{t})\\
\zeta_{i}q_{i,2}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{H}}q_{i,1}(m_{t})&q_{i,2}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{H}}G_{i}(m_{t})q_{i,2}(m_{t})\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\zeta_{i}&q_{i,1}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{H}}G_{i}(m_{t})q_{i,2}(m_{t})\\
0&q_{i,2}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{H}}G_{i}(m_{t})q_{i,2}(m_{t})\end{bmatrix}

because
q_{i,1}(m_{t})
is a unit eigenvector of
G_{i}(m_{t})
associated with
\zeta_{i}
, and is orthogonal to
q_{i,2}(m_{t})
.
Moreover, the product
Q_{i}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{H}}G_{i}(m_{t})Q_{i}(m_{t})
preserves the eigenvalues of
G_{i}(m_{t})
because
Q_{i}(m_{t})
is orthogonal, therefore

\displaystyle T_{i}(m_{t})=\begin{bmatrix}\zeta_{i}&q_{i,1}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{H}}G_{i}(m_{t})q_{i,2}(m_{t})\\
0&\xi_{i}\end{bmatrix},

(143)

where
\xi_{i}
denotes the other eigenvalue of
G_{i}(m_{t})
.
Now,
G_{i}(m_{t})
and, therefore,
Q_{i}(m_{t})
are continuous functions of
m_{t}
, thus

\displaystyle M_{2}=\max_{m_{t}\in\mathopen{[}m/\gamma,L\mathclose{]}}q_{i,1}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{H}}G_{i}(m_{t})q_{i,2}(m_{t})<+\infty

is well-defined.
Moreover, left-multiplying and right-multiplying (
143
) by
Q_{i}(m_{t})
and
Q_{i}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{H}}
, respectively, yields

\displaystyle Q_{i}(m_{t})T_{i}(m_{t})Q_{i}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{H}}=T_{i}(m_{t}).

Substituting the above for
G_{i}(m_{t})
, using submultiplicativity of the Euclidean norm, the fact that
Q_{i}(m_{t})
are orthogonal, and the fact that
\rho(m_{t},\lambda_{i})/\nu\leq 1/(1+\delta)
, where
\nu=(1+\delta)r
, we get

\displaystyle\|P\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\|((G_{i}(m_{t})/\nu)^{k})^{\mathsf{T}}(G_{i}(m_{t}^{k})/\nu)\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\|Q_{i}(m_{t})(T_{i}(m_{t})/\nu)^{k}Q_{i}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{H}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\|Q_{i}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{H}}\|^{2}\|T_{i}(m_{t})/\nu\|^{2k}\|Q_{i}(m_{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\nu^{-2(k+1)}\biggl\|\begin{bmatrix}\rho(m_{t},\lambda_{i})^{k+1}&(k+1)M_{2}\rho(m_{t},\lambda_{i})^{k}\\
0&\rho(m_{t},\lambda_{i})^{k+1}\end{bmatrix}\biggr\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\nu^{-2(k+1)}\Bigl(\rho(m_{t},\lambda_{i})^{k+1}+(k+1)M_{2}\rho(m_{t},\lambda_{i})^{k}\Bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\biggl((1+\delta)^{-(k+1)}+\frac{M_{2}}{\nu}(k+1)(1+\delta)^{-k}\biggr)^{2}.

Then, using the fact that
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
for any
a
and
b
, yields

\displaystyle\|P\|

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\biggl(2(1+\delta)^{-2(k+1)}+\frac{2M_{2}^{2}}{\nu^{2}}(k+1)^{2}(1+\delta)^{-2k}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\frac{2(1+\delta)^{-2}}{1-(1+\delta)^{-2}}+\frac{2M_{2}^{2}}{\nu^{2}}\frac{1+(1+\delta)^{-2}}{(1-(1+\delta)^{-2})^{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+(1+\delta)^{-2}}{1-(1+\delta)^{-2}}+\frac{2M_{2}^{2}}{\nu^{2}}\frac{1+(1+\delta)^{-2}}{(1-(1+\delta)^{-2})^{3}},

where the second inequality follows by noting that for any
\alpha
such that
0<\alpha<1
, we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}(k+1)^{2}\alpha^{k}=\frac{1}{\alpha}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}k^{2}\alpha^{k}=\frac{1}{\alpha}\frac{\alpha(1+\alpha)}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}=\frac{1+\alpha}{(1-\alpha)^{3}},

and then plugging
(1+\delta)^{-2}
into
\alpha
.
∎

Proposition B.14
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
be a quadratic function with
\kappa=L/m\geq 4
, and let
\bar{L}>L
.
Suppose that
4.2
holds for some
\gamma\geq 2
,
4.5
holds for some
\omega>0
, and that
4.4
holds as well.
Also, let
\delta_{m}
and
\delta_{u}
be positive numbers such that
\delta_{u}<\min\{\delta_{m},1/2\}
and
\delta_{m}\leq\gamma-1
.
If
Algorithm

1
receives
\bar{L}
as input, then its iterates
x_{t}
satisfy

\displaystyle\|x_{t+1}-x^{\star}\|\leq C\bar{\kappa}^{2(1+\nu)}r_{\textup{acc}}(2\sigma\bar{\kappa})^{t}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|,

(144)

where
\bar{\kappa}=\bar{L}/m>\kappa
,
\sigma=\max\{\gamma,\sigma_{m},\sigma_{\phi}\}
,
\sigma_{m}=1+2\delta_{m}+2\sqrt{\delta_{m}(1+\delta_{m})}
,
\sigma_{\phi}=\sigma_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\bar{\kappa})
is a function that depends on
\delta_{\ell}=(1+\delta_{m})/(1+\delta_{u})-1
and is bounded in
\bar{\kappa}\geq 1+\delta_{\ell}
, such that

\displaystyle\lim_{\bar{\kappa}\to+\infty}\sigma_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\bar{\kappa})=\frac{1}{4(\sqrt{\delta_{u}(1+\delta_{\ell})+\delta_{\ell}}-\sqrt{\delta_{u}(1+\delta_{\ell})})^{2}},

and
C
is a constant factor that depends on
\gamma,\delta_{u},\sigma
and
\omega
.

Proof.

Let
r^{\prime}=r^{\prime}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)
be a function such that
r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)\leq r^{\prime}<1
for all
\kappa\geq 1+\delta_{\ell}
, where
\sigma_{\phi}=\sigma_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)
is given by
Lemma

B.10
.
Then, by
Lemma

B.12
we have that
m_{t}\leq(1+\delta_{m})m
for all
t\geq\tau
, where

\displaystyle\tau=\frac{-\log(4\kappa^{2}M_{1}\omega/\delta_{u})}{\log r^{\prime}}\sum_{j=0}^{\log_{\gamma}(\kappa/(1+\delta_{m}))}\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{\phi}m(1+\delta_{\ell})(\gamma^{j}-1)},

(145)

M_{1}=\max_{i}\overline{C}_{i}/\underline{C}_{1}
.

4.2
implies that then
m_{t}\geq m/\gamma
.
If
