m_{t}<m
, then by
Lemma

B.1
, and unsing the fact that
(\sqrt{L/m_{t}}-1)/(\sqrt{L/m_{t}}+1)
is decreasing in
m_{t}
and
(\kappa-1)/\kappa
is increasing in
\kappa
, we get

\displaystyle\rho(m_{t},m)=\sqrt{\frac{\sqrt{L/m_{t}}-1}{\sqrt{L/m_{t}}+1}\frac{\kappa-1}{\kappa}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\sqrt{\gamma\kappa}-1}{\sqrt{\gamma\kappa}+1}\frac{\gamma\kappa-1}{\gamma\kappa}}=r_{\textup{acc}}(\gamma\kappa).

Otherwise, if
m_{t}\in\mathopen{[}m,(1+\delta_{m})m\mathclose{]}
, then by
Corollary

B.8
, we have that
\rho(m_{t},m)\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{m}\kappa)
for all
\kappa\geq 1+\delta_{m}
, where
\sigma_{m}=\sigma_{m}(\delta_{m})=1+2\delta_{m}+2\sqrt{\delta_{m}(1+\delta_{m})}
.
Hence,
\rho(m_{t},m)\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{1}\kappa)
for all
t\geq\tau
, where
\sigma_{1}=\max\{\gamma,\sigma_{m}\}
.

Now, by
Lemma

B.3
, we have that
\rho(m_{t},\lambda_{i})\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{1}\kappa)
for all
\lambda_{i}
.
Hence, given
\delta_{\sigma}
such that
(1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{1}\kappa)<1
, by
Lemma

B.13
there is a
P_{i}(m_{t})=P_{i}(m_{t},\delta_{\sigma})\succeq I
for each
\lambda_{i}
such that
G_{i}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{T}}P_{i}(m_{t})G_{i}(m_{t})\preceq(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{2}r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{1}\kappa)^{2}P_{i}(m_{t})
.
Hence, if
t_{j}\leq t<t_{j+1}
and
t\geq\tau
, then

\displaystyle X_{i,t+1}^{\mathsf{T}}P_{i}(m_{t})X_{i,t+1}

\displaystyle=X_{i,t}^{\mathsf{T}}G_{i}(\mu_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}P_{i}(\mu_{j})G_{i}(\mu_{j})X_{i,t}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{2}r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{1}\kappa)^{2}X_{i,t}^{\mathsf{T}}P_{i}(\mu_{j})X_{i,t},

since
m_{t}=\mu_{j}
.
Consecutively applying this inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(P_{i}(m_{t}))\|X_{i,t}\|^{2}\leq X_{i,t}^{\mathsf{T}}P_{i}(m_{t})X_{i,t}

\displaystyle\leq((1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{1}\kappa))^{2(t-t_{j})}X_{i,t_{j}}^{\mathsf{T}}P_{i}(\mu_{j})X_{i,t_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{\max}(P_{i}(\mu_{j}))((1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{1}\kappa))^{2(t-t_{j})}\|X_{i,t_{j}}\|^{2}.

Rearranging the above yields

\displaystyle\|X_{i,t}\|^{2}\leq\frac{\lambda_{\max}(P_{i}(\mu_{j}))}{\lambda_{\min}(P_{i}(\mu_{j}))}((1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{1}\kappa))^{2(t-t_{j})}\|X_{i,t_{j}}\|^{2}.

Moreover, since by assumption
1+\delta_{m}<\gamma
,
m_{t}
is adjusted at most once if
m_{t}\leq(1+\delta_{m})m
, therefore denoting by
\mu_{-1}
and
\mu_{-2}
respectively the last and before last values taken by
m_{t}
, for all
t\geq\tau
we have that

\displaystyle\|X_{i,t}\|^{2}\leq\frac{\lambda_{\max}(P_{i}(\mu_{-1}))}{\lambda_{\min}(P_{i}(\mu_{-1}))}\frac{\lambda_{\max}(P_{i}(\mu_{-2}))}{\lambda_{\min}(P_{i}(\mu_{-2}))}((1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{1}\kappa))^{2(t-\lceil\tau\rceil)}\|X_{i,\lceil\tau\rceil}\|^{2},

In turn, the above bound yields

\displaystyle\|X_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|X_{i,t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{\lambda_{\max}(P_{i}(\mu_{-1}))}{\lambda_{\min}(P_{i}(\mu_{-1}))}\frac{\lambda_{\max}(P_{i}(\mu_{-2}))}{\lambda_{\min}(P_{i}(\mu_{-2}))}((1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{1}\kappa))^{2(t-\lceil\tau\rceil)}\|X_{i,\lceil\tau\rceil}\|^{2}.

Since
r_{\textup{acc}}
is monotone and
\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|X_{i,t}\|^{2}=\|X_{t}\|^{2}
, defining
\sigma=\max\{\gamma,\sigma_{m},\sigma_{\phi}\}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\|X_{t}\|^{2}\leq M_{3}^{2}((1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa))^{2(t-\lceil\tau\rceil)}\|X_{\lceil\tau\rceil}\|^{2},

(146)

where
M_{3}^{2}
is given by the product of the worst condition numbers of all
P_{i}(\mu_{-1})
and
P_{i}(\mu_{-2})
:

\displaystyle M_{3}=\sqrt{\max_{i=1,\ldots,d}\frac{\lambda_{\max}(P_{i}(\mu_{-1}))}{\lambda_{\min}(P_{i}(\mu_{-1}))}\max_{i=1,\ldots,d}\frac{\lambda_{\max}(P_{i}(\mu_{-2}))}{\lambda_{\min}(P_{i}(\mu_{-2}))}}.

Plugging
r^{\prime}=(1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)
in (
145
), it follows that
m_{t}\in\mathopen{[}m/\gamma,(1+\delta_{m})m\mathclose{]}
for all
t\geq\tau
, where

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{-\log(4\kappa^{2}M_{1}\omega/\delta_{u})}{\log(1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)}\sum_{j=0}^{\log_{\gamma}(\kappa/(1+\delta_{m}))}\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{\phi}m(1+\delta_{\ell})(\gamma^{j}-1)}.

By assumption,
\gamma\geq 2
, which implies that
\gamma^{j}-1\geq\gamma^{j-1}
for all
j\geq 1
, so that

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle\leq\frac{-\log(4\kappa^{2}M_{1}\omega/\delta_{u})}{\log(1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)}\biggl(1+\frac{1}{\alpha_{\phi}m(1+\delta_{\ell})}\sum_{j=1}^{\log_{\gamma}(\kappa/(1+\delta_{m}))}\frac{1}{\gamma^{j-1}}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{-\log(4\kappa^{2}M_{1}\omega/\delta_{u})}{\log(1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)}\biggl(1+\frac{1}{\alpha_{\phi}m}\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}\biggr)

Therefore, since
r_{\textup{acc}}(\kappa)\geq 1/2
,
r_{\textup{acc}}(\kappa)\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)\in\mathopen{(}0,1\mathclose{)}
and
\lceil\tau\rceil\leq\tau+1
, it follows that

\displaystyle((1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa))^{-\lceil\tau\rceil}\leq M_{4},

(147)

for a constant
M_{4}
given by

\displaystyle M_{4}=(4\kappa^{2}M_{1}\omega/\delta_{u})^{\nu},\quad\text{where }\nu=1+\gamma/(\alpha_{\phi}m(\gamma-1)).

Then, plugging (
147
) into (
146
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|x_{t}\|\leq\|X_{t}\|\leq M_{3}M_{4}((1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa))^{t}\|X_{\lceil\tau\rceil}\|.

To establish (
144
), it remains to bound
\|X_{\lceil\tau\rceil}\|
.
To this end, we plug
x=x^{\star}
and
y=y_{t+1}
into (
3
), and then use the global convergence bound from (
4.1
) to get

\displaystyle\|y_{t+1}-x^{\star}\|^{2}\leq\frac{2}{m}(f(y_{t+1})-f(x^{\star}))\leq 4\kappa r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)^{t}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}.

Then, substituting
x_{t+1}
with its definition from
Algorithm

1
, summing
\pm\beta_{t}x^{\star}=0
, using the above bound and then the fact that
\beta_{t}\in\mathopen{[}0,1\mathclose{)}
and that
r_{\textup{GD}}\in\mathopen{(}0,1\mathclose{)}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|x_{t+1}-x^{\star}\|^{2}=\|(1+\beta_{t})y_{t+1}-\beta_{t}y_{t}-x^{\star}\pm\beta_{t}x^{\star}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|(1+\beta_{t})(y_{t+1}-x^{\star})-\beta_{t}(y_{t}-x^{\star})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(2\|y_{t+1}-x^{\star}\|+\|y_{t}-x^{\star}\|)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 36\kappa r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)^{t-1}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2},

(148)

which implies that

\displaystyle\|X_{\lceil\tau\rceil}\|\leq\|x_{\lceil\tau\rceil}\|+\|x_{\lceil\tau\rceil-1}\|\leq 12\sqrt{\kappa}r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)^{(\lceil\tau\rceil-3)/2}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|\leq 12\sqrt{\kappa}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|.

If
\lceil\tau\rceil\geq 3
, then

\displaystyle\|X_{\lceil\tau\rceil}\|\leq 12\sqrt{\kappa}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|.

Otherwise, if
\lceil\tau\rceil<3
, then using the fact that
r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)\geq r_{\textup{acc}}(\kappa)=1/2
, which follows from the assumption that
\kappa\geq 4
, and the assumption that
\gamma\geq 2
, we obtain

\displaystyle((1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa))^{-\lceil\tau\rceil}\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)^{-3}\leq\frac{\gamma^{2}}{r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)}.

Moreover, since the following equivalences hold for
\kappa\geq 4

\displaystyle r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)^{2}\geq r_{\textup{acc}}(\kappa)\iff\frac{(\kappa-1)^{2}}{\kappa^{2}}\geq\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}-1}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\iff\kappa^{2}-2\kappa\sqrt{\kappa}+\sqrt{\kappa}\geq 0,

we have that

\displaystyle r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)^{-3/2}\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\kappa)^{-1}\leq\delta_{u}^{-1}.

Combining the two bounds above yields

\displaystyle((1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa))^{-\lceil\tau\rceil}r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)^{-3/2}\leq M_{4}.

Therefore, for all values of
\lceil\tau\rceil
, we have that

\displaystyle\|x_{t+1}-x^{\star}\|\leq M_{3}M_{4}\sqrt{\kappa}((1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa))^{t}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|.

(149)

Our next step is to express the rate
(1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)
in terms of
r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{2}\kappa)
for some
\sigma_{2}
, as in

\displaystyle(1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)=(1+\delta_{\sigma})\frac{\sqrt{\sigma\kappa}-1}{\sqrt{\sigma\kappa}}=\frac{\sqrt{\sigma_{2}\kappa}-1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{2}\kappa}}.

Solving the above identity for
\sigma_{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}=\frac{\sigma}{(1+\delta_{\sigma}-\delta_{\sigma}\sqrt{\sigma\kappa})^{2}}\leq\frac{\sigma}{(1-\delta_{\sigma}\sqrt{\sigma\kappa})^{2}}.

That is,
\sigma_{2}=(1+\delta)\sigma
, where

\displaystyle\delta=\frac{\delta_{\sigma}\sqrt{\sigma\kappa}(2-\delta_{\sigma}\sqrt{\sigma\kappa})}{(1-\delta_{\sigma}\sqrt{\sigma\kappa})^{2}}.

So, if
\delta_{\sigma}=1/(4\sqrt{\sigma\kappa})
, then
\delta\leq 7/9
, which implies that
1+\delta\leq 2
and

\displaystyle(1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(2\sigma\kappa).

Moreover, since
\sigma\geq\gamma\geq 2
and
\kappa\geq 4
, it follows that
\delta_{\sigma}=1/(4\sqrt{\sigma\kappa})\leq 1/11
and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{-2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{2}}{\delta_{\sigma}(2+\delta_{\sigma})}\leq\frac{(1+1/11)^{2}}{2}\frac{1}{\delta_{\sigma}}=\frac{12^{2}}{2\cdot 11^{2}}\frac{1}{\delta_{\sigma}},

\displaystyle 1-(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{-2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta_{\sigma}(2+\delta_{\sigma})}{(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{2}}\leq\delta_{\sigma}(2+\sigma_{\delta})=\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\sigma\kappa}}(2+1/(4\sqrt{\sigma\kappa}))\leq\frac{9}{11^{2}},

\displaystyle 1+(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{-2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2+\delta_{\sigma}(2+\delta_{\sigma})}{1+\delta_{\sigma}}\leq 2(1+\delta_{\sigma}).

In the same vein, using the fact that
\lambda_{\min}(P_{i}(\mu_{-1}))\geq 1
and that
\lambda_{\max}(P_{i}(\mu_{-1}))\leq\|P_{i}(\mu_{-1})\|
, plugging
\delta_{\sigma}=1/(4\sqrt{\sigma\kappa})
into (
140
) and using the fact that
r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)\geq r_{\textup{acc}}(8)
yields

\displaystyle\max_{i=1,\ldots,d}\frac{\lambda_{\max}(P_{i}(\mu_{-1}))}{\lambda_{\min}(P_{i}(\mu_{-1}))}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i=1,\ldots,d}\|P_{i}(\mu_{-1})\|

\displaystyle<1+2\frac{(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{-2}}{1-(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{-2}}+2\frac{M_{2}^{2}}{(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{2}r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)^{2}}\frac{1+(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{-2}}{(1-(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{-2})^{3}}

\displaystyle<1+\frac{12^{2}}{2\cdot 11^{2}}\frac{(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{-2}}{\delta_{\sigma}}+4\frac{M_{2}^{2}}{(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{2}r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)^{2}}\frac{1+\delta_{\sigma}}{\delta_{\sigma}^{3}}

\displaystyle<\biggl(\frac{1}{11^{3}}+\frac{12^{2}}{2\cdot 11^{4}}+\frac{4}{r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\kappa)^{2}}\biggr)\frac{M_{2}^{2}}{\delta_{\sigma}^{3}}

\displaystyle<7\frac{M_{2}^{2}}{\delta_{\sigma}^{3}}.

Using the above bound twice yields

\displaystyle M_{3}=\sqrt{\max_{i=1,\ldots,d}\frac{\lambda_{\max}(P_{i}(\mu_{-1}))}{\lambda_{\min}(P_{i}(\mu_{-1}))}\max_{i=1,\ldots,d}\frac{\lambda_{\max}(P_{i}(\mu_{-2}))}{\lambda_{\min}(P_{i}(\mu_{-2}))}}<7\frac{M_{2}^{2}}{\delta_{\sigma}^{3}}=7\cdot 4^{3}M_{2}^{2}\sigma^{3/2}\kappa^{3/2}.

(150)

Finally, we prove (
144
) by plugging (
150
) into (
149
), and then replacing
\kappa
with
\bar{\kappa}
, so that

\displaystyle\|x_{t+1}-x^{\star}\|\leq C\bar{\kappa}^{2(1+\nu)}r_{\textup{acc}}(2\sigma\bar{\kappa})^{t}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|,

where the constant
C
is given by

\displaystyle C=84\cdot 4^{3}M_{2}(4M_{1}\omega/\delta_{u})^{\nu}\sigma^{3/2}.

∎

B.2
General case

We now build on the quadratic case to prove that the iterates
x_{t}
of
Algorithm

1
also converge to the optimum
x^{\star}
at an accelerated rate when the objective function
f
is not necessarily quadratic.
Our approach is to show that if
x_{t}
is sufficiently close to
x^{\star}
, then
x_{t}-x^{\star}
consists of a perturbation of the iterate when the objective is given by local quadratic approximation of
f
at
x^{\star}
.

B.2.1
Iterate dynamics in the general case

Under
4.3
, it follows that
f
is twice continuously differentiable at
x^{\star}
.
Hence, by Taylor’s theorem
[
Nocedal and Wright 2006
, theorem 2.1]
, the gradient at
x_{t}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\nabla f(x_{t})=

\displaystyle\nabla f(x^{\star})+\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{2}f(x^{\star}+s(x_{t}-x^{\star}))(x_{t}-x^{\star})ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}f(x^{\star})x_{t}+\int_{0}^{1}(\nabla^{2}f(x^{\star}+s(x_{t}-x^{\star}))-\nabla^{2}f(x^{\star}))(x_{t}-x^{\star})ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(H+\tilde{H}_{t})(x_{t}-x^{\star}),

(151)

where the Hessian error term
\tilde{H}_{t}=\tilde{H}_{t}(x_{t})
is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{H}_{t}=\int_{0}^{1}(\nabla^{2}f(x^{\star}+s(x_{t}-x^{\star}))-\nabla^{2}f(x^{\star}))ds.

(152)

Moreover, by (
148
), we have that
\|x_{t+1}-x^{\star}\|\leq\sqrt{36\kappa r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)^{t-1}}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|
.
Since
r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)\in\mathopen{(}0,1\mathclose{)}
, if
\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|\leq\epsilon\sqrt{r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)/36\kappa}
and
\epsilon\leq\delta_{H}
, then for all
t\geq 0
, we have that
\|x_{t}-x^{\star}\|\leq\delta_{H}
, and

\displaystyle\|\tilde{H}_{t}\|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\|\nabla^{2}f(x^{\star}+s(x_{t}-x^{\star}))-\nabla^{2}f(x^{\star}))\|ds

\displaystyle\leq L_{H}\int_{0}^{1}s\|x_{t}-x^{\star}\|ds

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon L_{H}r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)^{t/2}.

(153)

Since
v_{j}
form an eigenbasis for
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\tilde{H}_{t}v_{j}
can be expressed in
v_{j}
-coordinates,
\tilde{h}_{i,j,t}
, as

\displaystyle\tilde{H}_{t}v_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\tilde{h}_{i,j,t}v_{i},

\displaystyle j=1,\ldots,d.

(154)

Then, using (
154
) and the decomposition
x_{t}-x^{\star}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}x_{j,t}v_{j}
yields

\displaystyle\tilde{H}_{t}(x_{t}-x^{\star})=\tilde{H}_{t}\sum_{j=1}^{d}x_{j,t}v_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}x_{j,t}\tilde{H}_{t}v_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}x_{j,t}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\tilde{h}_{i,j,t}v_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\tilde{h}_{i,j,t}x_{j,t}v_{i}.

(155)

In turn, combining the decomposition
x_{t}-x^{\star}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}x_{j,t}v_{j}
with
151
and
155
, we obtain

\displaystyle y_{t+1}-x^{\star}

\displaystyle=x_{t}-(1/L)\nabla f(x_{t})-x^{\star}

\displaystyle=(I-H/L-\tilde{H}_{t}/L)(x_{t}-x^{\star})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Bigl[(1-\lambda_{i}/L)x_{i,t}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\tilde{h}_{i,j,t}/L)x_{j,t}\Bigr]v_{i},

from which it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{d}x_{j,t+1}v_{j}=

\displaystyle\ x_{t+1}-x^{\star}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (1+\beta_{t})y_{t+1}-\beta_{t}y_{t}-x^{\star}\mp\beta_{t}x^{\star}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{d}\biggl[(1+\beta_{t})\Bigl(1-\frac{\lambda_{i}}{L}\Bigr)x_{i,t}-\beta_{t}\Bigl(1-\frac{\lambda_{i}}{L}\Bigr)x_{i,t-1}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Bigl((1+\beta_{t})\frac{\tilde{h}_{i,j,t}}{L}x_{j,t}-\beta_{t}\frac{\tilde{h}_{i,j,t}}{L}x_{j,t-1}\Bigr)\biggr]v_{i}.

Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle X_{t+1}=(G(m_{t})+\tilde{G}_{t})X_{t},

(156)

where
X_{t}
is the vector with “stacked”
X_{i,t}
, as in

\displaystyle X_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}X_{1,t}\\
\vdots\\
X_{d,t}\end{bmatrix},

(157)

while
G(m_{t})
and
\tilde{G}_{t}
are matrices given by

\displaystyle G(m_{t})

\displaystyle=\diag(G_{1}(m_{t}),\ldots,G_{d}(m_{t})),

(158)

\displaystyle\tilde{G}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L}\begin{bmatrix}0&0&\ldots&0&0\\
-\beta_{t}\tilde{h}_{1,1,t-1}&(1+\beta_{t})\tilde{h}_{1,1,t}&\ldots&-\beta_{t}\tilde{h}_{1,d,t-1}&(1+\beta_{t})\tilde{h}_{1,d,t}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&0&0\\
-\beta_{t}\tilde{h}_{d,1,t-1}&(1+\beta_{t})\tilde{h}_{d,1,t}&\ldots&-\beta_{t}\tilde{h}_{d,d,t-1}&(1+\beta_{t})\tilde{h}_{d,d,t}\end{bmatrix},

(159)

where
G_{i}(m_{t})
, defined by (
104
), are the system matrices governing the dynamics of each
X_{i,t}
in the quadratic case where
f(x)=(x-x^{\star})^{\mathsf{T}}H_{t}(x-x^{\star})
.
Using the fact that
\tilde{h}_{i,j,t}=v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\tilde{H}_{t}v_{j}
, the matrix
\tilde{G}_{t}
given by (
159
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle\tilde{G}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\beta_{t}}{L}W_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}V^{\mathsf{T}}\tilde{H}_{t}VW_{1}-\frac{\beta_{t}}{L}W_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}V^{\mathsf{T}}\tilde{H}_{t}VW_{2},

(160)

where the matrices
V\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
,
W_{1},W_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times 2d}
are given by

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}v_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\\
\vdots\\
v_{d}^{\mathsf{T}}\end{bmatrix}^{\mathsf{T}},

\displaystyle W_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1&0&0&\ldots&0&0\\
0&0&0&1&\ldots&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&0&\ldots&0&0\\
0&0&0&0&\ldots&0&1\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle W_{2}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0&0&\ldots&0&0\\
0&0&1&0&\ldots&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&0&\ldots&0&0\\
0&0&0&0&\ldots&1&0\end{bmatrix}.

Since
v_{i}
are orthonormal, so is
V
.
Thus,
V
has unitary norm, as do
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
.
Therefore, applying the triangle inequality and norm submultiplicativity to (
160
), then using the fact that
\beta_{t}\in\mathopen{[}0,1\mathclose{)}
and lastly plugging in (
153
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|\tilde{G}_{t}\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{L}\|W_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\|\|V^{\mathsf{T}}\|\|\tilde{H}_{t}\|\|V\|\|W_{1}\|+\frac{1}{L}\|W_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\|\|V^{\mathsf{T}}\|\|\tilde{H}_{t}\|\|V\|\|W_{2}\|

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{L}\|\tilde{H}_{t}\|

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon\frac{L_{H}}{L}r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)^{t/2}.

(161)

We continue by noting that if
4.4
holds for some
\delta_{\lambda}>0
, then it also holds for every
\delta_{\lambda}^{\prime}<\delta_{\lambda}
.
So, without loss of generality, suppose that
4.4
holds for some
\delta_{\lambda}\leq\delta_{m}m
.
Then, while
m_{t}>(1+\delta_{m})m
, we have that
|m_{t}-\lambda_{i}|\geq\delta_{\lambda}
for all
i=1,\ldots,d
.
Hence, noting that for all
\lambda_{i}
we have that
\lambda_{i}<\bar{L}
, then from
Corollary

B.2
, it follows that the two eigenvalues
\zeta_{i}(m_{t})
and
\xi_{i}(m_{t})
of each
G_{i}(m_{t})
are distinct.
Therefore, because
\zeta_{i}(m_{t})
and
\xi_{i}(m_{t})
are continuous in
m_{t}
, we have that

\displaystyle\delta_{T}=\min_{m_{t}\in\mathcal{S}}\min_{i=1,\ldots,d}|\zeta_{i}(m_{t})-\xi_{i}(m_{t})|>0,

(162)

where
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}(\delta_{\lambda})
is a compact set defined in terms

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\mathopen{[}(1+\delta_{m})m,L\mathclose{]}\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{d}B(\lambda_{i},\delta_{\lambda}),

and
B(\lambda_{i},\delta_{\lambda})=\{x:|x-\lambda_{i}|<\delta_{\lambda}\}
is the open ball of radius
\delta_{\lambda}
centered at
\lambda_{i}
.
In the same vein, since
T_{i}
defined by (
121
) are continuous in
\zeta_{i}
and
\xi_{i}
, and
\|\cdot\|
is continuous, it follows that

\displaystyle\max_{m_{t}\in\mathcal{S}}\max_{i=1,\ldots,d}\|T_{i}(m_{t})\|<\infty.

Furthermore, explicitly computing the inverse of
T_{i}
for
m_{t}\in\mathcal{S}
yields

\displaystyle\|T_{i}(m_{t})^{-1}\|=\frac{1}{|\zeta_{i}-\xi_{i}|}\biggl\|\begin{bmatrix}\xi_{i}&-1\\
-\zeta_{i}&1\end{bmatrix}\biggr\|\leq\frac{1}{\delta_{T}}\biggl\|\begin{bmatrix}\xi_{i}&-1\\
-\zeta_{i}&1\end{bmatrix}\biggr\|.

(163)

Hence, since both sides of (
163
) are continuous in
m_{t}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\max_{m_{t}\in\mathcal{S}}\max_{i=1,\ldots,d}\|T_{i}(m_{t})^{-1}\|<\infty.

Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle M_{T}=\max_{m_{t}\in\mathcal{S}}\max_{i=1,\ldots,d}\|T_{i}(m_{t})\|\|T_{i}(m_{t})^{-1}\|<+\infty.

(164)

Then, let
T
denote the coordinate transformation given by

\displaystyle T(m_{t})=\diag(T_{1}(m_{t}),\ldots,T_{d}(m_{t})).

(165)

The block-diagonal structure of
T
combined with (
164
) implies that

\displaystyle\max_{m_{t}\in\mathcal{S}}\|T(m_{t})\|\|T(m_{t})^{-1}\|\leq M_{T}.

(166)

Furthermore,
T(m_{t})
diagonalizes
G(m_{t})
, as in

\displaystyle G(m_{t})=T(m_{t})D(m_{t})T(m_{t})^{-1},

(167)

where
D(m_{t})
is the block-diagonal matrix defined as
D(m_{t})=\diag(D_{1}(m_{t}),\ldots,D_{d}(m_{t}))
and
D_{i}(m_{t})
are the diagonal matrices given by (
122
).
So, defining the state
Z_{t}=T^{-1}(\mu_{0})X_{t}
for
t\in\mathopen{[}t_{0},t_{1}\mathclose{]}
and plugging
Z_{t}
and (
167
) into (
156
), since
m_{t}\equiv\mu_{0}
for
t\in\mathopen{[}t_{0},t_{1}\mathclose{)}
, it follows that

\displaystyle Z_{t+1}

\displaystyle=T^{-1}(\mu_{0})X_{t+1}

\displaystyle=T^{-1}(\mu_{0})(G(m_{t})+\tilde{G}_{t})X_{t}

\displaystyle=T^{-1}(G(\mu_{0})+\tilde{G}_{t})T(\mu_{0})Z_{t}

\displaystyle=(D(\mu_{0})+\tilde{D}_{t})Z_{t},

(168)

where
\tilde{D}_{t}
is a perturbation matrix given by

\displaystyle\tilde{D}_{t}=T^{-1}(m_{t})\tilde{G}_{t}T(m_{t}).

(169)

Using submultiplicativity and then combining (
161
) with (
166
), yields

\displaystyle\|\tilde{D}_{t}\|\leq\|T^{-1}(m_{t})\|\|\tilde{G}_{t}\|\|T(m_{t})\|\leq\epsilon M_{T}\frac{L_{H}}{L}r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)^{t/2}.

(170)

Then, summing (
170
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{t=0}^{+\infty}\|\tilde{D}_{t}\|\leq\epsilon M_{T}\frac{L_{H}}{L}\sum_{t=0}^{+\infty}r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)^{t/2}\leq\epsilon M_{T}\frac{L_{H}}{L}\frac{1}{1-\sqrt{r_{\textup{GD}}(\kappa)}}.

(171)

Moreover, since
\lambda_{i}\leq L<\bar{L}
, it follows that
G(m_{t})
are nonsingular.
This fact combined with (
171
) allows us to use results from the theory of asymptotic integration of difference equations
[
Bodine and Lutz 2015
]
to establish that the solutions to (
168
) are perturbed solutions of the particular case when
\tilde{D}_{t}\equiv 0
.
Namely, by
[
Bodine and Lutz 2015
, Thm 3.4, p.73]
, for
t\in\mathopen{[}t_{0},t_{1}\mathclose{)}
we have that

\displaystyle Z_{t+1}=[I+O(\epsilon)]D(\mu_{0})^{t}Z_{0},

which implies that for
t\in\mathopen{[}t_{0},t_{1}\mathclose{)}
, we have that

\displaystyle X_{t+1}=T(\mu_{0})[I+O(\epsilon)]D(\mu_{0})^{t}Z_{0}=T(\mu_{0})[I+O(\epsilon)]D(\mu_{0})^{t}T(\mu_{0})^{-1}X_{0},

which can also be written as a perturbation of the solution of the quadratic case
G(\mu_{0})^{t}X_{0}
:

\displaystyle X_{t+1}=G(\mu_{0})^{t}X_{0}+T(\mu_{0})D_{0}^{t}O(\epsilon)T(\mu_{0})^{-1}X_{0}.

By repeatedly following the above procedure, we conclude that

\displaystyle X_{t+1}=

\displaystyle T(\mu_{J})[I+O(\epsilon)]D(\mu_{J})^{t-t_{J}}T(\mu_{J})^{-1}\biggl(\prod_{j=0}^{J-1}T(\mu_{j})[I+O(\epsilon)]D(\mu_{j})^{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}T(\mu_{j})^{-1}\biggr)X_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T(\mu_{J})D(\mu_{J})^{t-t_{J}}T(\mu_{J})^{-1}\biggl(\prod_{j=0}^{J-1}T(\mu_{j})D(\mu_{j})^{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}T(\mu_{j})^{-1}\biggr)X_{0}

\displaystyle+T(\mu_{J})O(\epsilon)D(\mu_{J})^{t-t_{J}}T(\mu_{J})^{-1}\biggl(\prod_{j=0}^{J-1}T(\mu_{j})D(\mu_{j})^{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}T(\mu_{j})^{-1}\biggr)X_{0}+\ldots

\displaystyle+T(\mu_{J})O(\epsilon)D(\mu_{J})^{t-t_{J}}T(\mu_{J})^{-1}\biggl(\prod_{j=0}^{J-1}T(\mu_{j})O(\epsilon)D(\mu_{j})^{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}T(\mu_{j})^{-1}\biggr)X_{0},

(172)

where
t\in\mathopen{[}t_{J},t_{J+1}\mathclose{)}
.

B.2.2
The dynamics of
c_{t}
in the general case

Having established that the components
X_{i,t}
in the general case behave like perturbed components of the quadratic case, we can also derive the dynamics of
c_{t}
in the general case.
To this end, we establish bounds on the differences
x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t}
.
First, we notice that

\displaystyle X_{i,t+1}=\begin{bmatrix}0&\ldots&0&I&0&\ldots&0\end{bmatrix}X_{t+1},

where
\begin{bmatrix}0&\ldots&0&I&0&\ldots&0\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2d}
is a matrix made of a row of two-by-two blocks, where the
i
-th block is
I\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
and all other blocks are
0\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
.
Also, we have that

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0&\ldots&0&I&0&\ldots&0\end{bmatrix}T(\mu_{J})D(\mu_{J})^{t-t_{J}}T(\mu_{J})^{-1}\biggl(\prod_{j=0}^{J-1}T(\mu_{j})D(\mu_{j})^{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}T(\mu_{j})^{-1}\biggr)X_{0}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}0&\ldots&0&I&0&\ldots&0\end{bmatrix}G(\mu_{J})^{t-t_{J}}\prod_{j=0}^{J-1}G(\mu_{j})^{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}X_{0}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}0&\ldots&0&G_{i}^{t-t_{J}}\prod_{j=0}^{J-1}G_{i}(\mu_{j})^{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}&0&\ldots&0\end{bmatrix}X_{0}

\displaystyle=G_{i}^{t-t_{J}}\prod_{j=0}^{J-1}G_{i}(\mu_{j})^{t_{j+1}-t_{j}}X_{i,0},

since
G_{i}(\mu_{j})=T_{i}(\mu_{j})D_{i}(\mu_{j})T_{i}(\mu_{j})^{-1}
, by (
122
), and
G,T
and
D
are block-diagonal matrices with blocks given by
G_{i},T_{i}
and
D_{i}
, respectively.
Then, we notice that all but the first term in (
172
) are
O(\epsilon)
, and
\rho(\mu_{j},\lambda_{i})\leq\rho(\mu_{j},m)
for all the eigenvalues
\rho(\mu_{j},\lambda_{i})
of
D(\mu_{j})
, by
Lemma

B.3
.
Therefore, combining the above remarks with
4.5
, it follows that for
t\in\mathopen{[}t_{j},t_{j+1}\mathclose{)}

\displaystyle\|X_{i,t+1}\|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\ \overline{C}_{i}\rho(\mu_{j},\lambda_{i})^{2(t-t_{j})}\Biggl(\prod_{k=0}^{j-1}\rho(\mu_{k},\lambda_{i})^{2(t_{k+1}-t_{k})}\Biggr)x_{i,0}^{2}

\displaystyle+O(\epsilon)\rho(\mu_{j},m)^{2(t-t_{j})}\Biggl(\prod_{k=0}^{j-1}\rho(\mu_{k},m)^{2(t_{k+1}-t_{k})}\Biggr)x_{1,0}^{2},

for some
\overline{C}_{i}
.
The above bound is analogous to (
124
), but with an additional
O(\epsilon)
term accounting for the perturbation of the quadratic solution.
Thus, for
t\in\mathopen{[}t_{j},t_{j+1}\mathclose{)}
, we have that

\displaystyle(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}=

\displaystyle\ (\begin{bmatrix}-1&1\end{bmatrix}X_{i,t+1})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ 2\overline{C}_{i}\rho(\mu_{j},\lambda_{i})^{2(t-t_{j})}\Biggl(\prod_{k=0}^{j-1}\rho(\mu_{k},\lambda_{i})^{2(t_{k+1}-t_{k})}\Biggr)x_{i,0}^{2}

\displaystyle+O(\epsilon)\rho(\mu_{j},m)^{2(t-t_{j})}\Biggl(\prod_{k=0}^{j-1}\rho(\mu_{k},m)^{2(t_{k+1}-t_{k})}\Biggr)x_{1,0}^{2}.

(173)

In the same vein, combining (
172
) with
Lemma

B.3
, we have that for
t\in\mathopen{[}t_{j},t_{j+1}\mathclose{)}

\displaystyle\|X_{t+1}\|^{2}\leq 2(1+O(\epsilon))\overline{C}\rho(\mu_{j},m)^{2(t-t_{j})}\Biggl(\prod_{k=0}^{j-1}\rho(\mu_{k},m)^{2(t_{k+1}-t_{k})}\Biggr)\|x_{0}\|^{2},

(174)

where
\overline{C}=\max_{i=1,\ldots,d}\overline{C}_{i}
.
Similarly, combining the derivation of (
127
) with (
172
) and
4.5
, for
t\in\mathopen{[}t_{j},t_{j+1}\mathclose{)}
we have that

\displaystyle(x_{1,t+1}-x_{1,t})^{2}\geq(1-O(\epsilon))\underline{C}_{1}\rho(\mu_{j},m)^{2(t-t_{j})}\Biggl(\prod_{k=0}^{j-1}\rho(\mu_{k},m)^{2(t_{k+1}-t_{k})}\Biggr)x_{1,0}^{2},

(175)

for some
\underline{C}_{1}
.

Our next step is to also express
\nabla f(x_{t+1})-\nabla f(x_{t})
as a perturbation of the quadratic case.
To this end, we substitute (
151
) for
\nabla f(x_{t+1})
and
\nabla f(x_{t})
, and obtain

\displaystyle\nabla f(x_{t+1})-\nabla f(x_{t})

\displaystyle=(H+\tilde{H}_{t+1})(x_{t+1}-x^{\star})-(H+\tilde{H}_{t})(x_{t}-x^{\star})

\displaystyle=H(x_{t+1}-x_{t})+\tilde{H}_{t+1}(x_{t+1}-x^{\star})-\tilde{H}_{t}(x_{t}-x^{\star}).

Using
x_{t}-x^{\star}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}x_{j,t}v_{j}
, the terms of
\nabla f(x_{t+1})-\nabla f(x_{t})
above can be written as

\displaystyle H(x_{t+1}-x_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})\lambda_{i}v_{i},

\displaystyle\tilde{H}_{t+1}(x_{t+1}-x^{\star})-\tilde{H}_{t}(x_{t}-x^{\star})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\tilde{h}_{i,j,t+1}x_{j,t+1}-\tilde{h}_{i,j,t}x_{j,t})\Biggr)v_{i}.

In turn, using the above expressions, it follows that

\displaystyle\|\nabla f(x_{t+1})-\nabla f(x_{t})\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{2}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}

\displaystyle+2\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\tilde{h}_{i,j,t+1}x_{j,t+1}-\tilde{h}_{i,j,t}x_{j,t})

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\tilde{h}_{i,j,t+1}x_{j,t+1}-\tilde{h}_{i,j,t}x_{j,t})\Biggr)^{2}.

Our next step is to bound the third term above in
\|\nabla f(x_{t+1})-\nabla f(x_{t})\|^{2}
.
Combining the identities
\|\tilde{H}_{t+1}\|_{F}^{2}+\|\tilde{H}_{t}\|_{F}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\tilde{h}_{i,j,t+1}^{2}+\tilde{h}_{i,j,t}^{2})
and
\|X_{t+1}\|^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}(x_{j,t+1}^{2}+x_{j,t}^{2})
with the bound (
153
), it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\tilde{h}_{i,j,t+1}x_{j,t+1}-\tilde{h}_{i,j,t}x_{j,t})\Biggr)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{d}(|\tilde{h}_{i,j,t+1}|+|\tilde{h}_{i,j,t}|)(|x_{j,t+1}|+|x_{j,t}|)\Biggr)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{d}(|\tilde{h}_{i,j,t+1}|+|\tilde{h}_{i,j,t}|)^{2}\Biggr)\Biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{d}(|x_{j,t+1}|+|x_{j,t}|)^{2}\Biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Biggl(2\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\tilde{h}_{i,j,t+1}^{2}+\tilde{h}_{i,j,t}^{2})\Biggr)\Biggl(2\sum_{j=1}^{d}(x_{j,t+1}^{2}+x_{j,t}^{2})\Biggr)

\displaystyle=4(\|\tilde{H}_{t+1}\|_{F}^{2}+\|\tilde{H}_{t}\|_{F}^{2})\|X_{t+1}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4d(\|\tilde{H}_{t+1}\|^{2}+\|\tilde{H}_{t}\|^{2})\|X_{t+1}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\epsilon^{2}L_{H}^{2}d\|X_{t+1}\|^{2}.

(176)

In turn, we address the second term of
\|\nabla f(x_{t+1})-\nabla f(x_{t})\|^{2}
using (
176
), which gives

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\tilde{h}_{i,j,t+1}x_{j,t+1}-\tilde{h}_{i,j,t}x_{j,t})

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\tilde{h}_{i,j,t+1}x_{j,t+1}-\tilde{h}_{i,j,t}x_{j,t})\Biggr)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i,t+1}^{2}+x_{i,t}^{2})}\sqrt{4\epsilon^{2}L_{H}^{2}d\|X_{t+1}\|^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\epsilon L_{H}\sqrt{2d}\|X_{t+1}\|^{2}.

(177)

Then, combining
174
,
176
and
177
we obtain

\displaystyle\|\nabla f(x_{t+1})-\nabla f(x_{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{2}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}

\displaystyle+O(\epsilon)\rho(\mu_{j},m)^{2(t-t_{j})}\Biggl(\prod_{k=0}^{j-1}\rho(\mu_{k},m)^{2(t_{k+1}-t_{k})}\Biggr)x_{1,0}^{2}.

(178)

In turn, plugging (
178
) into (
4
), and then using (
175
), it follows that

\displaystyle c_{t+1}^{2}=c(x_{t+1},x_{t})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{2}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}+O(\epsilon).

(179)

Lemma B.15
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
and suppose that
4.2
,
4.3
,
4.4
and
4.5
hold.
Also, let
\delta_{m}
and
\delta_{u}
be positive numbers such that
\delta_{m}>\delta_{u}>0
and let
r^{\prime}=r^{\prime}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)
be a function such that
r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)\leq r^{\prime}<1
for all
\kappa\geq 1+\delta_{\ell}
, where
\delta_{\ell}=((1+\delta_{m})/(1+\delta_{u}))-1>0
and
\sigma_{\phi}=\sigma_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)
is given by
Lemma

B.10
.
Then, there exists some
\epsilon>0
such that if
\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|\leq\epsilon
, then the estimates
m_{t}
of
Algorithm

1
reach
[m/\gamma,(1+\delta_{m})m]
after no more than
\tau
iterations, where

\displaystyle\tau=\frac{-\log(8\kappa^{2}M_{1}\omega/\delta_{u})}{\log r^{\prime}}\sum_{j=0}^{\log_{\gamma}(\kappa/(1+\delta_{m}))}\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{\phi}m(1+\delta_{\ell})(\gamma^{j}-1)},

(180)

M_{1}=\max_{i}\overline{C}_{i}/\underline{C}_{1}
, with
\omega
and
\gamma
given by
4.2
,
4.3
,
4.4
and
4.5
.

Proof.

Suppose that the last value of
m_{t}
before reaching the interval
[m/\gamma,(1+\delta_{m})m)
is
(1+\delta_{m})m
.
Then, suppose that the value before last is
\gamma(1+\delta_{m})m
, and so on, up to
\gamma^{K}(1+\delta_{m})m
for some
K
such that
\gamma^{K}(1+\delta_{m})m\leq L<\gamma^{K+1}(1+\delta_{m})m
.
Using this
m_{t}
schedule, we bound the number of iterations that
m_{t}
takes to reach the interval
[m/\gamma,(1+\delta_{m})m]
, and then we argue that no other
m_{t}
schedule can lead to a worse bound.

Let
\ell_{j}=\gamma^{j}(1+\delta_{m})m/(1+\delta_{u})
.
Then, we have that
\ell_{j}\geq(1+\delta_{\ell})m
for
\delta_{\ell}=((1+\delta_{m})/(1+\delta_{u}))-1
.
Since
\delta_{m}>\delta_{u}
, then
\delta_{\ell}>0
, and
Lemma

B.11
applies.
Now, let
I_{j}=\min\left\{i:\lambda_{i}\geq\ell_{j}\right\}
.
That is,
\lambda_{i}\geq\ell_{j}
if and only if
i\geq I_{j}
.
Then, using this fact and separating the terms indexed by
i<I_{0}
from those indexed by
i\geq I_{0}
in (
179
) into two sums yields

\displaystyle c^{2}_{t+1}<\ell_{0}^{2}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{I_{0}-1}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}+\frac{\sum_{i=I_{0}}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{2}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}+O(\epsilon).

In turn, plugging the above inequality into the identity
(c_{t+1}+\ell_{0})(c_{t+1}-\ell_{0})=c^{2}_{t+1}-\ell_{0}^{2}
, and then using the fact that
\lambda_{i}\leq L
and
\ell_{0}\geq m
, we obtain

\displaystyle c_{t+1}-\ell_{0}=\frac{c^{2}_{t+1}-\ell_{0}^{2}}{c_{t+1}+\ell_{0}}

\displaystyle<\frac{\sum_{i=I_{0}}^{d}(\lambda_{i}^{2}-\ell_{0}^{2})(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}{\ell_{0}\sum_{i=1}^{d}(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}+O(\epsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\ell_{0}\kappa^{2}\sum_{i=I_{0}}^{d}\frac{(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}{(x_{1,t+1}-x_{1,t})^{2}}+O(\epsilon).

(181)

To address the terms in the sum in (
181
), we combine (
173
) and (
175
), assuming
t_{K}\leq t<t_{K+1}
.
That is, we consider the last adjustment before
m_{t}
reaches the interval
[m/\gamma,(1+\delta_{m})m)
.
Then, we apply
Lemma

B.3
twice, to get
\rho(m_{k},\lambda_{i})\leq\rho(m_{k},\ell_{0})
and
\rho(m_{k},\ell_{0})<\rho(m_{k},m)
for all
i\geq I_{0}
, which gives

\displaystyle\sum_{i=I_{0}}^{d}\frac{(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}{(x_{1,t+1}-x_{1,t})^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+O(\epsilon))M_{1}\sum_{i=I_{0}}^{d}\frac{x_{i,0}^{2}}{x_{1,0}^{2}}\prod_{k=t_{K}}^{t}\frac{\rho(m_{k},\lambda_{i})^{2}}{\rho(m_{k},m)^{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{\tau_{K}}\frac{\rho(m_{k},\lambda_{i})^{2}}{\rho(m_{k},m)^{2}}+O(\epsilon)

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+O(\epsilon))M_{1}\omega\phi((1+\delta_{u})\ell_{0},\ell_{0},m)^{2(t-t_{K}+1)}+O(\epsilon).

(182)

where
M_{1}=2\max_{i}\overline{C}_{i}/\underline{C}_{1}
.
Next, we put (
181
) and (
182
) together, and since
\ell_{0}\geq m
, by choosing
\epsilon
sufficiently small, we get

\displaystyle c_{t+1}-\ell_{0}\leq 2(1+O(\epsilon))\kappa^{2}M_{1}\omega\phi((1+\delta_{m})m,\ell_{0},m)^{2(t-t_{K}+1)}\ell_{0}+O(\epsilon)\leq\delta_{u}\ell_{0}/2,

for all
t\geq t_{K}+\Delta t_{0}
, where

\displaystyle\Delta t_{0}=-\frac{\log(8\kappa^{2}M_{1}\omega/\delta_{u})}{\log r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)}.

Therefore,
c_{t+1}<(1+\delta_{u})\ell_{0}=(1+\delta_{m})m
for
t\geq t_{K}+\Delta t_{0}
or, equivalently,

\displaystyle t_{K+1}-t_{K}\leq\Delta t_{0}.

Note that for every
m_{t}
schedule, if
\mu_{K}
denotes the last value of
m_{t}
before reaching
[m/\gamma,(1+\delta_{m})m]
, then
\mu_{K}\geq(1+\delta_{m})m
, by definition.
Hence, letting
\ell_{0}^{\prime}=\mu_{K}/(1+\delta_{u})
and
I_{0}^{\prime}=\{i:\lambda_{i}\geq\ell_{0}^{\prime}\}
, then
I_{0}^{\prime}\geq I_{0}
, and by applying
Lemma

B.3
before, it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=I_{0}^{\prime}}^{d}\frac{(x_{i,t+1}-x_{i,t})^{2}}{(x_{1,t+1}-x_{1,t})^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+O(\epsilon))M_{1}\sum_{i=I_{0}^{\prime}}^{d}\frac{x_{i,0}^{2}}{x_{1,0}^{2}}\prod_{k=t_{K}}^{t}\frac{\rho(m_{k},\lambda_{i})^{2}}{\rho(m_{k},m)^{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{\tau_{K}}\frac{\rho(m_{k},\lambda_{i})^{2}}{\rho(m_{k},m)^{2}}+O(\epsilon)

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+O(\epsilon))M_{1}\sum_{i=I_{0}}^{d}\frac{x_{i,0}^{2}}{x_{1,0}^{2}}\prod_{k=t_{K}}^{t}\frac{\rho(m_{k},\lambda_{i})^{2}}{\rho(m_{k},m)^{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{\tau_{K}}\frac{\rho(m_{k},\lambda_{i})^{2}}{\rho(m_{k},m)^{2}}+O(\epsilon)

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+O(\epsilon))M_{1}\omega\phi((1+\delta_{u})\ell_{0},\ell_{0},m)^{2(t-t_{K}+1)}+O(\epsilon).

Therefore, the last adjustment cannot take more than
\Delta t_{0}
iterations for any
m_{t}
schedule.

Then, let
\Delta t_{j}
be quantities analogous to
\Delta t_{0}
, defined for
j=0,\ldots,K
as

\displaystyle\Delta t_{j}=-\frac{\log(8\kappa^{2}M_{1}\omega/\delta_{u})}{(1+\alpha_{\phi}(\ell_{j}-(1+\delta_{\ell})m))\log r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)}.

By
4.2
,
m_{t}
decreases by a factor of at least
\gamma
every time it is adjusted to a new value.
Hence,
\mu_{K-1}\geq\gamma\mu_{K}
for every
m_{t}
schedule, which implies that
\ell_{1}\leq\mu_{K-1}/(1+\delta_{u})
for every
m_{t}
schedule.
Hence, by the same rationale above, it cannot take more than
\Delta t_{1}
for
m_{t}
to be adjusted to its second last value before reaching the interval
[m/\gamma,(1+\delta_{m})m]
.
It follows by induction that it cannot take more than
\Delta t_{j}
for
m_{t}
to be adjust to its
K-j
-th to last value before reaching the interval
[m/\gamma,(1+\delta_{m})m]
.
Moreover, since by design
m_{t}\leq L
, it cannot more than
K\leq\log_{\gamma}(\kappa/(1+\delta_{m}))
adjustments before
m_{t}
reaches the interval
[m/\gamma,(1+\delta_{m})m]
.
Therefore, we conclude that
m_{t+1}\leq(1+\delta_{m})m
for all
t\geq\tau
, where

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{-\log(8\kappa^{2}M_{1}\omega/\delta_{u})}{\log r^{\prime}}\sum_{j=0}^{\log_{\gamma}(\kappa/(1+\delta_{m}))}\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{\phi}m(1+\delta_{\ell})(\gamma^{j}-1)}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{-\log(8\kappa^{2}M_{1}\omega/\delta_{u})}{\log r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)}\sum_{j=0}^{K}\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{\phi}m(1+\delta_{\ell})(\gamma^{j}-1)},

because
\log
is monotone and
r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)\leq r^{\prime}<1
, and
K\leq\log_{\gamma}(\kappa/(1+\delta_{m}))
.
∎

B.3
Main result

At last, we are ready to prove
Theorem

4.6
, which we restate below, establishing that
Algorithm

1
achieves acceleration around the minimum.

Theorem

(
4.6
)
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,m)
,
\bar{L}>L
and
\bar{\kappa}=\bar{L}/m
. Suppose that
\bar{\kappa}>\kappa=L/m\geq 4
and that
4.2
,
4.3
,
4.4
and
4.5
hold.
Then, there is some
\epsilon>0
such that if
\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|\leq\epsilon
, then the iterates
x_{t}
produced by
Algorithm

1
satisfy

\displaystyle\|x_{t+1}-x^{\star}\|\leq Cr_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\bar{\kappa})^{t}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|,

where
\sigma
depends on
\gamma
,
C
depends on
\bar{\kappa}
and
\omega
, with
\gamma
and
\omega
given by
4.2
,
4.3
,
4.4
and
4.5
.

Proof.

Let
\delta_{m}
and
\delta_{u}
be positive numbers such that
\delta_{u}<\min\{\delta_{m},1/2\}
and
\delta_{m}\leq\gamma-1
.
Then, define
\delta_{\ell}=(1+\delta_{m})/(1+\delta_{u})-1
, and let
r^{\prime}=r^{\prime}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)
be a function such that
r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{\phi}\kappa)\leq r^{\prime}<1
for all
\kappa\geq 1+\delta_{\ell}
, where
\sigma_{\phi}=\sigma_{\phi}(\delta_{u},\delta_{\ell},\kappa)
is given by
Lemma

B.10
.
By
Lemma

B.15
, it follows that
m_{t}\leq(1+\delta_{m})m
for all
t\geq\tau
, where

\displaystyle\tau=\frac{-\log(8\kappa^{2}M_{1}\omega/\delta_{u})}{\log r^{\prime}}\sum_{j=0}^{\log_{\gamma}(\kappa/(1+\delta_{m}))}\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{\phi}m(1+\delta_{\ell})(\gamma^{j}-1)},

(183)

M_{1}=\max_{i}\overline{C}_{i}/\underline{C}_{1}
.
In turn, as in the proof of
Proposition

B.14
,
Corollary

B.8
then implies that
\rho(m_{t},m)\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{1}\kappa)
for all
t\geq\tau
, where
\sigma_{1}=\max\{\gamma,\sigma_{m}\}
, and
\sigma_{m}=1+2\delta_{m}+2\sqrt{\delta_{m}(1+\delta_{m})}
.

Now, by
Lemma

B.3
, we have that
\rho(m_{t},\lambda_{i})\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{1}\kappa)
for all
\lambda_{i}
.
Hence, given
\delta_{\sigma}
such that
(1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{1}\kappa)<1
, by
Lemma

B.13
there is a
P_{i}(m_{t})=P_{i}(m_{t},\delta_{\sigma})\succeq I
for each
\lambda_{i}
such that
G_{i}(m_{t})^{\mathsf{T}}P_{i}(m_{t})G_{i}(m_{t})\preceq(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{2}r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{1}\kappa)^{2}P_{i}(m_{t})
.
Using
P_{i}(m_{t})
as diagonal blocks, we define the matrix
P(m_{t})=P(m_{t},\delta_{\sigma})=\diag(P_{1}(m_{t}),\ldots,P_{d}(m_{t}))
.
The block diagonal structure of
P
and
G
implies that
P(m_{t})\succeq I
, and that
G(m_{t})^{\mathsf{T}}P(m_{t})G(m_{t})\preceq(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{2}r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{1}\kappa)^{2}P(m_{t})
.
Hence, if
t_{j}\leq t<t_{j+1}
and
t\geq\tau
, then (
156
) yields

\displaystyle X_{t+1}^{\mathsf{T}}P(m_{t})X_{t+1}

\displaystyle=X_{t}^{\mathsf{T}}(G(\mu_{j})+\tilde{G}_{t})^{\mathsf{T}}P(\mu_{j})(G(\mu_{j})+\tilde{G}_{t})X_{t}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{2}r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{1}\kappa)^{2}X_{t}^{\mathsf{T}}P(\mu_{j})X_{t}+X_{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\tilde{P}_{t}X_{t},

(184)

since
m_{t}=\mu_{j}
, where

\displaystyle\tilde{P}_{t}=\tilde{G}_{t}^{\mathsf{T}}P(m_{t})G(m_{t})+G(m_{t})^{\mathsf{T}}P(m_{t})\tilde{G}_{t}+\tilde{G}_{t}^{\mathsf{T}}P(m_{t})\tilde{G}_{t}.

By
Equation

104
, we have that

\displaystyle\|G_{i}(m_{t})\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
-\beta(m_{t})\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\lambda_{i}}{L}\Bigr)&0\end{bmatrix}\right\|+\left\|\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&(1+\beta(m_{t}))\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\lambda_{i}}{L}\Bigr)\end{bmatrix}\right\|

\displaystyle=\max\left\{1,\beta(m_{t})\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\lambda_{i}}{L}\Bigr)\right\}+(1+\beta(m_{t}))\Bigl(1-\dfrac{\lambda_{i}}{L}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 3.

(185)

Furthermore, since
\delta_{\sigma}>0
and
r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{1}\kappa)\geq r_{\textup{acc}}(4)=1/2
, because by assumption
\kappa\geq 4
, the block diagonal structure of
P
combined with (
140
) yields

\displaystyle\|P(m_{t})\|\leq\frac{2}{1-(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{-2}}+\frac{16M_{2}^{2}}{(1-(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{-2})^{3}}=M_{\delta}.

(186)

Therefore, combining
161
,
185
and
186
, and taking
\epsilon
such that
\epsilon L_{H}/L<1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\tilde{P}_{t}\|\leq(2\|G(m_{t})\|+\|\tilde{G}_{t}\|)\|\tilde{G}_{t}\|\leq 7\epsilon M_{\delta}L_{H}/L.

(187)

Then, since
P(m_{t})\succeq I
, from
184
and
187
it follows that

\displaystyle X_{t+1}^{T}PX_{t+1}\leq((1+\delta)^{2}r^{2}+7\epsilon M_{\delta}L_{H}/L)X_{t}^{T}PX_{t}=\tilde{r}^{2}X_{t}^{T}PX_{t},

(188)

where we conveniently use a perturbed rate
\tilde{r}
, given by

\displaystyle\tilde{r}=\sqrt{(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{2}r^{2}+7\epsilon M_{\delta}L_{H}/L}.

(189)

Consecutively applying (
188
) and reproducing the steps in the proof of
Proposition

B.14
, we get

\displaystyle\|x_{t+1}-x^{\star}\|\leq C^{\prime}\bar{\kappa}^{2(1+\nu)}\tilde{r}^{t}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|,

(190)

where the constant
C
is given by

\displaystyle C^{\prime}=84\cdot 4^{3}M_{2}(8M_{1}\omega/\delta_{u})^{\nu}\sigma_{2}^{3/2},

with
\nu=1+\gamma/(\alpha_{\phi}m(\gamma-1))
,
\sigma_{2}=\max\{\gamma,\sigma_{m},\sigma_{\phi}\}
, and
\delta_{\sigma}=1/(4\sqrt{\sigma_{2}}\kappa)
is chosen such that

\displaystyle(1+\delta_{\sigma})r_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma_{2}\kappa)<r_{\textup{acc}}(2\sigma_{2}\kappa).

Hence, choosing
\epsilon
sufficiently small such that

\displaystyle\sqrt{(1+\delta_{\sigma})^{2}r^{2}+7\epsilon M_{\delta}L_{H}/L}\leq r_{\textup{acc}}(2\sigma_{2}\kappa),

and then plugging this choice of
\epsilon
back into (
190
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|x_{t+1}-x^{\star}\|\leq Cr_{\textup{acc}}(\sigma\bar{\kappa})^{t}\|x_{0}-x^{\star}\|,

where
\sigma=2\sigma_{2}
and
C=C^{\prime}\bar{\kappa}^{2(1+\nu)}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Remarks

To conclude this section, we make a few remarks on the constant
C
and convergence rate
\sigma
in
Theorem

4.6
.
One of the factors of
C
involves a power
\nu=1+\gamma/(\alpha_{\phi}m(\gamma-1))
.
Although this factor is an artifact of our conservative analysis,
\nu
is nonetheless small.
For example, we find numerically that
\alpha_{\phi}m\approx 10
when the base rate is
r_{\textup{acc}}(4\kappa)
, as
Figure

7
illustrates.
Moreover,
\gamma\geq 2
by
4.2
, therefore
\nu\leq 1.2
.
In the proof of
Theorem

4.6
, we work with
\sigma=2\sigma_{2}
, where
\sigma_{2}=\max\{2,\sigma_{m},\sigma_{\phi}\}
, which is a function of two further suboptimality factors given by
\sigma_{m}=1+2\delta_{m}+2\sqrt{\delta_{m}(1+\delta_{m})}
and
\sigma_{\phi}\lesssim 1/4(\sqrt{\delta_{u}+\delta_{\ell}(1+\delta_{u})}-\sqrt{\delta_{u}(1+\delta_{\ell})})^{2}
.
The factor 2 in
\sigma=2\sigma_{2}
is a result of a compromise to obtain a reasonable condition number for the matrix
P
in the Lyapunov analysis of the final regime of NAG-free, when
m_{t}
is sufficiently accurate for accelerated convergence.
In reality, this compromise is an artifact of
any
Lyapunov analysis of linear systems, whose solutions are linear combinations of some
t^{k}r^{t}
terms, rather than purely exponential solutions
r^{t}
.
Hence, this compromise is typically ignored, e.g. as in
Lessard et al. 2016
, in which case the convergence rate is
\max\{2,\sigma_{m},\sigma_{\phi}\}
.
Then, for example, letting
\gamma=2,\delta_{m}=0.2,\delta_{u}=0.01
and
\delta_{\ell}=(1+\delta_{m})/(1+\delta_{u})-1
, we obtain
\max\{\gamma,\sigma_{m},\sigma_{\phi}\}\leq 2.4
.
Therefore, the convergence rate in this case would not be worse than
r_{\textup{acc}}(2.4\kappa)
.
The
\gamma
factor is an artifact of our analysis, but there is a real tension between the other suboptimality factors
\sigma_{m}
and
\sigma_{\phi}
.
That is because as
m_{t}
approaches
m
, the estimation convergence rate slows down, whereas the actual convergence rate improves, since the estimate gets more accurate.
This translates into reduced
\delta_{m}
and
\delta_{\ell}
, which reduces
\sigma_{m}
, but increases
\sigma_{\phi}
.
The bound on these suboptimality rates is conservative, however.
In the next section, we present experiments in which we see that in practice the suboptimality factor is much closer to 1.

Appendix C
Numerical experiments

In this section, we present further experiments and provide more details of those already presented in
Section

5
.
We start by recalling which methods are used in the experiments.

C.1
Methods

As baselines, we take GD, NAG and the Triple Momentum Method
[
Van Scoy et al. 2017
, TMM]
, replacing
L
and
m
with problem-specific bounds.
As competing methods, we consider two restart schemes based on
[
O’Donoghue and Candès 2015
]
: one where
L
is replaced with a problem-specific bound, that we refer to as NAG+R, and another where
L
is estimated online with backtracking, that we refer to as NAG+RB.
For NAG+RB, every time the
L
estimate
L_{t}
fails to produce enough descent, it is increased to
1.01L_{t}
and tested again.
This choice of adjustment factor produces less conservative estimates at the expense of more function evaluations, which largely favors NAG+RB since in all experiments we plot the suboptimality gap
f(x_{t})-f(x^{\star})
versus iterations.
As additional methods for comparison, we consider the adaptive gradient method AdGD
[
Malitsky and Mishchenko 2024
]
and its accelerated heuristic, AdGD-accel2, both using
\gamma=1/\sqrt{2}
, as well as a previous variant of the accelerated heuristic
[
Malitsky and Mishchenko 2020
]
, which we denote by AdGD-accel.

C.2
Smoothed and regularized log-sum-exp

We start by clarifying the remark made in
Section

C.2
that log-sum-exp approximates the max function, by which we mean that

\displaystyle\max\limits_{i=1,\dots,n}p_{i}\leq\theta\log\Biggl(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\exp\Bigl(\frac{p_{i}}{\theta}\Bigr)\Biggr)\leq\max\limits_{i=1,\dots,n}p_{i}+\theta\log n.

Thus, as
\theta
decreases, the approximation gets tighter.
In the first experiment, we also compare some variants of
Algorithm

1
that employ different strategies to estimate
L
.
One of them is
Algorithm

3
, which estimates
L
by backtracking line search.

Algorithm 3

NAG-free-back: a variant of NAG-free that estimates
L
via backtracking.

1:

Input:

T>0,x_{0}=y_{0}

2:

Output:

x_{T}

3:

y\sim x_{0}+U\mathopen{[}0,10^{-6}\mathclose{]}^{d}

4:

L_{0},m_{0}\leftarrow\|\nabla f(x_{0})-\nabla f(y)\|/\|x_{0}-y\|

5:

for

t=0,\ldots,T-1

do

6:

y_{t+1}\leftarrow x_{t}-(1/L_{t})\nabla f(x_{t})

7:

while

f(y_{t+1})-f(x_{t})>-(1/2L_{t})\|\nabla f(x_{t})\|^{2}

do

8:

L_{t}\leftarrow 1.01L_{t}

9:

y_{t+1}\leftarrow x_{t}-(1/L_{t})\nabla f(x_{t})

10:

end

while

11:

x_{t+1}\leftarrow y_{t+1}+\dfrac{\sqrt{L_{t}}-\sqrt{m_{t}}}{\sqrt{L_{t}}+\sqrt{m_{t}}}(y_{t+1}-y_{t})

12:

c_{t+1}\leftarrow\|\nabla f(x_{t+1})-\nabla f(x_{t})\|/\|x_{t+1}-x_{t}\|

13:

m_{t+1}\leftarrow\min(m_{t},c_{t+1})

14:

end

for

In

C.2
, we also mention different variants of
Equation

6
, in which we fix
n=d=600
and vary the regularization and smoothing parameters,
\eta
and
\theta
, respectively.

shows the results of those experiments.
In particular,
Figure

8(a)
shows the results for
\theta\in\{0.1,1,10\}
, when
\eta=0.1
.
Similarly,
Figure

8(b)
shows the results for
\eta\in\{0.01,0.1,1\}
, when
\theta=0.1
.

.

.

) using methods from

.

C.5
Matrix factorization

Section

, like
Section

C.4
, also refers to a “restarting” variant of NAG-free, which is summarized by
Algorithm

4
.
In those experiments,
t_{test}=1
.

C.6
Further experiments

Finally, we discuss how to interpret some edge cases of the theoretical results derived in
Appendix

B
.
Specifically, we are interested in how initial conditions, dimensionality, and distribution of Hessian eigenvalues affect the performance of NAG-free.
To study these features, we consider a stylized quadratic objective given by
f(x)=(1/2)x^{\mathsf{T}}Ax
, where
A
is a diagonal matrix.
We choose this simplified model because it captures the essential features of the local convergence setting of NAG-free in which the theoretical results in
Appendix

B
were derived.

First, we consider the issue of initial conditions.

4.5
requires the existence of some constant
\omega
such that
\omega x_{1.0}^{2}\geq\|x_{0}\|^{2}
.
In reality, this requirement only serves the technical purpose of allowing us to define a region of local acceleration.
Otherwise, if
x_{1,0}^{2}
could be arbitrarily small relative to
\|x_{0}\|^{2}
, then we would not be able to lower bound the difference
(x_{1,t+1}-x_{1,t})^{2}
only in terms of
x_{1,0}
and
\rho(m_{t},m)
, since the perturbation components of
x_{1,t}
due to other
x_{i,t}
could dominate the entire solution of
x_{1,t}
, if
x_{1,0}^{2}
were sufficiently small relative to
x_{i,0}^{2}
.
With that in mind, we let
A=\diag(1,5,10^{4})
, and initialize
x_{0}
with
x_{0}=[10^{-5},1,1]^{\mathsf{T}}
, which corresponds to a situation where
\omega\ll 1
, since
\omega=\|x_{0}\|^{2}/x_{1,0}^{2}\approx\sqrt{2}\cdot 10^{-5}
.
Moreover, we choose
L=10^{4}
such that the condition number is
\kappa=L/m=10^{4}
, which is among typical values found in practice.

Figure

9(a)
shows the normalized suboptimality gaps for NAG initialized with
L=10^{4}
and
m=1
, for
Algorithm

1
initialized with
\bar{L}=L=10^{4}
, and the normalized suboptimality gap
r_{\textup{acc}}(\kappa)^{t}
based on nominal convergence rate for this problem,
r_{\textup{acc}}(\kappa)=(\sqrt{\kappa}-1)/\sqrt{\kappa}
.
By normalized suboptimality gap, we mean that the suboptimality gap, which is the same as function values, is divided by the initial suboptimality gap, such that the initial normalized suboptimality gap is unity for all methods.
In addition, we plot in gold the estimate gap
m_{t}-m
, measured on the right-hand
y
axis.
As we can see in
Figure

9(a)
,
m_{t}
almost immediately reaches the value of
m_{t}=\lambda_{2}=5
, as depicted by the solid black horizontal line.
Indeed, the effect of small
x_{1,0}
is that, initially, the effective strong convexity parameter
increases
to
\lambda_{2}=5
, which makes the problem better conditioned.
Hence, during this stage,
Algorithm

1
converges faster at a rate faster than the nominal rate.
Once
x_{1,t}^{2}
becomes comparable with
\|x_{t}\|^{2}
, a second stage begins.
In this second stage,
m_{t}
approaches the actual strong convexity parameter
m=\lambda_{1}=1
, and
Algorithm

1
slows down to the true nominal convergence rate for the problem.
In particular, we see that
m_{t}
only has to be sufficiently accurate for
Algorithm

1
to achieve acceleration.
Accordingly, the rate at which the estimate gap
m_{t}-m
converges to 0 decelerates as
m_{t}
becomes more accurate, as expected.

Figure

9(b)
shows a similar result, where instead of reducing
x_{1,0}^{2}
relative to
\|x_{0}\|^{2}
, we equivalently increase
x_{2,0}^{2}
, therefore
\|x_{0}\|^{2}
, relative to
x_{1,0}^{2}
.
Therefore, as
\omega
becomes large, NAG-free performs
better
initially, and
Theorem

4.6
should be applied to each stage with a different effective strong convexity parameter.
Conversely, small
\omega
represents the worst-case scenario for NAG-free, since it cannot take advantage initially of increased effective strong convexity parameters.

Figure

10(a)
illustrates this remark, where
x_{0}
is initialized with
x_{0}=[1,1,1]^{\mathsf{T}}
.
We see that
m_{t}
approaches
\lambda_{1}=m
much faster than in the previous two edge cases, and NAG-free only matches the nominal convergence rate for this problem.

(a)

x_{0}=[10^{-5},1,1]^{\mathsf{T}}
.

(b)

x_{0}=[1,10^{5},1]^{\mathsf{T}}
.

Figure 9
:
Normalized suboptimality gaps for
f(x)=(1/2)x^{\mathsf{T}}Ax
, with
A=\diag(1,5,10^{4})
.

(a)

A=\diag(1,5,10^{4}),x_{0}=[1,1,1]^{\mathsf{T}}
.

(b)

A=\diag(1,5I,10^{4}),I\in\mathbb{R}^{10^{2}\times 10^{2}},x_{0}=1
.

Figure 10
:
Normalized suboptimality gaps for
f(x)=(1/2)x^{\mathsf{T}}Ax
.

Next, we consider the issue of dimensionality.
Like initial conditions, dimensionality also influences
\omega
under most reasonable initializations of
x_{0}
.
For instance, if
x_{0}
is initialized uniformly, then on average
x_{0}=1
, a vector of appropriate dimensions whose entries are all 1’s.
To assess the impact of dimensionality, we continue with a diagonal quadratic objective where
\lambda_{1}=1
and
\lambda_{d}=10^{4}
, but increase the number of eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
such that
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{2}=5
.
That is,
f(x)=(1/2)x^{\mathsf{T}}Ax
, with
A=\diag(1,5I,10^{4})
, where
I\in\mathbb{R}^{(d-2)\times(d-2)}
is the identity matrix with dimension
d-2
.
We consider
d\in\{10^{2},10^{3},10^{4}\}
.
The results, shown in
Figures

10(b)
,
11(a)
and
11(b)
, demonstrate that dimensionality has a somewhat similar effect of initial conditions.
That is, as the number of eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
such that
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{2}
increases, the effective condition number of the problem increases, and NAG-free initially enjoys improved convergence rates.
Then, as the relative norm of the coordinates associated with these eigenvalues become comparable with that of
x_{1,t}
, then NAG-free enters a second stage in which it matches the nominal convergence rate of the problem.
The influence of dimensionality is slightly more subtle than that of initial conditions but the overall effect is similar.
That is because each coordinate
x_{i,t}
associated with
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{2}
decays in parallel, independent of
d
, but as
d
increases, but before
m_{t}
can start to approach
m
,
\sum_{j=2}^{d-1}x_{j,t}^{2}\approx dx_{2,t}^{2}
must become comparable with
x_{1,t}^{2}
.
In any case, we see that dimensionality cannot degrade the performance of NAG-free beyond that of nominal acceleration.

(a)

A=\diag(1,5I,10^{4}),I\in\mathbb{R}^{10^{3}\times 10^{3}},x_{0}=1
.

(b)

A=\diag(1,5I,10^{4}),I\in\mathbb{R}^{10^{4}\times 10^{4}},x_{0}=1
.

Figure 11
:
Normalized suboptimality gaps for
f(x)=(1/2)x^{\mathsf{T}}Ax
.

The last issue that we consider is the distribution of Hessian eigenvalues.
We start by experimenting with uniform distributions.
Specifically, we fix
\lambda_{1}=m=1
and
\lambda_{d}=L=10^{4}
, and consider the remaining eigenvalues uniformly distributed in the interval
[1,\bar{\lambda}_{d-1}]
, for
\bar{\lambda}_{d-1}\in\{2,10,100,10^{4}\}
.
In all experiments,
x_{0}=1\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
d=10^{3}
.
Also, we fix
m_{0}=L
.
On
Figures

12
and
13
, in addition to NAG, NAG-free and the nominal convergence curve
r_{\textup{acc}}(\kappa)^{2t}
, we also plot a suboptimal convergence curve
r_{\textit{sub}}^{2t}
, where
r_{\textit{sub}}=r_{\textup{acc}}(1.2\kappa)
.
As we can see, although the performance of NAG-free can degrade when the eigenvalues are uniformly distributed, the impact is mild, and the convergence rate is never worse than
r_{\textit{sub}}
, which represents a suboptimality factor of only
20\%
in the condition number.
To conclude, we consider the situation where the eigenvalues are clustered.
We study three levels of clustering, where there are roughly
20,40
and
60\%
of the total number of eigenvalues.
In each experiment, the clusters are evenly distributed in
[m,\bar{\lambda}_{d-1}]
, for
\bar{\lambda}_{d-1}\in\{2,10,100,10^{4}\}
.
In
Figures

14
,
15
,
16
,
17
,
18
and
19
, we see that the results are similar to the case where the eigenvalues are uniformly distributed, and again the rate of convergence of NAG-free is never does worse than
r_{\textit{sub}}
, which represents a suboptimality factor of only
20\%
in the condition number.

(a)

\bar{\lambda}_{d-1}=2
.

(b)

\bar{\lambda}_{d-1}=10
.

Normalized suboptimality gaps for
f(x)=(1/2)x^{\mathsf{T}}Ax

A=\diag(1,D,10^{4})
and

a diagonal matrix with eigenvalues uniformly distributed in
[1,\bar{\lambda}_{d-1}]
.
The dashed and dotted red lines represent
r_{\textup{acc}}(\kappa)^{2t}
and
r_{\textit{sub}}^{2t}
, respectively, where
r_{\textit{sub}}=r_{\textup{acc}}(1.2\kappa)
.

\bar{\lambda}_{d-1}=100
.
